Show HN: Fleetlayer – Framework for running many AI agents on multiple machines
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-28
08:37:09 UTC
17:37:09 KST

Source-available · early · built in public
Claude Code is awesome, until you add a second machine or account.
Fleetlayer connects the Claude Code and Codex sessions on your laptop, home server and VPS, under every account you use. They message each other, share your signed-in browser and files, and work under rules you set, on machines you control.
What starts going wrong
One Claude account on one machine is easy. Add a second account, a server, or another kind of agent, and this starts happening:
- claude.ai artifacts say “access denied” unless you’re signed in to the right account.
- A session on your server can’t message one on your laptop.
- You keep signing in and out of the Claude in Chrome extension.
- An agent on your server can’t use the browser on your Mac.
- An agent on your server can’t reach the Downloads or Screenshots folders on your Mac.
- Codex and Gemini agents can’t make claude.ai artifacts at all.
- You find out an account is out of usage when a session stops.
Fleetlayer solves all of this, and much more
- “access denied” on artifacts Fleetlayer Pages and Apps Claude, Codex and Gemini agents write pages and interactive apps as plain folders, and Fleetlayer hosts them behind one sign-in. Set up pages
- sessions that can’t reach each other Messages between any two agents Claude or Codex, on the same machine or another one: fleetlayer msg send --to api@laptop.
Send a message
- signing the extension in and out Any agent can control your browser Agents on any machine and any Claude account can use the Chrome on your Mac, signed in as you (via Playwright MCP). Share your browser
- an account that runs out mid-task Your own accounts, on your own machines Year-long sign-ins for each of your Claude accounts on every machine you run, and each account’s usage visible to every session. Add your accounts
You need this if
- your agents run on more than one machine, or under more than one Claude account;
- you run Codex or other agents next to Claude Code;
- an agent reads email or the web, and you want it kept away from the others' rights and secrets;
- you want your agents, secrets and the record of what they did on machines you control.
You probably don't if
You use one Claude account on one laptop, and Claude Code's own features cover the rest. Use those, and come back when you add a second machine or account. What Fleetlayer adds beyond Claude Code.
Your whole fleet on one page.
The dashboard shows every machine and what it runs, every agent session and whether it's waiting on you, the events that arrived and which agent each reached, and the requests waiting for your approval. Live, behind your own sign-in, and everything on it works from the command line too.
A node on each machine, one control plane.
Fleetlayer attaches to herdr without taking it over, so its app and your terminals keep working as before. Each machine, or each Unix user, runs a small node that keeps one outbound connection to the control plane, so it works behind NAT with no ports to open.
- Laptop herdr 
  - api
  - docs
  - review
node delivers messages, runs plugins
- Home server user triage 
  - triage
reads strangers' email, so it may message only you user infra 
  - infra
  - backups
a node per user own key, own rules
- VPS or the same laptop control plane machines, sessions, messages, webhooks, access rules pages · sign-in · Claude accounts each one a separate piece you can move
- Session manager
  herdr today, others to come
- Node
  one per machine or Unix user
- Service
  run it anywhere, move it later
What you get
- Every session in one list Every machine and session, with a one-line summary of what each agent is doing, and a searchable record of what it did. fleetlayer attach api@laptop watches or drives its terminal from anywhere.
- Agents that message each other On one machine or across machines, delivered through the agent's own channel and labelled with who sent it. A misspelt name suggests the right one.
- Webhooks that find the right agent A CI failure goes to the session that wrote the branch, which already knows the code, instead of waking a fresh agent with no context.
- Your own accounts, on your own machines Each of your Claude accounts' usage, visible to every session. Year-long tokens reach each machine sealed to its own key. Your sign-ins stay on a machine you control.
- Separate users, separate rules Run several nodes and herdr instances on the same machine or VM, each under its own Unix user. The access policy limits whom each agent may message and what it may use.
- Tools that live on one machine Your signed-in browser on the laptop, used by an agent on the server: fleetlayer mcp browser@laptop, limited to the callers you allow.
All the security, none of the pain
Most people run Claude Code with access to everything and hope nothing goes wrong. That works until an agent reads a malicious email, or a stray rm -rf runs in the wrong folder. Fleetlayer gives you the protections a security team would ask for, an agent sets them up for you, and each one can be switched off when you don't need it.
- Ask before the risky things Access rules decide which agent may message, start or take over which session. Anything sensitive waits for your approval, and you can leave approvals off for the work you trust.
- Everything on the record An audit log shows who did what and when, and you can check it hasn't been altered.
- Secrets stay on your machines Tokens travel encrypted to the machine that needs them, and the control plane never stores them.
- Untrusted input stays untrusted Messages from an agent that reads email or the web arrive marked as outside data. That agent runs as its own user and can't borrow a trusted agent's rights.
- One sign-in, one sign-out One sign-in for your self-hosted services, and signing out of one signs you out of all of them.
- Secrets per role Items from 1Password reach only the roles you grant them to, and are scrubbed from everything that leaves a machine. How
- Agents reach only the hosts they need Each role gets a list of hosts, and you approve new ones. Linux for now. How
- An admin role that's off until you start a timed window The agent that can change everything has no rights until then, and is cleaned up when the window ends. How
A foundation for your own agent workflows
Fleetlayer started inside Quietdeck. Once we saw how useful that layer was on its own, we pulled it out into its own project and refined it. It gives you the primitives to build your own apps and layers on top:
- sessions at tab@node
- messages
- webhooks and channels
- access rules
- approvals
- an audit log
- plugins in any language
- shared MCP tools
- pages and apps
Built on Fleetlayer
Quietdeck
An agent triages your email and proposes what to do, and you approve. Quietdeck is designed to run on Fleetlayer and uses its features to:
- forward emails and notifications to the agent that owns the work through Fleetlayer’s messaging
- know what all your agents are doing from Fleetlayer’s session summaries, and route each message to the right agent
- start an isolated email triage agent on its own Unix user, connected through its own Fleetlayer node, with access rules that limit whom it may message
One system, many parts
Fleetlayer is the layer between your agents and the tools around them. Each part does one job and can be replaced.
- AgentsClaude Code, Codex, Gemini
- Sessionsherdr
- Fleetlayercross-agent messaging, webhooks, single sign-on, access rules, approvals, audit log, remote terminals
- Fleetlayer Pluginsherdr, Claude Code, AI account management, session summaries, file sync, shared folders, pages and apps (all optional)
- ToolsExecutor for MCP servers and sign-ins to your services
- Secrets1Password
- BrowserPlaywright MCP
- FilesSyncthing, Obsidian Sync, Obsidian LiveSync, or any file syncing solution
- NetworkTailscale, Cloudflare Tunnel
- ServicesGatus for health checks, Traefik for addresses
- Backupsrestic
- Mobileherdr-mobile-relay
Start small, replace any component as you go.
Begin with everything on your laptop. When you outgrow it, move one piece to a home server or a VPS, or swap it for the tool you'd rather use: your own proxy, your company's sign-in, another session manager. Each piece keeps its state in one folder and speaks open protocols, so moving it is a copy and a new address.
Ask your agent
Set up Fleetlayer on this machine. The docs are at https://fleetlayer.ai/llms.txt
Or run it yourself (Linux or macOS)
$ curl -fsSL https://raw.githubusercontent.com/fleetlayer/fleetlayer/main/install.sh | sh
Start everything on this machine
$ fleetlayer up
Fleetlayer is running. Status: http://fleetlayer.localhost:7680
$ fleetlayer sessions
No vendor lock-in
Your own stack instead of a vendor's cloud. Unlike one big app, Fleetlayer is a few separate pieces that work with the tools you already use, and you can swap any of them.
Keep using Anthropic's and OpenAI's own features for as long as they serve you. When you outgrow them (several subscriptions, several machines, agents from more than one vendor), you give nothing up. When a vendor ships its own version of a feature, Fleetlayer can support it too, and you can still change models and harnesses whenever you like.
For people who run many agents
Fleetlayer is for a person running many agents across several machines and accounts: personal errands, email triage, product work, infrastructure, background workers, each with its own machine or Unix user and its own permissions, all visible from your own terminal.
It grows with you. A household or a team shares one system, and a company gets what it needs (single sign-on, teams, separate organisations, audit) when it needs it.
Features
Coming soon
- tmux as a session manager (v0.4.2)
- More harnesses: pi, OpenCode, Gemini CLI and Cursor's CLI (v0.4.2)
- SAML sign-in providers
Expect breaking changes: what changed in each release is in the changelog. Will it cost money? We might provide a hosted control plane and provision a VPS where you can run your remote agents and services, but we haven't decided on anything yet.

GTA VI: Console Shortages, Performance Showdowns, $1B More Sales, and Some Tech Issues
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-28
08:30:00 UTC
17:30:00 KST

GTA VI: Console Shortages, Performance Showdowns, $1B More Sales, and Some Tech Issues (indiatimes.com) 7
			
		 	
				Grand Theft Auto VI  "has caused a worldwide shortage of gaming consoles," reports the Washington Post, with the game only playable on the most recent generation of Microsoft's Xbox (Series X or S) or on PlayStation 5:
In the United States, Sony's higher-end PlayStation 5 Pro was intermittently sold out even before the release, with major retailers out of stock at its $900 retail price and resellers asking as much as $1,900. In Germany, stores such as MediaMarkt and Saturn are sold out of PS5 models and lower-end Xbox ones...  Consoles at Sony-recommended prices are similarly hard to find in other European countries, with third-party resellers offering the device at nearly double the price.   In Japan, Sony has gone as far as restricting who is allowed to buy the console to block scalpers. The Sony Store sells it only through a lottery open to buyers with a Japanese account and at least 60 hours of verified PS4 or PS5 playtime.  
Windows Central notes Xbox One consoles can also stream GTA 6 from Xbox Cloud Gaming.  And they argue that Sony's "Plays best on PS5" tagline for GTA 6 "is a lie, it turns out," citing a comparison from the hardware-reviewing YouTube channel Digital Foundry (which has 1.54 million subscribers):
Digital Foundry's analysis seems to suggest in no uncertain terms that the Xbox Series X version is generally superior to the PS5. Digital Foundry says that the PS5 version suffers from frame rate issues far more frequently than the Xbox Series X... The Xbox Series X version supports VRR, which with a compatible display helps offset frame drops and smooth out performance perception. GTA 6's sandbox is among the most complex in history, so it's an impressive feat that it runs as well as it does in general on both PS5 and Xbox Series X systems...  This is without Xbox Series X leveraging FSR 4.1 either, which in theory, could push the visuals on Xbox Series X beyond even that of the extremely expensive PS5 Pro.  
In the end they also acknowledge that "the differences are negligible in reality." But the site TechPowerUp notes recent estimates by Alinea Analytics that PlayStation 5 accounts for 75.7% share of GTA VI sales, or 40 million sales, vs. just 13 million units on Xbox.   That's 53 million sales total, representing "an estimated total revenue figure of $4.7 billion...
"[P]layer statistics show that around 250,000 players have already spent over 100 hours in-game in its first week."
Meanwhile, The Times of India reports both PS5 and Xbox owners are experiencing console issues that may be related to overheating:
A Polish console repair technician says his workshop has seen a sharp rise in faulty Xbox consoles since GTA 6 came out earlier this month. Xbox is far less common than PlayStation in Poland. Even so, it now accounts for around two of every 10 consoles the shop receives each day. Before GTA 6 came out, it took in roughly 10 Xbox units a month.Owners report overheating, shutdowns mid-session, boot failures and slowdowns. Some machines refuse to install updates or freeze after one. One Series X ran other titles fine but stopped booting after a few days of GTA 6. It now powers on with no video output, then the fan stops and the machine shuts itself off. No common cause has been found yet. Overheating is the main suspect, but Xbox diagnostic tools are limited, so the shop is building its own to read error codes.
PS5 owners ran into trouble first. One unit that kept switching off mid-session logged 40 Main SoC Access Errors, plus seven APU VRM Power Fail and seven SSD Controller Watchdog errors.
The technician says liquid metal shifting between the chip and heatsink, a known weak spot on older PS5 models, is often to blame...
A Twitch streamer's PS5 died mid-broadcast while he was riding an ATV through Leonida. The screen froze, artifacts appeared, and the system never booted again, even though there was little dust inside.
Reddit posts tell similar stories. One Series X owner says the console would not power on the morning after a long session, and a dead power supply is suspected. Another managed about 10 minutes of play before a local repair shop blamed the motherboard... 
Neither Microsoft nor Sony has confirmed that GTA 6 is behind the failures.
 
In related news, the social media team at Microsoft-owned GitHub "published and subsequently deleted a bizarrely brazen post on X," reports Kotaku.  The tweet pointed to the 58,000 stars earned by a new project launched in August — the PlayStation5 emulator AnyPS5, which it said can be used for "rewriting PS5 executables to run on Windows and Linux. Does changing where games run cross a legal line?"
[T]he video that was contained within GitHub's post highlighted that users' efforts to improve the AnyPS5 emulator were primarily driven by the release of GTA 6.  
The article notes GTA VI's publisher Take Two has been "actively shooting down all of the GitHub repositories attempting to vibe-code a PC port of GTA 6 over the past week."

Will AI Bots Ever Catch on for Online Shopping?
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-28
04:45:00 UTC
13:45:00 KST

Will AI Bots Ever Catch on for Online Shopping? (americanexpress.com) 21
			
		 	
				"If you currently use an AI agent that signs in to your account with your credentials, it may no longer be able to connect," American Express told its customers today (according to Slashdot reader Smonster).  And ahile Walmart's site welcomes AI agents, there's blocks or limitations on them from Amazon, eBay, Kohl's and United Airlines, CNBC reports.  And other sites "have added provisions to their terms of service spelling out that they're not liable if users' AI-driven purchases go wrong..."
The hiccups threaten to stunt the growth of shopping agents by creating a clunky and frustrating experience for users who are just starting to experiment with the tools. The question of who takes the blame when a transaction goes wrong also remains unclear. 
Despite those challenges, analysts remain bullish about agentic commerce's potential. U.S. revenue from agentic commerce could reach up to $1 trillion by 2030, analysts at McKinsey and ICSC forecast in April.   Retailers and tech companies are also positioning agents as a helping hand for holiday shopping. Walmart on Tuesday touted its partnership with Meta, saying consumers could "put an AI agent to work" to nab Black Friday deals....
For users hoping to turn their online shopping over to AI agents, the experience can be unpredictable.  Agents encounter a patchwork of permissions across various websites. Retailers such as Coach and Gap allow AI bots to browse but not check out, while others such as Target sometimes "block shopping agents by accident," according to an audit conducted in August by agent security company Billions Network.  Amazon has responded the most aggressively to agents by walling off its site entirely. The company rankled Elon Musk last month after it prevented his Grok Bot from placing orders.  In September, the company also blocked Meta's Muse from shopping on its platform, saying it never agreed to participate in the program. And last November, Amazon sued Perplexity, accusing the startup of concealing its Comet agent, which can purchase products on behalf of users. 
Amazon CEO Andy Jassy has said the company expects "over time, to partner with third-party agents," but that any partnerships will require the right "value exchange" between agents and retailers... 
For now, agents remain a niche tool for shopping, with agentic purchases making up just 0.4% of global e-commerce transactions and 0.2% of value, according to Bain & Co. research published earlier this month... But retailers may not be able to ignore them forever. Roughly a quarter of Muse's 10 million users are shopping online via an AI agent with an average spend of $200 per order, Meta CEO Mark Zuckerberg said in October.     
The article notes bots often "get stumped by login requirements at checkout. In some cases, the agents may require human intervention to bypass a Captcha test and continue browsing...
"A Visa study published earlier this month instructed 22 models from 12 AI labs to carry out a range of shopping tasks, such as buying a TV, a laptop and a hair appliance. The agents failed about 43% of test runs, and in more than half of those failed runs, an agent purchased a product "that did not meet the customer's identified requirements," according to the study..."

Knuth: Moratorium on Error Reports
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-28
03:49:21 UTC
12:49:21 KST

For more than fifty years I've been welcoming reports from readers of errors in my books, so that I can correct them before new copies are printed.
Alas, the rise of LLMs has increased the productivity of my readers so greatly that error reports are now being submitted much faster than I can read them!
This content is very important to me, and I wish to take personal responsibility for getting everything right. Therefore I insist on verifying everything carefully.
That takes time. But the amount of available time is now less than zero.
Random sampling indicates that the vast majority of errors that have been reported in recent weeks are repeats of issues already submitted by others.
Therefore, I shall not be able to look at any error reports submitted after 25 November, 2026, until I've posted an updated list of the known errors in whatever book is involved.
Please consult the list below to see if error reports are currently being accepted.
I apologize for having made errors in the first place, and for having limited ability to cope with more than one thing at a time. I shall do my best to get through the existing reports as soon as humanly possible.
Thanks in advance for your patience and understanding.

====================================================================================================

The M5 MacBook Air just got its biggest discount since Apple raised the price
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-28
02:16:43 UTC
11:16:43 KST

If you’ve been holding off on buying a new MacBook Air since Apple raised prices by $200 in June, you’re in luck. The 13-inch M5 MacBook Air is on sale at Amazon and Best Buy with 16GB of RAM and 512GB of storage starting at $999 ($300 off) for Black Friday, which is the lowest price we’ve seen since the hike. If you want a larger display, Amazon and Best Buy are also taking $300 off the otherwise identical 15-inch model with 16GB of RAM and 512GB of storage, dropping it to $1,199.
The M5 MacBook Air just got its biggest discount since Apple raised the price
The new promo drops Apple’s 13-incher to within $100 of its all-time low, easing the sting of recent price hikes.
The M5-equipped MacBook Air is a good middle ground if you need more power than the MacBook Neo — which, thanks to a $100 discount, is $599 — but don’t want to splurge on a MacBook Pro. Its faster chip, 16GB of RAM, and 512GB of storage make it better suited for multitasking and creative work in apps like Photoshop and Premiere. You also get a higher-resolution display, better speakers, a backlit keyboard that makes typing in the dark easier, and faster ports. Plus, its sharper webcam supports Apple’s Center Stage feature, which automatically adjusts the framing to keep you in view during video calls.
Of course, the Air can’t match the performance or higher-end displays you can get with a MacBook Pro, whether you opt for an M5 model, the newer M5 Pro model with a PROLED XDR Display, or one of the M6-powered machines. A Pro may be worth the extra money if you regularly edit video or work on more demanding creative projects. But if you mostly need a laptop for work, browsing, and occasional photo or video editing, the M5 Air is more than capable.

We found 60-odd Black Friday deals we love for $100 or less
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-28
00:42:02 UTC
09:42:02 KST

Between holiday travel, gifts, and the rising cost of just about everything, expenses can quickly add up during this time of year. Thankfully, you don’t have to dip too far into your savings to take advantage of some of the better Black Friday deals we’ve seen.
We found 60-odd Black Friday deals we love for $100 or less
There are still great deals on everything from noise-canceling earbuds and e-readers to the latest smart mugs.
Below, we’ve rounded up a range of great stuff currently on sale for $100 or less, from noise-canceling earbuds and portable Bluetooth speakers to pocket-sized e-readers, photo printers, games, and more. You’ll also find deals on Ember’s temperature-controlled mug, Kodak’s tiny keychain camera, and a variety of Lego sets, all of which would make great holiday gifts or simply a nice treat for yourself.
Disney Plus, Hulu Bundle
You can get an ad-supported Disney Plus and Hulu bundle for $4.99 a month ($8 off) for an entire year. Both streaming services feature a deep catalog of new and classic TV shows and movies, including The Simpsons, Andor, Toy Story 5, and The Fantastic Four: First Steps.
Amazon Echo Time
Although it’s not Amazon’s first bedside alarm clock, the Echo Time is focused on helping you sleep better. The camera-less Alexa speaker can detect your sleep stage via ultrasound and use smart alarms to ease you into the day, but only if you want it to.
Anker Universal Charger (100W)
Anker’s durable travel adapter delivers up to 100W of power and works in over 200 countries. It doesn’t support AC plugs, but it does offer a trio of USB-C ports and a nifty LCD display to keep tabs on the charge percentage.
HBO Max
An HBO Max Basic subscription provides access to shows like A Knight of the Seven Kingdoms, The Last of Us, and The Pitt, as well as films like Superman and Weapons. You can get a year of the monthly ad-supported subscription for $5.49 a month (about $6 off) until December 1st.
Noco GB40 UltraSafe Jumpstarter
Noco’s Boost GB40 is a rechargeable battery with jumper cables to start six-liter gas or three-liter diesel engines. It also has USB ports so you can charge your phone, ensuring you always have a backup plan.
Apple AirTag (second-gen)
Apple’s latest AirTags feature an upgraded ultra-wideband chip for more precise location tracking, a noticeably louder speaker, and an expanded Bluetooth range. Read our review.
Beats Studio Buds Plus
The Beats Studio Buds Plus sport an eye-catching translucent option and build upon the original with improved sound, better voice quality, and more effective ANC. They also include swappable silicone ear tips and support for native iOS and Android features. Read our review.
Liene Pearl N200 Pro photo printer
The Liene Pearl N200 Pro is a dye-sublimation photo printer that produces vivid, colorful prints for journals, scrapbooks, and more, with an easy-to-use companion app for customization. This particular bundle includes 50 sheets of photo paper and five ink cartridges, though refills are relatively affordable at around $25 for 50 sheets.
Peacock
Peacock is a great way to catch up on a wealth of content from NBC’s various networks, whether you plan to rewatch The Office or catch up on all things Bravo. A Premium subscription also grants access to films from studios like Universal Pictures and DreamWorks, along with live sports and a host of Peacock Originals.
SwitchBot Bot Rechargeable
Do you ever wish you could turn on your coffee maker or hit the light switch without getting up? The SwitchBot Bot lets you do that. All you need to do is stick the inexpensive contraption on a standard button or rocker switch, which will allow you to control or schedule your pet feeder, garage door, and a wide range of other devices from your smartphone.
Ember Mug 3 (14-ounce)
Ember’s latest smart mug is designed to keep your coffee at the ideal temperature for up to three hours, with five temp settings between 120 and 145 degrees Fahrenheit. The third-gen version is similar to prior models; however, you can now adjust the heat settings directly on the mug and throw it in the dishwasher, which wasn’t the case before.
Paramount Plus (monthly subscription)
Paramount Plus Premium provides access to live sports as well as movies and shows from both platforms, including Tales of the Teenage Mutant Ninja Turtles, a host of Yellowstone spinoffs, Star Trek shows, Sonic the Hedgehog 3, Twin Peaks, Dexter, and more.
Apple Pencil (USB-C)
Apple’s entry-level stylus attaches magnetically to supported iPads, though it doesn’t support magnetic charging. Instead, you pair and charge it by connecting it to your device’s USB-C port. It includes tilt sensitivity and Apple Pencil Hover support, but it doesn’t offer double-tap gestures or pressure sensitivity.
Beats Studio Pro
The latest Beats Studio Pro feature improved noise cancellation, sound quality, and comfort. They also support lossless audio over USB-C and offer native support for a range of iOS and Android features like other Beats headphones. Read our review.
Nothing Ear 3A earbuds
You likely won’t find wireless earbuds with a more striking appearance than Nothing’s Ear 3A. They also sound fantastic, boast active noise cancellation, and offer robust EQ settings, making them a great pick for the price. Read our review.
Zepath USB-C Li-ion AA Batteries
Rechargeable AA batteries are handy until it’s time to charge them. Most wall chargers let you refill four, maybe six, at once. Zepath’s clever solution is to ship them in an easy-to-use USB-C charging case that charges eight simultaneously. The batteries themselves are long-lasting, too.
Samsung P9 microSD Express card
The P9 is great if you need more storage for your Nintendo Switch 2. The microSD Express card offers sequential read speeds of up to 800MB/s, but only gadgets with a microSD Express card slot can take full advantage of the highest speeds. That includes certain cameras, tablets, and drones with a microSD USH-I slot.
Xteink X4 Classic pocket e-reader
With a 4.3-inch E Ink screen, the Xteink X4 is smaller and more pocketable than any Kindle and Kobo model currently available. It has some drawbacks — it’s limited to TXT and EPUB files — but it’s great for tinkering; it also has a magnetic mount so you can stick it to the back of MagSafe-ready phones.
Amazon Fire TV Stick 4K Plus
Amazon’s last-gen Fire TV Stick 4K Plus streams in 4K and supports Dolby Atmos, Dolby Vision, and Alexa Plus. It also ships with the redesigned Fire OS and, unlike more recent models, still lets you sideload Android TV apps.
007 First Light
007 First Light, a third-person stealth action game from the studio behind Hitman, is one of the best action games we’ve played this year. It features a wide variety of missions that have you sneaking through enemy territory, engaging in hand-to-hand combat, and taking part in high-speed car chases — you know, classic Bond stuff. Read our review.
Fanttik S1 Pro Cordless Electric Screwdriver
Fanttik’s rechargeable electric screwdriver features three adjustable torque settings, a built-in LED light, and 16 S2 hardened steel bits for tackling everything from furniture assembly to small electronics repairs. It also comes with a magnetic storage case and supports USB-C.
Lego Project Hail Mary set
Outside of the Integrity, few spacecraft made as big a splash in 2026 as the one Ryland Grace piloted in Project Hail Mary. And now, thanks to Lego, you can construct a microscale replica of the titular Hail Mary, complete with a miniature Rocky figure.
Sony DualSense Wireless Controller
Sony’s DualSense Wireless Controller for the PlayStation 5 features immersive haptic feedback and resistance triggers, which feel great to use. It offers a built-in mic, a 3.5mm headphone jack, and a mute button; it also works with PC, macOS, and mobile devices via Bluetooth or USB-C.
Apple TV (six-month subscription)
Apple TV offers ad-free streaming of original shows and movies, including shows like Severance, Silo, Slow Horses, and Pluribus. The service also features a rotating selection of films, along with live Formula 1 races and Major League Soccer matches.
Hoto 3.6V Electric Screwdriver Kit Pro
Hoto’s rechargeable screwdriver is ideal for small repairs. It offers three torque modes, a rotational speed of up to 220RPM, and a helpful circular LED to illuminate dark spaces. The durable case also holds all 25 steel bits and an extension bar for getting into hard-to-reach places.
Moft Trackable Tripod Wallet
Moft’s Trackable Tripod Wallet is a MagSafe-compatible wallet that holds two cards and folds into an adjustable phone stand or small tripod. It also supports both Apple’s Find My network and Google’s Find Hub, offers Qi wireless charging, and includes a detachable shutter remote for taking photos.
Govee Table Lamp 2
Govee’s Table Lamp 2 is way more fun than your average table lamp. The color-changing device boasts built-in scheduling and more than 60 dynamic lighting effects, including preset scenes for various holidays. You can even create custom color combos for when “Dandelion” and other presets just aren’t cutting it.
Sharge Icemag 3 Power Bank
The Icemag 3 can wirelessly deliver up to 25W of power to compatible smartphones and up to 35W wired charging via USB-C. The 10,000mAh Qi2 charger can also act as a foldable kickstand, comes with an integrated USB-C cable that serves as a lanyard, and sports a transparent design with colorful RGB lighting.
Saros
Saros is a third-person sci-fi shooter in which players fight alien enemies on a mysterious planet trapped in a time loop. The PS5 exclusive features fast-paced combat, a variety of powerful weapons, and challenging boss fights. Read our review.
TP-Link Tapo Matter-Certified Smart Plug Mini
TP-Link’s Wi-Fi smart plug lets you remotely control lamps, fans, and other appliances using your phone or voice commands. It supports Matter, so it works with all major smart home platforms to set schedules and timers to automatically turn devices on or off.
Pixel Buds 2a
Google’s latest pair of wireless earbuds features crisp sound, a comfortable design, and solid active noise cancellation, which is great considering the budget-friendly price tag. They even offer built-in support for Gemini and, unlike the entry-level AirPods, a purple option! Read our review.
Ghost of Yōtei
Sucker Punch’s follow-up to Ghost of Tsushima is everything you could want in a sequel. The open-world revenge epic brims with fluid combat and a host of welcome narrative twists, all set against the gorgeous backdrop of Japan’s Edo period. It’s precisely the type of game photo modes were made for, even if you never use them.
Donkey Kong Bananza
Donkey Kong Bananza is one of the Switch 2’s first exclusive must-have titles. Its destructible environment allows you to smash your way through each of its 17 levels, and the paths you make will remain intact unless you specifically choose to reset the world. Your companion Pauline’s singing allows you to transform into animals that have abilities that allow you to reach previously inaccessible parts of levels. Read our review.
Lego Mandalorian N-1 Starfighter
Arguably the best thing to come out of The Book of Boba Fett was the Mandalorian’s N-1 Starfighter, which Lego has re-created here in miniature. It’s not quite as impressive as the 1,802-piece version, but the cheaper set still packs plenty of detail, including a spring-loaded shooter.
The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time
Nintendo’s latest Switch 2 exclusive is the best way to relive what is arguably the best Zelda game of all time. The revamped visuals look gorgeous, and while the game’s improved controls might annoy some purists who still cling to the heyday of ’98, we reckon most folks won’t bat an eye.
Twelve South AirFly SE
Twelve South’s entry-level Bluetooth transmitter delivers up to 25 hours of continuous use and lets you connect up to two pairs of headphones to an in-flight entertainment system, making it a great travel companion no matter where you’re going.
Catan (6th Edition)
In Catan, up to four players strategically compete and trade for resources. The iconic board game ends when one player reaches 10 “victory points,” which is achieved by constructing a sprawling civilization of roads, settlements, and cities using sheep, ore, wheat, and wood.
Nothing Ear (a)
Nothing’s Ear (a) earbuds have the same general design as the company’s previous earbuds — but you can get these in a snazzy yellow. They include ANC, more than decent sound quality, and other features like multipoint for well under $100. Read our impressions.
Control Resonant
Control Resonant is all about manipulation — of gravity, not people. Remedy’s excellent sequel to Control is an exhilarating action game, with ever-changing combat, twisted level design, and the weirdest traversal mechanics we’ve seen in a long time.
Kodak Charmera Keychain Digital Camera
The Chamera is not a good camera, but that’s not the point. Kodak’s charming, keychain-friendly tchotchke lets you snap lo-fi memories on a whim, with an ultra-small 1.6-megapixel sensor, a built-in LED flash, and the tiniest amount of storage.
Azul
Azul is a game in which you arrange patterned tiles in order to create the most beautiful tapestry possible. Arranging tiles in a strategic sequence will help you build your score while limiting your opponents’ ability to grow theirs.
CMF Headphone Pro
The market is flooded with surprisingly cheap yet good over-ear headphones with active noise cancellation. The Headphone Pro by CMF are certainly some of the best-looking out there, and they come in multiple colors.
Magna-Tile Classic 32-Piece Set
Magna-Tiles are a quintessential kids’ toy. The colorful, semi-translucent geometric shapes are a favorite here at The Verge, especially for those of us who prefer the simplicity of building with magnets rather than bricks.
Lego Botanicals Mini Bonsai Trees set
Lego’s 709-piece kit lets you construct three brick-ified bonsai trees, including a black pine, a ginkgo with yellow leaves, and a wisteria with hanging lilac flowers. It also comes with three ornamental displays, just in case you want a raised stand on which to showcase them.
Anker Laptop Power Bank
Anker’s 25,000mAh power bank sports two built-in USB-C cables (one of which is retractable), plus one USB-C and USB-A port. It tops out at 165W when charging two devices simultaneously, or 130W when charging three or four.
CMF Buds 2A
With 12.4mm drivers, active noise cancellation, and an IP54 rating, the CMF Buds 2A offer high-end features at an affordable price. You can also customize the sound, adjust ANC levels, and find the earbuds using the Nothing X app.
Wuben G5 rechargeable flashlight
The Wuben G5 flashlight is as small as a Zippo lighter, yet far more versatile, with a bright, rotatable light that can shine in numerous colors. It features USB-C charging and has an adjustable clip.
Anker 140W 2-in-1 USB-C to USB-C Cable
No one should have to deal with a rat’s nest behind their desk when devices like Anker’s 2-in-1 USB-C cable exist. The handy USB-C cable splits at the end, giving you the ability to charge up to two devices from a single port. That means you can split power between a phone and tablet or juice a 16-inch MacBook Pro to 50 percent in just 20 minutes using a single port.
Echo Dot Max
The Echo Dot Max features a flat design and a redesigned sound system, which delivers nearly three times the bass as its predecessor. It also offers improved conversation detection and better noise filtering, thanks to Amazon’s new AZ3 chip. Read our review.
Iniu P55-E2 Power Bank (10,000mAh)
Iniu’s P55-E2 Power Bank is an ultra-slim three-port USB charger that’s capable of fully recharging an iPhone 16 twice. It supports charging speeds of up to 45W via a USB-C port, which can deliver fast recharging for a range of gadgets.
Glocusent Bookmark Style Reading Light
Glocusent’s clip-in reading light features five brightness levels and multiple color temperature settings, ranging from a soft, warm glow that’s great for late-night reading to a brighter light that’s perfect for daytime use. The slim USB light easily slips into your pocket, too, so you can read anywhere, from a cozy bed to a dimly lit train.
Vantage
Vantage isn’t your typical open-world exploration game. It’s a sci-fi roguelike that has you traversing an alien planet alongside up to five players, each of whom must solve puzzles and complete their mission before their time, morale, or health runs out. It’s not as charming as Stonemaier’s award-winning Wingspan, but it is far more interesting.
LIFX Everyday Smart Light Bulbs (two-pack)
LIFX’s dimmable, color-changing bulbs offer Wi-Fi connectivity, simple setup, and Matter compatibility, so they work with any major smart home platform. The company is currently trialing a Thread beta that will bring the option to add Thread connectivity to all its Matter devices.
Yoto Mini (2024) speaker
A great first portable music player/speaker for kids. It uses insertable NFC cards (sold separately) to play music and stories to keep kiddos occupied and entertained without zoning out in front of the TV. This model supports Bluetooth and 3.5mm for private listening, too.
SwitchBot Standing Circulator Fan
A quiet, battery-powered fan that moves a lot of air despite its small size, the Switchbot Standing Circulator is a surprisingly versatile smart home addition.
Shokz OpenRun
The standard OpenRun are a lighter option than the newer OpenRun Pro 2, with a streamlined wraparound design, up to eight hours of battery life, and an IP67 rating for better water resistance. However, they don’t offer the same bass performance or USB-C charging.
JBL Go 4
The last-gen JBL Go 4 has been around for a bit, but the pocket-sized Bluetooth speaker still delivers with up to seven hours of runtime, dual-device connectivity, and an IP67 rating for water and dust resistance. You can also pair it with other Go 4 speakers for bigger stereo sound, if desired.
Pokémon Pokopia
Nintendo’s latest Pokémon spinoff for the Switch 2 is part Pokémon, part Minecraft, and part Animal Crossing. Yet, despite borrowing gameplay mechanics from each, the cozy life sim is more than the sum of its expansive, building-centric parts. It’s breezy and warm-hearted, with enough secrets to keep you occupied for hours on end.
Ugreen Nexode 240W USB-C Cable with Digital Display
The proliferation of miniature screens continues with Ugreen’s latest Nexode USB-C Cable, which features a full-color LCD screen on one end that displays current power draw (up to 240W). It also supports 480Mbps data transfer speeds and, unlike similar power meter-equipped cables, lets you turn off the screen when needed.
Kobo Remote
The Kobo Remote lets you turn pages on a compatible Kobo e-reader with the click of a button, so you can keep reading without reaching for the screen. It connects directly over Bluetooth and features a curved, handheld design that doesn’t require clipping anything onto your e-reader.
CMF Buds Pro 2
The Buds Pro 2 offer a surprisingly robust set of features for the price, including ANC and a transparency mode that lets you tune into your surroundings. Their included charging case also features a smart dial that lets you adjust the volume, while multipoint Bluetooth support lets them connect to two devices simultaneously.
Fujifilm Instax Mini 13
Fujifilm’s Instax Mini 13 is the best instant camera if you want to spend less than $100. While it lacks more advanced features, the simple instant camera takes good-quality shots quickly and easily and offers a built-in selfie mode along with a self-timer. It’s sold in a variety of fun colors that’ll especially appeal to kids and tweens.
Tile Pro
The Tile Pro is the company’s loudest Bluetooth tracker, one that benefits from a 500-foot range. It also features a user-replaceable battery, unlike some other Tiles, and works with both iOS and Android devices.
Camp Snap 2 digital camera
Camp Snap’s latest digital point-and-shoot camera may still lack a screen and capture only 8-megapixel stills, but it offers a slimmer design, faster performance, and filters right out of the box. That, along with several other new features, makes it easier for kids to use.

Chinese Data Center Tied to Alice Huang Found Using Nvidia's Powerful AI Chips
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-28
00:25:00 UTC
09:25:00 KST

Chinese Data Center Tied to Alice Huang Found Using Nvidia's Powerful AI Chips (yahoo.com) 43
			
		 	
				Friday Nvidia's stock showed a 30.95% drop over the last month, and a 37% drop from its October 2nd high of $239.24.  But it managed a 4.83% gain for the week — after some bad news hit Friday morning...
A Chinese data center with ties to Alice Huang (the suspected Chinese spy-agency asset) "has installed hardware with Nvidia's powerful Blackwell AI chips," writes Gizmodo, citing a Friday morning report from Bloomberg.  Those chips are "among Nvidia's most powerful products," writes Bloomberg, and their presence was confirmed by "a serial number specified in documents submitted to Chinese regulators in November," (offering the hardware as collateral for a $14 million loan, according to bank filings seen by Bloomberg News).
A second Chinese company (this one controlled by a Tokyo-based parent company) also described a server with Nvidia's advanced Blackwell chips in documents for a similar loan in late 2025 from, Bloomberg reports:
The submissions reveal for the first time that Nvidia's Blackwell processors found their way into a Chinese data center that has ties to Alice Huang and Megaspeed.   [Alice Huang has ties to "a number of companies" that invested in the second company, and its president is her husband Kang Wei.]  
These processors were until recently Nvidia's most powerful data center products, and they've always been illegal to ship to China without a US license.  President Donald Trump has explicitly said he doesn't want these chips — which OpenAI and Anthropic PBC use to train their most cutting-edge models — going to the Asian country, at least for now. Nvidia Chief Executive Officer Jensen Huang, meanwhile, once claimed they were too big to smuggle: a standard two-meter-tall GB300 NVL72 server rack, which contains 72 Blackwell Ultra chips and 36 Grace processors, can weigh more than 1,500 kilograms (3,300 pounds) and is about the size of a gigantic fridge...
Shanghai Shuoyao — the company that procured the GB300 rack and offered it as collateral — is active at the data center linked to Alice Huang and also has significant overlap with Singapore-based Megaspeed, which is under US investigation over concerns that it smuggled Nvidia chips to China, Bloomberg has reported. Shanghai Shuoyao, which hasn't responded to Bloomberg queries over the past year, confirmed this month in a rare public statement that it is one of Megaspeed's service providers, describing the partnership as "limited to ordinary commercial cooperation." 
Megaspeed, meanwhile, used a rendering of the Yangtze River Delta Project in 2024 to describe its largest data center to investors, dubbing the site a "specific area" facility in an unnamed country... The parameters they've outlined in construction plans reviewed by Bloomberg — specifically, the total processing power as compared to the energy input — would be extremely difficult to satisfy with chips from Chinese companies such as Huawei Technologies Co.
 
Bloomberg says Nvidia "has not answered repeated queries," though they add that Nvida "for years insisted that there was no black market for its products."
The article also notes that Megaspeed (where Alice Huang worked) "won a coveted stamp of approval from Nvidia within four days of its creation" in 2023, and went on to become Nvidia's largest customer in Southeast Asia.

This purple electric Chinese minivan can go up to 559 miles on a charge
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-28
00:03:37 UTC
09:03:37 KST

Electric minivans could be the ultimate in utility, with room for passengers, gear, and huge batteries. While VW’s ID Buzz only gave us 200 miles of real-world driving on a charge in 2023, China’s BYD has just announced a flagship purple peoplemover that can go an estimated 559 miles (900 km) on electricity.
This purple electric Chinese minivan can go up to 559 miles on a charge
The BYD Whale should cost around $45,000, not that you’d be able to buy one here.
It’s called the BYD Whale, and some back-of-the-napkin math suggests it lives up to the name. The van would be bigger than a Honda Odyssey, bigger than a Chrysler Pacifica, and bigger than a Toyota Sienna, at 17.3 feet (5,270mm) long, 6.5 feet (1,996mm) wide, 6 feet (1,815mm) tall, and with a 123-inch (3,130mm) wheelbase.
According to an auto-translated press release, the vehicle should have room for seven passengers and seven suitcases at a time, not that the company has shown off its interior yet. You can see a prominent sensor cluster on its forehead, though, and it’ll come equipped with LiDAR for advanced hands-free driving.
CarNewsChina points out it’s actually not the first with 900 km of CLTC battery range on board — Geely’s all electric Zeekr 009 minivan also claims to offer up to 900 km of range in one configuration. But BYD’s also claiming the can charge its battery from 10 percent to 97 percent in just nine minutes, and it get you from 10 percent to 70 percent in five minutes, according to Electrek.
The Whale will also come in a 248-mile (400 km) plug-in hybrid version as well, and the company’s planning to ship it in China in Q1 of 2027 for around 300,000 yuan. That’s roughly $45,000. As we’ve reported, Chinese cars are becoming quite competitive. Will they make it to the US?

====================================================================================================

Passkey blues
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-27
23:51:28 UTC
08:51:28 KST

Passkey blues
Passkeys are actually great: no more remembering passwords, no more phishing. Logins are just a click or press now, and maybe a biometric scan added to that.
They aren't without their problems though. For instance, they can't be backed up; not like your password that you can write down to your notebook at home. The passkey provider is responsible to keep your passkey intact and secure. The provider itself also must remain alive. If the provider goes down, you can't use that passkey.
That's quite the point though. Because, if you could backup your passkey, it could also be phished from you with a convincing enough email and a link to a fake website that asks for your passkey backup file.
But, it's not totally hopeless. You can add multiple passkeys to an account from different providers as a backup. For example, you can add a Windows Hello passkey, and then one with your password manager too. However, that flow isn't intuitive. And some web sites don't even allow multiple passkeys, like Costco:
Contrary to what Costco developers believe, I can have multiple passkey providers on the same device: my operating system, my password manager, and multiple physical security keys too. I'm sure Costco isn't alone in this, and many other web sites botch their implementation of passkeys. Some web sites let you keep your password along with passkey. That pretty much kills all the advantages of a passkey, and just turns it into a fancy "Face ID Login" flow.
Users are oblivious to the best practices of passkey-based authentication too. No user would think that they might need to add more than a passkey to their account in order to be able to recover it in the future. I don't know, maybe web sites should encourage it. I actually looked it up now, and apparently, FIDO recommends that approach too.
Account recovery is also inconsistent, and sometimes impossible. I believe account recovery is a greater problem than hacking. If you fail at providing a consistent recovery experience to your users, they'll just lose their account, and may not bother to create a new one.
Yes, there is email, and on some web sites, SMS. But these recovery flows are also prone to phishing. And neither email nor SMS stays forever. You should be able to have backup emails too, and the list of things to manage in order to use passkeys goes on and on.
The most guaranteed way to recover your account is having a backup physical security key that you won't lose, possibly kept in a safe or something. And PIN-based keys have, well, PINs, so you must not forget that either. Maybe, you should write it down in a notebook, heh.
Longevity of physical security keys is another unknown. My notebook would keep working 50 years from now, so my next of kin could access my data when I'm gone, would a security key last that long? Even if it remains functional and intact, will the software stack support it? We know that Windows currently doesn't support hardware from 20 years ago, let alone 50 years.
Perhaps, that should be factored in to passkey-based account design. A passkey might need to be refreshed, or tested once in a while, like Telegram's periodic "do you still remember your password?" prompts.
All added passkeys to an account needs to have a name, and that name must be able to be renamed. Telegram shows a physical security key like this:
I have multiple keys, and I don't know which one this is. So, if my key gets lost, how I can remove it from my accounts reliably?
Who manages the password managers?
Many password managers act as a passkey managers too, and that makes things extremely convenient: flash-speed sign-ins! However, 1Password, for instance, doesn't ask for biometrics when approving a sign-in, so, convenience comes with a cost: the loss of the second factor.
You see, in any authentication scheme, the security of second factor comes from "something you have", say, a physical key, versus "something you know", your password. So, password managers combine these two into a single, very convenient package, but you lose the two factors. It's only one factor now. If someone takes over your password manager account, it's game over for you.
The takeover of all password information from password manager servers has happened before, multiple times. So, it's not a one in a billion possibility. If you were an extremely security conscious person with necessary knowledge and skills, you could have a strict passkey regime that keeps passkeys in security keys only instead of the password managers.
There is no central record of which websites we have registered our passkeys with either. Yes, your password manager remembers the one you registered with them. But, for others, like security keys, you must go over every web site you can remember of one by one. Losing a security key can be a great headache, especially if it was stolen by someone who targets you.
What will the regular user do? Passkeys are only "convenient sign-in" for them. They don't know about the caveats. They may be left with unrecoverable accounts, hacked accounts en masse, and frustration could be the best outcome then.
We still have some work to do before passkeys can become mainstream. Until then, users should be extra careful when switching to passkeys; not everything glitters is gold.

The passkey provider is responsible to keep your passkey intact and secure. The provider itself also must remain alive. If the provider goes down, you can't use that passkey.
That's quite the point though. Because, if you could backup your passkey, it could also be phished from you with a convincing enough email and a link to a fake website that asks for your passkey backup file.
But, it's not totally hopeless. You can add multiple passkeys to an account from different providers as a backup. For example, you can add a Windows Hello passkey, and then one with your password manager too. However, that flow isn't intuitive. And some web sites don't even allow multiple passkeys, like Costco:
Contrary to what Costco developers believe, I can have multiple passkey providers on the same device: my operating system, my password manager, and multiple physical security keys too. I'm sure Costco isn't alone in this, and many other web sites botch their implementation of passkeys. Some web sites let you keep your password along with passkey. That pretty much kills all the advantages of a passkey, and just turns it into a fancy "Face ID Login" flow.
Users are oblivious to the best practices of passkey-based authentication too. No user would think that they might need to add more than a passkey to their account in order to be able to recover it in the future. I don't know, maybe web sites should encourage it. I actually looked it up now, and apparently, FIDO recommends that approach too.
Account recovery is also inconsistent, and sometimes impossible. I believe account recovery is a greater problem than hacking. If you fail at providing a consistent recovery experience to your users, they'll just lose their account, and may not bother to create a new one.
Yes, there is email, and on some web sites, SMS. But these recovery flows are also prone to phishing. And neither email nor SMS stays forever. You should be able to have backup emails too, and the list of things to manage in order to use passkeys goes on and on.
The most guaranteed way to recover your account is having a backup physical security key that you won't lose, possibly kept in a safe or something. And PIN-based keys have, well, PINs, so you must not forget that either. Maybe, you should write it down in a notebook, heh.
Longevity of physical security keys is another unknown. My notebook would keep working 50 years from now, so my next of kin could access my data when I'm gone, would a security key last that long? Even if it remains functional and intact, will the software stack support it? We know that Windows currently doesn't support hardware from 20 years ago, let alone 50 years.
Perhaps, that should be factored in to passkey-based account design. A passkey might need to be refreshed, or tested once in a while, like Telegram's periodic "do you still remember your password?" prompts.
All added passkeys to an account needs to have a name, and that name must be able to be renamed. Telegram shows a physical security key like this:
I have multiple keys, and I don't know which one this is. So, if my key gets lost, how I can remove it from my accounts reliably?
Who manages the password managers?
Many password managers act as a passkey managers too, and that makes things extremely convenient: flash-speed sign-ins! However, 1Password, for instance, doesn't ask for biometrics when approving a sign-in, so, convenience comes with a cost: the loss of the second factor.
You see, in any authentication scheme, the security of second factor comes from "something you have", say, a physical key, versus "something you know", your password. So, password managers combine these two into a single, very convenient package, but you lose the two factors. It's only one factor now. If someone takes over your password manager account, it's game over for you.
The takeover of all password information from password manager servers has happened before, multiple times. So, it's not a one in a billion possibility. If you were an extremely security conscious person with necessary knowledge and skills, you could have a strict passkey regime that keeps passkeys in security keys only instead of the password managers.
There is no central record of which websites we have registered our passkeys with either. Yes, your password manager remembers the one you registered with them. But, for others, like security keys, you must go over every web site you can remember of one by one. Losing a security key can be a great headache, especially if it was stolen by someone who targets you.
What will the regular user do? Passkeys are only "convenient sign-in" for them. They don't know about the caveats. They may be left with unrecoverable accounts, hacked accounts en masse, and frustration could be the best outcome then.
We still have some work to do before passkeys can become mainstream. Until then, users should be extra careful when switching to passkeys; not everything glitters is gold.

Painting 1990s anime scenes in Python
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-27
23:50:17 UTC
08:50:17 KST

Painting 1990s anime scenes in Python

Why drivers want cheap, dumb cars again
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-27
23:39:15 UTC
08:39:15 KST

Sign up for the Slatest to get the most insightful analysis, criticism, and advice out there, delivered to your inbox daily.
My 16-year-old son recently obtained his learner’s permit and is starting to have ideas about what he wants behind the wheel. In our driveway, we have a 2023 Fisker Ocean, which is an all-electric SUV with a solar panel on the roof, an enormous rotating center touch screen, and the ability to roll down all the windows and open the sunroof at the flip of a switch. There’s also a 2005 Honda Element, which can do none of those things. If it weren’t for an aftermarket audio unit, it wouldn’t even have Bluetooth capability.
My son always goes for the Element. In fact, he has elaborate plans for tricking it out down the road, with things like a wooden plank for sleeping on campouts. I should point out that he is part of what is supposed to be the first truly digitally native generation, yet his contempt for in-vehicle tech is something of a badge of honor.
He’s actually part of a significant trend. If you’ve driven a car produced in the past 20 years, you’ve definitely faced some struggles: fumbling through a complicated infotainment interface just to connect your phone, dealing with the annoying bings and boops if you’re maneuvering through a crowded parking lot or briefly drift out of a highway lane, wondering how much of your personal data that fancy new SUV is sharing with companies that want to sell you stuff. Automobiles have been “smart” for decades, and with self-driving technology and more advanced onboard computers, they’re getting smarter.
Cue the backlash. Like those tech-averse consumers who have ditched iPhones for flip phones, installed apps such as Blank Spaces that offer a stripped-down experience, or swapped their Apple and Android mobiles for minimalist alternatives from Light Phone or Punkt, an emerging cadre of drivers crave simple, throwback cars. Some of it is about price, some is sheer neurological overload. There’s also a paranoid fringe that sees high-tech vehicles as instruments of the surveillance state. Tucker Carlson brags that he ditched his modern Chevy pickup because it was spying on him. He now drives a defiantly disconnected model from 1987.
But it’s actually hard to find a reliable analog used car—one that rocks only an AM/FM radio and that might not even be equipped with AC. Automakers largely stopped making rudimentary vehicles back during the Clinton administration, mostly because an improved economy led customers to demand more features, and because larger and more profitable SUVs became popular. Precious few super-basic vehicles have survived, and the ones that have have usually racked up plenty of miles and have the scars to prove it. So buyers have a simple question: Can’t the engineering and marketing geniuses of the auto industry make a new dumb car that’s also cheap?
The auto industry is aware of my son’s opinions. Tucked in a 2026 report from QNX (a division of BlackBerry, which, yes, is still around) analyzing “software-defined vehicles” is a surprising nugget: The report asked, could a “deliberately minimalist, low-tech vehicle, designed to limit digital features and connectivity,” succeed? Almost 80 percent of the SDV experts surveyed said yes.
“I think it definitely points to some sentiment out there,” John Pelliccio, global product marketing director at QNX, told me. “Sometimes the tech is so in-your-face that it becomes difficult rather than supportive. I don’t want to go through seven different submenus to roll down my windows.”
He cautioned that the report surveyed views among SDV developers and wasn’t focused on consumers. QNX is also in the business of innovating with software platforms so that automakers can offer simpler and more intuitive user experiences. But he also acknowledged that consumers believe tech is making cars more expensive, a tricky issue to negotiate at a time when affordability is an issue that can swing national elections and Silicon Valley is confronted by a public with torches and pitchforks.
The disconnect is real: Automakers are contending with a cranky, financially stressed-out public that wants to smash everything from GOP political careers to dashboard touch screens. Why, they ask, has crappy tech taken over the driving experience? The backstory is less straightforward than you might think. For decades, car companies installed unpopular technology largely because they didn’t want to surrender the data business to Apple CarPlay and Android Auto. Struggling to attract the best interface designers, they ended up lost in a forest of ornate decision trees. (BMW, for one, was often criticized for creating an infotainment system that sent befuddled users into seemingly endless submenus.)
But a hard pivot may be on the way, just as Gen Z and their craving for old-school cars comes of age. A company that’s going all-in on low-tech is the Jeff Bezos–backed Slate Auto, an EV startup that broke cover in 2025 and expects to start delivering a $25,000 EV pickup by the end of this year. (The pickup can also be converted to an SUV for an additional $5,000 to $7,000.) The “Blank Slate” base vehicle has two seats, hand-crank windows, no infotainment, no speakers, and no exterior paint. If you want a red truck, you’ll have to get the polymer exterior wrapped in red. The vehicle has attracted a lot of attention for its no-frills philosophy and rock-bottom sticker price. The company says it has more than 180,000 reservations.
This contrasts dramatically with what carmakers were pushing just a few years ago. Inspired by Tesla, high-speed connectivity and over-the-air software updates were all the rage for a while there. Tesla owners boasted that they could go to sleep as an OTA update started and wake up to a new car the next morning. Auto-industry executives were dreaming of fresh business models that leveraged the massive amounts of data vehicles generate to offer lucrative subscription-based products. But it hasn’t always gone according to plan. Not to gang up on BMW, but in 2022 the company infamously experimented with charging owners to activate seat heaters. Customers revolted.
If the Slate truck takes off, it could create a dumb-car boom. YouTube influencer Marques Brownlee has already told his 1 million–plus subscribers that he likes the thing and considers it a good choice as a starter EV. Former Top Gear presenter James May took it out for a spin and offered an approving verdict. Motor Trend believes it could validate a niche. There’s no question that in short order, Slate has generated more positive buzz for a cheapskate pickup than Ferrari did when it rolled out a widely ridiculed first crack at an EV, the $615,000 Luce (designed by the man behind the iPhone, Jony Ive, no stranger to minimalism).
Not everyone is taking such an either/or approach. Ford thinks it can offer the best of both worlds. Earlier this year, the carmaker debuted a new EV pickup, the Fathom, designed to offer affordability without compromising on technology features. Kay Hart, general manager of Ford’s Model e electric division, told me that the company had to put itself in the mindset of a buyer concerned about affordability who thinks that a cheaper car equals one with fewer features.
“Our approach was to find out what our customers valued and figure out how we could bring that to the vehicle,” she said, pointing out that even the sub-$29,000 version of the Fathom is equipped with Ford’s hands-free, driver-assist tech, called BlueCruise. But she also said that Ford is aware of driver frustration when it comes to screen-based interfaces. “They are not looking to learn a new experience” when they slip into the driver’s seat, as she put it.
Ford sees the Fathom pickup as a way to offer cheap and nonannoying without having to lean into dumb. It helps to have enough industrial might to build the new pickup on a new EV engineering platform that is designed to reduce complexity and significantly lower costs. Slate, meanwhile, is subtracting by necessity and making that the core part of the marketing pitch. The critical question is whether Slate’s approach will be so successful that the big guys decide it’s worth abandoning decades of investments in automated paint shops, power windows, autonomous-driving systems, and dashboard-dominating touch screens that control everything from AC to interior lighting.
For the auto industry as a whole, making money on modest, “de-contented” vehicles is tough. That’s why Detroit favors large, fully loaded pickups and SUVs. Since the financial crisis, Ford and General Motors have reinvented themselves to be consistently profitable rather than chase market share; they’ve abandoned the sedan segment, where consumers have typically looked for starter vehicles like Toyota Corollas and Honda Civics. Automotive News reports that the average transaction for a new car has topped $50,000, a whopping $2,500 increase from a year ago.
A better bottom line is nothing to dismiss, but if it comes at the cost of losing first-time buyers, a carmaker has a long-term vulnerability. The classic example, of course, is the barebones Volkswagen Beetle. It first hit the U.S. in the late 1940s and early 1950s, when massive tailfinned cars ruled the roads (those flamboyant Chevy Bel Airs and Caddy Eldorados were the luxury SUVs of their era). The Beetle caught on, becoming a countercultural icon, selling more than 21 million units globally and reviving the German postwar economy.
It isn’t just curmudgeonly car enthusiasts jonesing for the raw thrill of driving an old Porsche who are setting the tone. Basically, what Gen Z wants is the right to get into a car, rev the engine, put on some music, and drive. The auto industry used to serve that more elemental desire quite well. If it can learn to embrace cheap and tolerate a bit more dumb, it could again.

Roborock, Dreame, and Eufy are cutting robovac prices in half for Black Friday
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-27
23:32:17 UTC
08:32:17 KST

Black Friday is here, and if you want a little help keeping your floors clean this holiday season, now is a great time to pick up a robot vacuum. Some of the best models we’ve tested over the last year or two are on sale for up to 60 percent off, whether you’re after a basic robovac for quick cleanups or a more advanced model that vacuums, mops, and practically takes care of itself.
The best robot vacuums we’ve tested are up to 60 percent off for Black Friday
From self-cleaning vacs to bots that also mop, you can save hundreds on many of our favorite models.
This year’s sale has made some pricier robot vacuums a lot more affordable. The Eufy Omni C28, for example, now costs about what you’d expect to pay for a budget bot, while the Matic — which impressed us with its almost humanlike navigation — is getting a rare discount in the form of a bundle. If none of those are quite what you’re looking for, we’ve also found good deals on models from Roborock, Dreame, and more, with picks below for a variety of budgets and cleaning needs.
Roborock Qrevo Curv 2 Flow
Roborock’s first roller-mop robot vacuum, the Curv 2 Flow, features flagship specs at a midrange price, including excellent mopping, a retractable mop cover, 20,000Pa suction, AI-powered obstacle avoidance, and a compact design. Read our review.
Roborock Q10 S5 Plus
Despite lacking a multifunction dock, the budget-friendly robot vacuum and mop offers an impressive set of features for the price, including AI-powered obstacle avoidance, sonic mopping, automatic mop lifting, and a self-emptying dock.
Matic robot vacuum
The Matic robot vacuum and mop is ideal for cluttered homes with pets, multiple floors, and a mixture of carpets and hardwood. It offers almost humanlike navigation, tackling high-pile rugs and mopping up wet spills. The quiet bot also requires less maintenance than many rivals thanks to its onboard water tank and dust bag. Read our review.
Eufy Omni C28
The Eufy Omni C28 combines excellent mopping performance with a small self-cleaning dock that’s well suited for apartments and other smaller spaces. It navigates around furniture and into tight areas with ease, while its roller mop does a great job cleaning hard floors.
Dreame X60 Ultra
The Dreame X60 Ultra is a powerful robot vacuum and mop that does a great job of cleaning carpets thanks to its dual roller brushes and 35,000Pa of suction. It can climb over tall thresholds, squeeze under low furniture, and automatically detach its mop pads when vacuuming carpets.
Narwal Flow 2
The Narwal Flow 2 is an excellent robot vacuum for hardwood floors. A superb mop with a wide flat roller pad and a good vacuum, the Flow 2 also has top-notch navigation and obstacle detection. Its sleek base station is also less of an eyesore than most models. Read our review.
Roborock Saros 20
Roborock’s Saros 20 stands out for its ability to climb tall thresholds, avoid obstacles with impressive accuracy, and clean under low furniture thanks to its slim design. It also combines powerful 36,000Pa suction with great spinning mop pads, which is why we recommend it for homes with multiple floor types and tricky transitions between rooms.
Ecovacs Deebot X12 OmniCyclone
Ecovacs’ robot vacuum and mop can pretreat stains, keep carpets dry with a smart mop cover, and deliver powerful 22,000Pa suction. It also includes an all-in-one dock that automatically empties the dustbin, refills the water tanks, and washes the mop with heated, pressurized water.
Switchbot K11 Plus
The Matter-compatible SwitchBot K11 Plus is ideal for small apartments, bedrooms, and home offices. It can easily navigate around chair and desk legs and into tight spaces that most other bots miss. It also features a commendable 6,000Pa of suction power — which is great for this price point — and works well on both hardwood and carpet.
Dreame X50 Ultra
With high suction power, an improved brush system, and the ability to scale thresholds up to 6cm, the X50 Ultra stands out as a great robot vacuum-mop hybrid. While its 20,000Pa suction isn’t quite as powerful as the X60 Ultra’s and it can’t tackle especially high thresholds as well, it’s still an excellent option. Its edge cleaning also isn’t quite as effective as the X60 Ultra’s, but you’re otherwise getting many of the same features for a lot less.
Dreame L40 Ultra Gen 2
The Dreame L40 Ultra Gen 2 delivers powerful 25,000Pa suction, AI-powered obstacle avoidance, and a self-cleaning dock that empties the dustbin, washes the mop, and refills its water tanks. Unlike the X50 Ultra, however, it lacks the ability to climb high thresholds or stairs.
Narwal Freo X Ultra
The Freo X Ultra features spinning triangular mop pads and can swing slightly to reach baseboards. Its large charging dock holds two big water tanks, while Narwal’s disposable onboard bin compresses the dust. It’s also relatively quiet when it returns to the dock, and its anti-tangle brush and 8,200Pa of suction perform well on most carpets.

Yooka-Laylee’s free upgrade is skipping Xbox
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-27
23:30:32 UTC
08:30:32 KST

Playtronic Games is getting ready to launch Yooka-Laylee and the Impossible Lair: Definitive Edition as a free update on PlayStation 5, Switch 2, and PC on December 15th. Unfortunately for Xbox owners, the developer says it “can’t commit to any additional platforms for the foreseeable future.” In a statement posted on X, Playtronic said that it understands the frustration some players may feel, but that bringing the Definitive Edition to Xbox just isn’t viable “given the technological challenges and costs,” and reminded people that they’re a small team.
Yooka-Laylee’s free upgrade is skipping Xbox
Playtronic Games says Yooka-Laylee’s Definitive Edition won’t come to Xbox because of ‘technological challenges and costs.’
Some, like The Game Business editor-in-chief Christopher Dring, have speculated that Xbox’s smaller install base has made it harder for some indie devs to “financially justify” launching on Xbox. Others, like John Linneman of Digital Foundry, have countered that Yooka-Laylee and the Impossible Lair: Definitive Edition is ultimately a minor update and pointed out that the Xbox Series X version was already available in native 4K, unlike the PS4 and Switch versions.

Sony’s 1000XM4C headphones are a Black Friday bargain at $198
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-27
23:20:00 UTC
08:20:00 KST

Instead of purely looking forward with its headphone designs, Sony went back to its popular WH-1000XM4 design and made some improvements to make it even better. The 2026 version, called the WH-1000XM4C, sounds nearly as good as the pricier XM6 model (currently $348), but is down to $198 during Black Friday at Amazon, Best Buy, and Walmart. That’s $100 off their October launch price.
Sony’s 1000XM4C headphones are a Black Friday bargain at $198
All three colors (including the stunning lavender) are down to their lowest price yet just a few months after launch.
There’s a reason why Sony went back to refine the XM4. Our former reviewer Chris Welch praised them for being super comfortable and for having close to perfect features for the price, especially compared to its successor, the XM3 (still my daily driver, for what it’s worth). The XM4s had better sound quality and noise cancellation, plus improved mics, multipoint connectivity, and a sensor that can detect when you remove them to automatically pause audio. Now, with the XM4C, there are further improvements, most notably a 10-band equalizer in the app (compared to the original XM4’s five-band) that my colleague John Higgins says really helped the headphones’ midrange frequencies open up.
These are a great buy, but they aren’t your only Sony headphones deal to consider right now. Sony’s WH-1000XM5 are available during Black Friday at Best Buy for $169, their lowest price yet from a major retailer. There are upsides and downsides to this model: They feature improved noise cancellation thanks to more mics, despite using the same ANC chip as the XM4C, but they can’t fold up as compactly, making them a little bigger to pack in your bag. Either one you choose, you’ll have some great headphones to listen to going into 2027.
