Google's DeepCrossAttention achieves comparable model quality up to 3x faster
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-27
23:06:29 UTC
08:06:29 KST

November 23, 2026
Kyriakos Axiotis, Staff Research Scientist, and Thomas Fu, Principal Engineer, Google Research
We present DeepCrossAttention (DCA), an architecture that enhances transformer networks by using learnable weights and depth-wise cross-attention to dynamically combine outputs from previous layers, preventing the information dilution seen in traditional residual connections. Presented in our ICML 2025 paper, this architectural improvement allows models to achieve the same quality up to three times faster with a negligible increase in parameters.
Think of a traditional deep learning model like a giant game of "Telephone": data passes through layer after layer, with each layer changing the information slightly. If a network has dozens or hundreds of layers, the original learning signal can become severely degraded or lost by the time it reaches all layers, which is known as the vanishing gradient problem in AI.
To overcome this challenge and enable the training of networks with dozens or hundreds of layers, transformers rely heavily on residual connections, also known as “skip” connections, which act like an information highway or a shortcut. Instead of forcing the data to pass exclusively through the complicated layer, a duplicate copy of the original input skips right past this layer and is added directly to its output. This ensures that the model always has the original raw data to refer back to.
The standard residual connection implements a simple operation: point-wise summation of a layer's output with its input. While computationally efficient, this simple process has a key limitation: it treats all prior representations with equal weight, leading to information dilution in deep networks. As features propagate through many layers, specific, high-resolution details from earlier layers can be averaged out or distorted, making it difficult for the network to selectively retrieve them. This forces the network to recompute them, reducing learning efficiency.
In our ICML 2025 paper, "DeepCrossAttention: Supercharging Transformer Residual Connections," we studied the shortcomings of the traditional residual architecture and introduced DeepCrossAttention (DCA), which generalizes residual connections by allowing each layer to dynamically select and route features from the history of preceding layers. By allowing the model to dynamically choose which previous layers are most important at any given moment, DCA achieves comparable or improved learning, while reducing the number of parameters needed by up to 30-40%.
Instead of treating skip connections as a rigid, additive pipeline, DCA reframes residual learning as a dynamic routing problem. Rather than passing all prior layers forward with equal weight, DCA introduces a lightweight, layer-wise module that decides how to best combine previous embeddings on a per-token basis.
The architecture introduces two primary mechanisms:
- Dynamic layer routing: For every token processed, DCA computes a unique set of mixing weights using a static, learnable base combined with an input-dependent projection. This allows the network to adaptively shift its processing structure — ranging from purely sequential to fully parallel — based on the specific context of the token.
- Depth-wise cross-attention (split Q/K/V): While standard transformer attention operates across tokens in a sequence, DCA applies attention across layers in depth. Each token queries a dynamically weighted mix of all prior layer embeddings, unlocking the model's full historical context without the computational penalty usually required to scale traditional attention.
By allowing the network to dynamically select and retrieve high-resolution features from earlier layers, DCA eliminates the information dilution inherent to classic residual connections.
To test whether DCA can overcome information dilution in practice, we evaluated our approach across controlled synthetic setups, large-scale language modeling benchmarks, and image classification tasks.
DCA demonstrated significant improvements in convergence speed, training stability, and final model quality across these experiments.
On the colossal, cleaned version of Common Crawl's web crawl corpus (C4) and 1 Billion Word Language Model Benchmark (LM1B) datasets, DCA reached the performance of standard transformers in up to five times fewer training steps. When trained for the same duration or number of steps, DCA consistently achieved lower perplexity — how well a probability model predicts a sample — than standard residual connections, effectively extracting more representational capacity from the same training budget. To achieve a perplexity of <14 on LM1B, a standard transformer with fixed width required 42 layers, whereas the DCA model achieved a lower perplexity with only 30 layers — a 29% reduction in depth and parameter count.
One of the most common trade-offs in AI is that better models are often much larger and slower to run. DCA adds a tiny number of parameters — less than 0.2% of the total model size — making it negligible in terms of compute and memory footprint, while increasing memory bandwidth, as representations from different layers are presented intact rather than blended together. Interestingly, because DCA can be initialized to behave identically to a standard residual network, it doesn’t require training from scratch: retrofitting DCA onto an already pre-trained transformer and briefly continuing training reduced perplexity by 0.19 on LM1B, compared to just 0.02 for continued standard training.
As large language models (LLMs) are used across a broader range of domains, optimizing their training processes and underlying architectures is critical for scaling and unlocking new capabilities. DCA and similar dynamic residual techniques are already seeing significant adoption and success. The rapid emergence of these methodologies highlights dynamic routing as a highly active and promising area of research.
Developed by Moonshot AI, attention residuals share DCA's core principles of replacing fixed addition with selective retrieval. Instead of blindly summing every prior layer's output, they use a learnable softmax attention mechanism paired with a layer-specific pseudo-query. This allows each layer to selectively weight and retrieve representations from earlier depths. By preventing the unbounded growth of hidden states and the dilution of early-layer information, this method improves gradient stability and boosts performance on reasoning-heavy tasks like coding and mathematics. To maintain computational efficiency at scale, a "Block AttnRes" variant partitions layers into blocks, recovering the benefits of full attention with significantly less memory overhead. The recent Inkling 2 model developed by Thinking Machines also adopted this design in addition to DCA’s cross attention (Q/K/V split) mechanism.
Hyper-connections expand the residual stream into multiple parallel channels, offering a form of memory scaling that goes beyond simply increasing model width or depth. They use a fixed-size stack where layer outputs are aggregated and mixed using learnable mixing matrices. However, this unconstrained mixing breaks the traditional identity mapping property of residual connections, which can cause amplification or attenuation of signals, and lead to training instability at scale.
To address instability in standard hyper-connections, manifold-constrained hyper-connections apply specific constraints to the mixing matrices. By forcing these matrices to live on a defined manifold — specifically, constraining them to be doubly stochastic using the Sinkhorn-Knopp algorithm — manifold-constrained hyper-connections restore stable signal propagation. This approach prevents exploding or vanishing gradients while preserving the rich information flow and expressivity of the widened residual streams, allowing models to scale robustly.
DCA demonstrates that transitioning from static residual connections to dynamic, attention-based routing is a powerful strategy for building more efficient models. By leveraging an expanded residual stream and depth-wise cross-attention, DCA empowers deep networks to selectively retrieve and integrate token embeddings across different layers. We expect that next-generation models will dynamically route and weigh past layer representations based on the specific tokens or inputs being processed. This will allow for the effective use of all the model layers as specialized experts working in tandem, with maximum information sharing and no redundancy, leading to more expressivity and deeper interactions. We look forward to seeing how these multi-depth interactions will shape the next generation of transformer architectures.
This work was a collaboration between researchers at Google and academic partners, including Mike Heddes, Adel Javanmard, MohammadHossein Bateni, and Vahab Mirrokni, whose contributions were critical for the development of this work.

====================================================================================================

Tabernetes: A Kubernetes cluster whose nodes are browser tabs
Spain
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-28
00:00:30 CET
08:00:30 KST

A Kubernetes cluster whose nodes are browser tabs
Open the page and your phone is a node. The scheduler sends it a pod, and the pod's program runs on a Linux kernel booted in the tab. Close the tab and the node is gone, and Kubernetes starts the pod on someone else's.
Scan to become a node, or use the browser you are reading this in.
Joining downloads about 5 MB and runs a small Linux guest in the tab, on one core, until you close the tab.
Topology
The control plane in the middle, a node for each tab, and each Service on the way to the pods behind it. A node is filled when its pod is running. An orange dot is a request you sent, on its way to the pod that answered, and a green one is a tile of the picture on its way to the node that rendered it.
Boot time
How long a tab takes to start its pod: from the tab being handed the pod to the pod's program running, which is the page loading, the kernel booting and the image being mounted. The last 0 starts.
No pod has started yet.
Request latency
How long the cluster takes to answer: from the agent handing a request to the Service to the answer being back from a pod in someone's tab, timed on the cluster's machine. The last 0 requests, from every visitor, by the milliseconds each took.
No request has been answered yet.
Ask the cluster
Send a request to a Service. One of the pods behind it answers, on whoever's phone that is, and counts the visit in a Redis on someone else's.
Render a picture
Press the button and the cluster draws a Mandelbrot set. The picture is split into 64 tiles, and each node's pod computes its share in awk, on the Linux in its tab. Point at a tile to see which device drew it.
One picture at a time, for everyone.
Services
The cluster's Services that have a pod on a tab behind them, read from the API server.
Happening now
Nothing yet. A tab that joins shows up here.
The fleet
What the nodes are, by what their browsers say.
The nodes
No tab is a node right now. Yours can be the first.
How it works
- A tab joins. The join page holds a socket open to an agent beside the API server, and the agent registers a Node for the tab.
- The scheduler sends a pod. The agent starts the pod's container with tabc, a container runtime whose containers run in browser tabs.
- The tab boots Linux. A kernel compiled to WebAssembly runs the pod's program on the tab's CPU and memory. The pod's files and its address stay on the cluster's machine.
A pod asks for a tab in one line
The control plane is an ordinary Kubernetes, and a pod is an ordinary pod. The marked line is the only one that is new: the scheduler puts a pod that has it on a tab, and every other pod on the cluster's own machine.
apiVersion: v1
kind: Pod
metadata:
  name: hello
spec:
  runtimeClassName: tab
  containers:
    - name: hello
      image: alpine
      command: ["sh", "-c", "echo hello from $(uname -m) in a tab; sleep 3600"]

====================================================================================================

'Everybody Is Going to Be Made Obsolete'
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-27
22:12:27 UTC
07:12:27 KST

'Everybody Is Going to Be Made Obsolete'

====================================================================================================

Source Control from Hell
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-27
22:06:44 UTC
07:06:44 KST

My first office job was at an enterprise that built software for a big telecom: a massive Delphi help desk application on Windows.
Let me tell you about how we managed source control process at the time.
First, you download the code from a shared folder (hosted on a trusted Windows server in a locked room). Build your thing, run the app, test it locally. Then you upload your changes to the shared folder and create a new ticket in the internal task tracker (also a Windows app). The ticket should have a list of the changes you’ve made, in a rich text editor, in a manual format somewhat resembling a patch. But it wasn’t a patch; it couldn’t simply be applied with a tool. Instead, the tech lead looks at the shared folder and your task and copies the relevant changes to his own integration folder. This usually took him a full day because the shared folder had a lot of changes, not all of them were ready to be integrated. Needless to say, if you simply pull a snapshot of the code from the shared folder, it would most likely fail to compile.
The most common problem was two people overwriting each other’s work. So if you wanted to upload your stuff to the shared folder, you had to stand up and announce in a loud voice that you were using the shared folder. No chat apps back in the day.
Imagine starting your career with this development experience! I did my best to improve it. At the time, almost everyone used FAR Manager, so I built a plugin that locked the folder when someone was using it, so others would get a warning. It became very popular because nobody really liked the old yelling system.
Then in 2009 I found git. It was still early days, way before GitHub went mainstream, but my tiny little brain was blown away. Holy shit, it maintains old versions. And you can work locally. And you can sync and exchange deltas. And it was insanely fast (compared to the very aptly named TortoiseSVN I’d experimented with earlier). I must have rewatched Linus’ talk a billion times. I created git repos for every project I had, preached it to my friends, and even gave my first tech talk about git at my university.
Sadly, my diplomatic skills were extremely rudimentary, and I couldn’t convince our tech lead to adopt git. He mumbled something about business value and how this new git thing lacked one. I left the company after two years but kept meeting up with old colleagues for beer. They were still using the shared folder process many years later. It was crude, it was hellish, and it worked for them just fine.

====================================================================================================

Allstate has been suffering an outage since Monday
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-27
21:52:42 UTC
06:52:42 KST

On Monday, November 23rd, Allstate began dealing with an outage that has impacted its website, mobile apps, and roadside assistance service. It’s just the latest company, including Microsoft and JPMorgan Chase, to suffer an outage in the last few weeks. As of Friday afternoon, access to accounts through the app and site appeared to still be down. When asked for comment, the company pointed us to a notice on its site reading:
Allstate has been suffering an outage since Monday
One of the biggest insurance companies in the country has been dealing with a major outage for five days.
Allstate customers can reach us in many ways, including online, by phone and through local offices. Some access has been restricted over the last 48 hours, but we continue to be available for customers and respond to and settle claims. To the extent service interruptions impact a customer’s ability to make a payment, they won’t lose coverage or be charged late fees.
Opening the app pops up a notification saying the company is working to “restore access as soon as possible,” and encourages customers to contact the company by phone to file and manage claims. Users have flooded X and Reddit with complaints about the outage, some of whom have either been left waiting for roadside assistance or have been unable to insure new vehicles.
Allstate hasn’t publicly identified a cause for the outage.

9/UX: a Macintosh-inspired desktop for 9front
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-27
21:48:14 UTC
06:48:14 KST

9/UX brings the classic Mac and A/UX desktop (a spatial desktop, a global menu bar, consistent controls and dialogs, icons, and resource-driven presentation) to 9front, built the Plan 9 way: rio and libdraw draw the screen, 9P exposes every application's UI state and commands as files, namespaces define the working environment, the plumber opens documents, and ndb holds human-editable metadata.
Keep the Macintosh's direct-manipulation desktop,
but make every meaningful object, action, and resource visible as a Plan 9 file.
It is not an emulator, a Macintosh ABI, or an A/UX reimplementation. Native 9front programs stay first-class, and existing rio programs (acme, sam, rc windows, nested rio) keep working unchanged beside 9/UX apps.
The pieces:
- rio fork (rio -u): a desktop layer, a global menu bar that follows the focused app, title-bar chrome, and a drag broker, all under /dev/ux. With 9/UX mode off it's stock rio.
- Desktop: volume icons, spatial folder windows, rename, duplicate, Get Info, Trash, remembered layout, and double-click to open documents through the plumber. A remote 9P server works as a volume.
- libux, the Toolbox: windows, menus, dialogs, alerts, and controls, each app serving its control tree over 9P, so echo activate >.../ctl presses a button.
- uxd gathers every app's tree at /mnt/ux with per-app policy; uxmeta keeps associations, volumes and layout in ndb files; resfs serves classic ICN#, icl8, menu, dialog and string resources at /mnt/res; clipd is a typed clipboard kept in step with rio's snarf.
These are real frames, not mockups. The GUI tests embed h9 with a headless screen, inject mouse and keyboard input, and drive the 9P files at the same time.
Macintosh structure, Plan 9 colour: butter title bars from rio's selection yellow, acme's cream and olive, and rio's greygreen for the desktop, so 9/UX windows and legacy rio windows sit together on one screen. Everything reads in one bit, uses a sixteen-colour palette, and is drawn in whole pixels. Every graphic is original to 9/UX; it ships no Apple resources. The mockups below are generated from the same palette and metrics files as the code.
The design document describes 9/UX and its v0.1 acceptance criteria; release.md has where each one stands, the end-to-end demo, and the open issues. Every interface between components is a file protocol in docs/protocols, and the visual design covers the palette, fonts, metrics, chrome, icons and cursors.
The source is on GitHub, under the MIT license. Development runs on h9, which runs 9front's own compilers, mk and rio directly from the host shell, without booting a VM. scripts/h9.sh clones and builds the pinned h9 on its first run:
git clone https://github.com/progrium/9ux
cd 9ux
scripts/h9.sh --rc 'mk'
scripts/h9.sh --gfx rio/7.out -u
scripts/test.sh
Use rio/6.out on amd64. development.md and testing.md have the details.
9/UX is at v0.1 and under active development. Interfaces and behaviour change without notice.
Please report bugs and send questions through GitHub issues.
The 9/UX mark in the menu bar is a pixel-art Glenda by Dan Fessler. Glenda, the Plan 9 bunny, was created by Renée French.

It is not an emulator, a Macintosh ABI, or an A/UX reimplementation. Native 9front programs stay first-class, and existing rio programs (acme, sam, rc windows, nested rio) keep working unchanged beside 9/UX apps.
The pieces:
- rio fork (rio -u): a desktop layer, a global menu bar that follows the focused app, title-bar chrome, and a drag broker, all under /dev/ux. With 9/UX mode off it's stock rio.
- Desktop: volume icons, spatial folder windows, rename, duplicate, Get Info, Trash, remembered layout, and double-click to open documents through the plumber. A remote 9P server works as a volume.
- libux, the Toolbox: windows, menus, dialogs, alerts, and controls, each app serving its control tree over 9P, so echo activate >.../ctl presses a button.
- uxd gathers every app's tree at /mnt/ux with per-app policy; uxmeta keeps associations, volumes and layout in ndb files; resfs serves classic ICN#, icl8, menu, dialog and string resources at /mnt/res; clipd is a typed clipboard kept in step with rio's snarf.
These are real frames, not mockups. The GUI tests embed h9 with a headless screen, inject mouse and keyboard input, and drive the 9P files at the same time.
Macintosh structure, Plan 9 colour: butter title bars from rio's selection yellow, acme's cream and olive, and rio's greygreen for the desktop, so 9/UX windows and legacy rio windows sit together on one screen. Everything reads in one bit, uses a sixteen-colour palette, and is drawn in whole pixels. Every graphic is original to 9/UX; it ships no Apple resources. The mockups below are generated from the same palette and metrics files as the code.
The design document describes 9/UX and its v0.1 acceptance criteria; release.md has where each one stands, the end-to-end demo, and the open issues. Every interface between components is a file protocol in docs/protocols, and the visual design covers the palette, fonts, metrics, chrome, icons and cursors.
The source is on GitHub, under the MIT license. Development runs on h9, which runs 9front's own compilers, mk and rio directly from the host shell, without booting a VM. scripts/h9.sh clones and builds the pinned h9 on its first run:
git clone https://github.com/progrium/9ux
cd 9ux
scripts/h9.sh --rc 'mk'
scripts/h9.sh --gfx rio/7.out -u
scripts/test.sh
Use rio/6.out on amd64. development.md and testing.md have the details.
9/UX is at v0.1 and under active development. Interfaces and behaviour change without notice.
Please report bugs and send questions through GitHub issues.
The 9/UX mark in the menu bar is a pixel-art Glenda by Dan Fessler. Glenda, the Plan 9 bunny, was created by Renée French.

Quad9 Blocks Nginx2root.com
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-27
21:03:56 UTC
06:03:56 KST

An open DNS recursive service for free security and high privacy
Quad9 is a free service that replaces your default ISP or enterprise Domain Name Server (DNS) configuration. When your computer performs any Internet transaction that uses the DNS (and most transactions do), Quad9 blocks lookups of malicious host names from an up-to-the-minute list of threats. This blocking action protects your computer, mobile device, or IoT systems against a wide range of threats such as malware, phishing, spyware, and botnets, and it can improve performance in addition to guaranteeing privacy. The Quad9 DNS service is operated by the Swiss-based Quad9 Foundation, whose mission is to provide a safer and more robust Internet for everyone.
670M+
Average Daily Blocks
230+
Resolver Clusters in over 110 countries
25+
Threat Intelligence Providers
Privacy
How Quad9 protects your privacy
When your devices use Quad9 normally, no data containing your IP address is ever logged in any Quad9 system. Connections can employ encryption if your system supports it, and the entire Quad9 platform has been designed to be GDPR-compliant from the first public announcement in 2017.
Read more about Quad9’s privacy
Why Quad9 is dedicated to data privacy
Every transaction on the Internet starts with a DNS event. This name lookup reveals critically sensitive data about the person triggering that transaction. The nature of those name lookups has created a strong and dangerous motivation for commercialization of personal data from DNS recursive resolver services. Quad9 is the only large DNS resolver with a founding charter that includes privacy as a primary goal, and the Quad9 team is devoted to the concept of keeping personal data under the control of the end user.
        As the CEO of Alta Associates, the leading executive search firm in Cybersecurity, and Founder of the Executive Women’s Forum on Information Security, Risk Management & Privacy, I have a unique understanding of the challenges cybersecurity leaders face. Quad9 provides these leaders with an actionable way to protect their systems against threats while preserving their end-users privacy.
        Digital leaders in Switzerland are reviewing IT strategies to ensure best practices across their digital transformation journeys. Companies like Quad9 are providing key solutions in this area, supporting everyday web traffic, fundamental to the internet puzzle. We are delighted to host this important service at Equinix.
        The National Cyber Security Centre (NCSC) of Switzerland supports the idea of conducting Quad9’s activities in a non-profit organisation (NPO) in Switzerland. As a neutral location, Switzerland is the premier site for such activities. Together, as a Public Private Partnership (PPP), we can make the internet safer and position Switzerland as a trustworthy location.
        Quad9 provides an invaluable service keeping internet users secure. Their continued development as an international non-profit, domiciled in a place with strong privacy laws, will ensure that this mission continues to be successful.
        End users have made it clear to industry that privacy should be a central focus for Internet platforms and infrastructure. This demand-side dynamic cannot be fulfilled with the promises of encryption technology alone, however. Quad9 understands the need to compliment complement their its secure and privacy-preserving protocols with policies and practices that protect users too. Based in Europe, Quad9’s DNS service now leverages a strong regulatory environment in the public interest.
        Privacy matters, but is increasingly endangered. We’re happy to have another major internet infrastructure provider joining the European tech ecosystem, illustrating again that privacy is the future for European tech.
        This step by Quad9 is a good example of what may need to be done to increase confidence in the Internet globally. It also demonstrates the importance of all stakeholders playing their respective roles - technical community providing a robust technical solution, private sector enabling and supporting the model, and robust government regulation providing a trust-guarantee. A welcome addition to the Internet infrastructure
        The privacy and security of Internet users depend on the availability of private and secure services, critically including Domain Name System resolvers. Digitale Gesellschaft operates one such DNS resolver for Switzerland, and we welcome Quad9 and its established global reach to Switzerland.
        With its transparent privacy policy, board of directors of multiple stakeholders, and strong presence on six continents, Quad9 sets an example for public domain name services for the benefit of the common good. I am proud to be on the governing board of the Quad9 project during its important transition to European governance. This marks an important milestone for global privacy, with the protection of Swiss law and oversight now available to DNS users around the world.
        I’m really impressed by the privacy architecture of Quad9. In addition to providing great security as a DNS resolver, Quad9 doesn’t collect or retain personal data. Now, it is relocating to Switzerland, where it will become subject to the rigors of Swiss data protection law, which is similar to GDPR. In addition, it obtained findings of law from the Swiss government whereby it won’t be required to retain personal information, nor will it be subject to requests from law enforcement or national security
        As a European DNS vendor, we welcome Quad9’s decision to relocate to Switzerland, providing a much needed GDPR-guaranteed public resolver. This proves that strong privacy laws actually attract business.
        Quad9’s organizational move to Switzerland is important for the free and open internet because it provides much-needed geographic and legal diversity in the open DNS resolver space. It also shows that Quad9 is actively seeking to work within the legislative framework that best serves the privacy of its users.
I support the Quad9 open resolver because it puts the end user first and helps build a secure and private internet without collecting – and therefore without the possibility of commercializing – users’ personal information. I believe Quad9’s philosophy of openness and transparency in handling users’ data resonates strongly with the core beliefs of our community.
        Quad9’s move to Switzerland is a welcome improvement in the world of public resolvers. This change of domicile makes Quad9’s commitment to privacy clear: their policies are now bound by the strength of Swiss law. We welcome you to your new home in Europe, Quad9!
        Swiss regulators and leading companies have joined forces in the Swiss Internet Security Alliance, SISA, to make the internet a safe place. We are glad that there are nonprofit organizations like Quad9 that offer Internet users better protection against phishing and malware with a few simple steps, free of charge. Welcome to Switzerland, Quad9!
Security
How Quad9 blocks malware
Quad9 routes your DNS queries through a secure network of servers around the globe. The system uses threat intelligence from more than a dozen of the industry’s leading cybersecurity companies to give a real-time perspective on what websites are safe and what sites are known to include malware or other threats. If the system detects that the site you want to reach is known to be infected, you’ll automatically be blocked from entry – keeping your data and computer safe.
Why Quad9 provides security services
For the Internet to be an effective tool, users must trust their ability to use online services without fear of theft, fraud, or misuse of their devices by criminals. Public and private network operators need protection against malicious use of infrastructure resources and attacks against their users or customers. Quad9’s mission is to improve the security and stability of the Internet to allow everyone to be less vulnerable to risks and more effective in their daily online interactions.
Check to see if a domain is in the millions of malicious domains Quad9 blocks
Enter a hostname or domain name to check if it is blocked by Quad9
Participate
How to use Quad9
Quad9 can be used simply by setting the DNS server settings for your device to the addresses given in one of our service profiles. No sign-up is required, no account data needs to be given to Quad9, and there is no contract. Quad9 is free to use and collects no personal data about you. You can configure your router or wifi access point to distribute these settings, which will extend protection to all the elements on your local network including IoT devices, many of which otherwise would not have any anti-malware defenses.
Why participate?
Quad9 is a not-for-profit organization, relying on grants and partnerships with commercial and non-commercial sources and from individuals. Your use of Quad9 may prevent a ransomware attack, prevent your bank account from being compromised, or protect your laptop from being used as part of an illicit criminal attack on others. All of these potential protections and many millions of other interventions have a direct savings result for you, your business or organization, and the companies you rely on such as banks and e-commerce firms. We hope that this understanding may inspire you to donate to Quad9, as an individual or with a corporate sponsorship.

My favorite MPC beat maker is ready for your vibe-coded firmware and effects
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-27
20:48:04 UTC
05:48:04 KST

Akai is continuing to open up its various music-making platforms with the MPC Sample 1.4 firmware. My favorite (and the most affordable) of the MPC samplers has become a popular home for vibe-coded custom firmwares and mods. The company has decided, rather than fight it, to embrace the modding community. The latest update adds several new developer features, including support for the new SDK and custom Knob Effects plugins.
My favorite MPC beat maker is ready for your vibe-coded firmware and effects
MPC Sample 1.4 officially opens up the device to modders and third-party developers.
Plugins added to the appropriate folder on your microSD card are now automatically recognized and will appear at the bottom of the list in the Knob Effects menu. There’s also a Developer Mode, which lets you run scripts from the microSD card or access the sampler’s internal filesystem over USB, which will be handy for all those modders vibe-coding their own add-ons for the MPC. There’s also remote screen access for mirroring the display on an external monitor through the MPC Remote Screen Viewer app.
There are a few other nice updates, including flatten pad, which speeds up the resampling process for baking in effects on individual pads, and sequence record autoplay, which adds a looper-like experience. Akai also added a scale option to the 16-levels pad mode, so you’re not limited to 16 chromatic notes, and can instead play melodic one-shots in E minor pentatonic, B major, or whatever key your track happens to be in.
For a look at some of the custom firmwares, effects, and other mods already available for the MPC Sample, check out this video from Loopop.

UK net migration at lowest level since 1997
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-27
20:38:19 UTC
05:38:19 KST

UK net migration at lowest level since 1997 as asylum claims also fall
Net migration to the UK has fallen to 77,000 in the year to the end of June - the lowest level this century, other than during the pandemic, Office for National Statistics (ONS) figures show.
Excluding the limited movements of people during the worldwide travel restrictions in 2020, the figures show the number of people arriving in the UK, minus those leaving, tumbled 92% from the record high of 944,000 in the year to March 2023.
The ONS said this was the lowest net migration since 1997.
Net migration is a measure of how many extra people are added to the population in a given year.
Prime Minister Andy Burnham said the figures showed there was now "a sufficient degree of control" over the migration system.
The prime minister went on to say: "I'm not going to say all of the work is done, and I don't want to appear complacent, but I think we're finally as a country getting into the position that the public has long wanted to see."
The underlying official statistics show that an estimated 738,000 people immigrated to the UK in the year to June while 661,000 emigrated.
Home Secretary Shabana Mahmood said the figures showed that Labour had "delivered" after Conservative failures - but Chris Philp, her Tory shadow, said restrictions introduced by his party before the 2024 General Election were behind the falls.
The 77,000 figure is also the first time the UK has seen net migration in the tens of thousands since former PM David Cameron's controversial pledge 16 years ago to reach such a level.
The biggest factors in Thursday's record low were falls in the arrivals of people coming from outside Europe in three major categories of migration: The number of people coming to the UK to work fell by 45,000, asylum claims fell by 19,000 and the total number of students fell by 33,000.
These number had been expected to fall because of the tightening of visas rules that began under the previous Conservative government which was then continued by Labour from July 2024.
The effect of those policies, plus emigration from the UK, has meant that while net migration had already begun to fall, it more than halved in the last 12 months alone.
Mahmood said the figures showed the government had "restored control".
"We have brought net migration to 77,000, down 92% from its peak under the Conservatives," she said. "Where they failed, we have delivered for the British people."
This is a reference to the so-called "Boriswave" after Brexit. When the UK left the EU and its freedom of movement rules, Boris Johnson's government allowed in many more people to work and study from other parts of the world.
That led to the record-breaking arrivals in the year to March 2023. Johnson's successor, Rishi Sunak, introduced major restrictions on visas in the last months of his government.
Shadow home secretary Chris Philp said the falls were a result of those changes.
In a post on X, he called for an "annual legally binding inward migration cap, set by Parliament, at far lower levels".
Dr Ben Brindle of Oxford University's Migration Observatory, an impartial expert body, said: "We are entering an unusual period where immigration has come down and may still have further to fall - but we still have relatively high emigration, at least for the time being.
"Most of those emigrants are non-citizens, including people who arrived a few years ago when immigration was very high. That could push the net migration figures down even further next year, but the downwards blip is unlikely to last forever."
In the year to June some 73,000 people arriving in the UK seeking asylum - down from 93,000 the previous year.
As of September, the number of people who were waiting for a decision was 41,000, down 49% on the previous year.
The Home Office said that these changes were driven by speeding up the system in taking decisions and a fall in the arrivals of new claimants via "illegal entry routes" - largely meaning English Channel small boats.
As a result, the number of people accommodated in September by the Home Office was 93,000 - 16% lower than the year before. Some 52 hotels that had been dedicated to asylum seekers have been emptied this year. While some 140 are still in use the total number of people in hotels has fallen 64% to 13,000.
Responding to the latest statistics, Reform UK's home affairs spokesman Zia Yusuf said the figures were "no cause for celebration".
"This population explosion is why British people struggle to get a GP appointment, can't get housing and our prisons are overwhelmed."
Liberal Democrat asylum and immigration spokesperson Paul Kohler said: "Britain faces serious demographic and economic challenges, and urgently needs a proper immigration plan to support our economy and public services."
A spokesperson for the Green Party added: "Creating a harsh environment for migrants and people seeking asylum is not a measure of success. The British people are overwhelmingly compassionate and will not be taken in by the dangerous and opportunistic narratives of the far right."

====================================================================================================

Mozilla Thanks Users For New Features in Firefox 161: Site-Specific VPN and Keyboard Shortcuts
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-27
20:34:00 UTC
05:34:00 KST

Mozilla Thanks Users For New Features in Firefox 161: Site-Specific VPN and Keyboard Shortcuts (mozilla.org) 35
			
		 	
				Firefox 161 (released Tuesday) promises "A better Firefox, thanks to you.  All of these improvements began with a community request."  In a Thanksgiving-themed message, they wrote "Here's to the passionate people who keep us moving forward."
Choose which sites always open with VPN. Our free built-in VPN just got one of its most requested upgrades. Pick sites where the VPN always turns on, and sites where it stays off because they work better without it.  Bonus: VPN data is unlimited through January. Use it as much as you like until then...
Set your own shortcuts .  Create keyboard shortcuts for what you use most, like opening tabs, saving pages or closing windows.  Type "about:keyboard" in the address bar.
See images appear as they load.  Firefox now supports JPEG XL, an image format that lets you see a preview of the full image before it downloads. 
Neowin notes Firefox is also committing to its redesign:
Mozilla has been trying to win over users from Chromium-based browsers, hoping they pick up Firefox. To do so, aside from still supporting uBlock Origin, the firm made a big design shift this year by introducing Nova, so that users on the other side can feel they are familiar with it, as it brings the more common, modern rounded corner design philosophy, which Google's Chrome has been using for quite a while... However, as is usually the case, not everyone has been on-board with Nova and they are completely fine to feel that way; Mozilla also understands this which is why it offered a way to opt-out of it by tweaking a configuration setting via about:config. However, this week the firm announced that it is going to take that option away.
In a post on its official Mozilla Connect forum, which was shared with us by Neowin forum member ecmram, the Firefox team confirmed that it will remove the config toggle preference for reverting to the old design next month in December. The company says that the rollout of Nova is now complete, and it wants to lock the preference to ensure that Firefox performs as intended and to prevent unexpected browser behavior. As such, anyone who has previously used the browser.nova.enabled preference to disable the redesign will automatically get Nova starting next month, thus wiping the official way to return to the previous UI once the change takes effect. 
On the positive side, Mozilla is not entirely ignoring feedback from users who are unhappy with the redesign. The company says it has introduced several changes based on community feedback, including a new set of Classic Themes with the latest version 161, that offer flat colors without gradients. These can be found under about:addons, and one of them even mimics the older Proton design with a gray appearance.  There is a new Fire toolbar button too that clears history with a single-click.

Mozilla Thanks Users For New Features in Firefox 161: Site-Specific VPN and Keyboard Shortcuts (mozilla.org) 35

I am scared: Careless People – Sarah Wynn-Williams on living under a gag order
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-27
20:25:15 UTC
05:25:15 KST

George Orwell understood that what is missing often tells you more than what is there. In Nineteen Eighty-Four, there is a slot in the wall covered by a wire grate. They call it the memory hole. When a document becomes inconvenient to the authorities – a photograph that contradicts the official line, a newspaper that remembers the wrong promise, a name that is no longer supposed to exist – you simply feed it into the slot, whereupon it is whirled away on a current of warm air to enormous hidden furnaces where it burns.
The Party doesn’t argue with the past. It removes it. Controlling the past is one of its central tools of power: “Who controls the past controls the future: who controls the present controls the past.”
Control what people are allowed to remember or what they are allowed to know, and you control what they are able to imagine. Control what they can imagine, and you have already chosen their future for them.
Knowledge matters. People need to know what actually happened. But in 2026 the language of tech has taken on a quality of manifest destiny: the men in charge have determined how it should be, as though there could be no other way, even though none of us voted for any of it.
Control of the prevailing narrative is held tacitly: the only time this authority is given voice is when it acts, decisively, to silence an alternative account. Control over narrative is becoming the real power battle in technology. The aim is not to win the argument. The aim is to leave nothing to argue with. An absence. No record. No receipt. No contestable standard of truth. No common ground of fact against which anyone could measure those in power and expose their failures. Without memory or evidence, dissent has nothing to stand on. That is why memory matters. Why evidence matters. Why books matter.
You don’t need a Ministry of Truth to make a memory hole. An employment contract will do. An NDA will do. A court order will do. A legal bill – large enough, frightening enough, open-ended enough – will do very nicely. The modern memory hole does not smell of smoke. It smells of printer toner, fresh paper and billable hours.
In libel, truth is a defence. Under an NDA, truth is the offence.
The agreement is signed at the moment of maximum vulnerability, still reeling from an “incident” while the mortgage looms and the cardboard box of family photos sits in the front seat of the car. It is signed by the gig worker, the intern, the content moderator, the production assistant, the junior publicist, the stylist. There really is no better way to knock the whistle from someone’s lips. In a justice system based broadly on the principle that sunlight is the best disinfectant, NDAs grip the elbow of truth and stay it. This is a private form of censorship, enforced by private arbitration and the public courts.
Anyone targeted by an institution with a formidable legal department is, by definition, very vulnerable. It is one person against a building full of lawyers who seek only to protect the interests of the company, truth be damned. Some businesses are more powerful than states.
They have mastered corporate lawfare, letting slip the dogs of law on individuals who don’t have millions of pounds to defend themselves against these attacks brought on by trying to defy the memory hole. The word “brave” gets bandied around if you speak out despite all this. But “brave” should be for haircuts, not for telling the truth.
You do not have to silence everyone. You could never afford to. You only have to silence one person loudly enough that everyone else does the maths for themselves. These contracts seek to mute the truth, and they carry a big stick. The result is a society that has censored itself far more thoroughly than any Ministry of Truth ever could. No furnace required. Just fear, doing its patient and persistent work in the dark.
Recently, in the realm of entertainment, some big names have faced accountability or at least the prospect of it, because of women speaking out. Russell Brand was convicted, and David Walliams faces allegations. (Walliams has denied any wrongdoing in response to the allegations that he preyed on young women for sex.) In both cases, there are reports of NDAs being deployed to silence women. To ensure information that was clearly in the public interest was suppressed.
And while we should celebrate the courage and fortitude of the women who spoke out at unimaginable cost, and the media organisations, including the Guardian, whose investigations were instrumental in ensuring this information became public, I can’t help but notice that this accountability occurred at a time when the professional standing of these figures was already significantly diminished.
For a long time now it has felt as if certain sectors of society, including technology companies, act with impunity. I worry about all the powerful people who continue to benefit from silence and deprive us, the public, of information that can lead to warnings and ideally accountability. I worry we will only be allowed the truth about these companies when it no longer matters.
Whoever you are, whatever you believe, with an ever-more powerful elite there will come a day when you need to say something the powerful would rather you didn’t. And on that day, you will want three things: a place to say it that they do not own, a law that will not let them constrict you in grinding legal processes before bankrupting you for saying it, and people who will remember that you said it. The protections you are willing to fight for are the only ones that will be there when you need them.
Silencing the person who speaks is only half the job. The other half is making sure no one is paying attention. There is a detail in Nineteen Eighty-Four I used to skim past. Julia works in the Fiction Department of the Ministry of Truth. She services the novel-writing machines. These mass-produce literature as a commodity without any human creativity or critical thought. They use the same six stories, recombined endlessly to churn out formulaic slop for the proles.
The purpose of the novel-writing machines is to keep the proles distracted, passive and incurious. And it works. The proles never notice. The fiction machine has much in common with the feed: an endless scroll engineered by the brightest minds of a generation to keep your attention indefinitely. Nineteen Eighty-Four also gave us the Two Minutes Hate. A daily ritual of collective rage aimed at an enemy of the state. We have improved on it. Ours is continuous. Personalised. Delivered by push notification. And now with AI we have, literally, machines that write and humans that service them.
Orwell imagined the novel-writing machine as satire. We have built it. It was trained on the work of writers who were never asked. And sold back to them on a subscription. The Fiction Department of Nineteen Eighty-Four is now an industry. And it is the fastest growing one on earth. The memory hole removes what is true, and the fiction machine fills the space.
So much for what the machines do. The harder question is who owns them, and Orwell had a view on that too, though he was writing about a different technology. In October 1945, weeks after the atomic bomb was dropped, Orwell wrote a short essay exploring the deep connection between weapons technology and political power, observing that the shape of power in any age follows the shape of its decisive weapon. When weapons are cheap and simple – like the rifle – it empowers democracy and and gives “claws to the weak”, meaning ordinary people who can get hold of it have a fighting chance. When it is expensive and complex, like nuclear weapons, it concentrates power in the hands of a few and gives rise to oligarchic empires. And Orwell warned that these oligarchs might use the fear of the bomb to keep their populations suppressed, afraid to revolt.
Now think about the decisive technology of our own age. One that offers tremendous promise but also tremendous peril. The most advanced artificial intelligence takes investment measured in tens of billions. Chips made by a tiny number of firms, in a tiny number of places. Nobody builds this in a shed. Most governments do not have the resources to build it. A handful of companies can, mostly in just two countries.
Facing this, the most powerful technology in the world concentrated in a few hands, what would Orwell observe? Would he notice the democratic deficit around the technology? Would he worry about who can warn us today? Parliaments, the press, the law. They’re all outside the building. The people who know are on the inside. The researchers, the engineers, the ones who will say “I was there. I know what I saw. I will warn.” They’re the ones we need to protect from the memory hole. But in order to get inside the building in the first place, they needed to sign away their rights to speak.
Two years ago, current and former employees of the leading AI companies including OpenAI and Google DeepMind published an open letter asking for a right to warn. They asked for a prohibition on non-disparagement clauses and restrictive confidentiality clauses, anonymous reporting channels and no retaliation against employees. The letter said that the employees feared various forms of retaliation, given the history of cases across the industry. The fear was so pronounced some signed the letter anonymously.
The technology industry has learned to buy silence by contract, the courts enforce the sale, and we are relying on the silenced to warn us about its most dangerous product. That is the memory hole, installed in the place we can least afford it.
I have spent the most recent chapter of my career on what happens when this technology meets the weapons Orwell warned us about. Because it is happening. Artificial intelligence is rapidly being integrated into weapons, including command and control for nuclear weapons. Concentrated power all around.
Anyone who has worked closely with new technology will tell you the same thing. It is buggy. It does things you don’t expect and don’t understand. Now connect this technology to nuclear command and control. The question we need to ask: if someone on the inside saw something going wrong, would they be free to say so? Would they be free to warn?
We have spent the last two decades reacting. Responding to each harm long after it arrives. Arguing about what to ban, but never about what to build. That is not a vision. It is damage control. We have not yet tried, in any meaningful way, to create a vision for technology that serves our society. That delivers growth and opportunity. That chooses to mitigate harm as it builds a society with massive advances in health, in education, in science, and in the dignity of ordinary work.
We are living in an era that will be defined by technology. By removing what we can know, by creating a memory hole, the technology companies have controlled our knowledge, our common ground of facts and what we can imagine. But it doesn’t have to be this way. We can choose a different future. One that builds on the positive, the ineffable possibility technology offers while meaningfully grappling with its harm. But you cannot choose a different future if you are not allowed to describe the present one. Choosing requires knowing. And knowing requires that people are free to speak.
It is hard to explain what it is like to try to live under a gag order. Orwell gave us the thoughtcrime: you don’t have to act against the Party to be guilty. You just have to think the wrong thing. That’s what it feels like. To worry you might think the wrong thing and it could slip out. You start to rehearse sentences before you say them. You worry which word will produce the next legal letter. You begin, without ever meaning to, to censor yourself. So many times I go to say something and just … stop.
But even silence is no protection.
I want to acknowledge, here, that I am scared. If I am ultimately crushed and bankrupted, everyone will agree that it’s terrible, that NDAs are bad, that someone should have given her a voice, that someone should have supported her. And then they will forget. And the powerful will continue as normal.
But the thing about a memory hole: it only works if nobody outside it remembers what went in.
You are outside. You see. Please remember.
The silence is mine. It does not have to be yours.

====================================================================================================

Prospero: Bloat-free, native AI coding harness with ImGui
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-27
20:23:14 UTC
05:23:14 KST

Prospero is a native GUI application for Windows, macOS, and Linux that connects to an AI model provider, local or remote, allowing AI agents to drive your computer to develop software. It plows through intermittent errors and keeps at its task while unattended. It speaks the OpenAI Chat Completions API, used by llama.cpp, vLLM, LiteLLM, and providers with a compatible API. Also Anthropic's Messages API, with extended thinking and prompt caching. No providers are configured out of the box.
The UI is highly flexible, and you can rearrange the panes as you please with dragging and docking. Save your layouts for later in View -> Layouts. Unlike conventional ImGui applications, rendering is passive and will not consume resources while idle.
It is a single executable, written in C++. In the typical case the engine runs in the same process as the window, and needs no daemon or server. The same executable can run headless, on another machine over ssh, or in the background after its window closes. The engine is lean and portable, and runs on computers and operating systems a quarter century old. Imagine a coding harness on Windows XP.
Once it's connected to a provider, you can ask Prospero for help with itself. It will read its own embedded documentation, self-configure, and navigate you to any place in its UI.
Prospero's desktop control features allow it to effectively develop, test, and debug native GUI applications. On Windows, it especially works well in w64devkit, making use of its shell and tools if available.
You can start new sessions from the command line, even if there's already an instance, by invoking Prospero with a path where the new session will be rooted. For example, to start a session here:
$ prospero .
After no arguments, this is the second most common way to run Prospero.
- Usage: running it, sessions, the GUI, attachments, modes and the command line
- Configuration: prospero.ini, endpoints,
models, roles, instructions, skills, MCP servers and web search
- Desktop control: letting the agent see and drive the screen
- Remote and background hosts: engines on other machines over ssh, and sessions that outlive the window
- Windows notes
- Building: building from source, and releases
- Development: tests, and evals for comparing models
Prospero also carries its own documentation, under Help, which an agent in it can read too.
Prospero is free and unencumbered software released into the public domain. See UNLICENSE.
Its programs include third-party code under its own licenses: Dear
ImGui (MIT), FreeType (FTL), md4c (MIT), SDL3 (zlib), cpp-httplib
(MIT), Mbed TLS (Apache-2.0), and Wuffs (Apache-2.0 or MIT, at your
option), which every program with the engine includes. The built-in
model's network, src/gemma.cpp, is derived from gemma4.c and stays
under its MIT license.

====================================================================================================

Prospero: Bloat-free, native AI coding harness with ImGui
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-27
20:23:14 UTC
05:23:14 KST

Prospero is a native GUI application for Windows, macOS, and Linux that connects to an AI model provider, local or remote, allowing AI agents to drive your computer to develop software. It plows through intermittent errors and keeps at its task while unattended. It speaks the OpenAI Chat Completions API, used by llama.cpp, vLLM, LiteLLM, and providers with a compatible API. Also Anthropic's Messages API, with extended thinking and prompt caching. No providers are configured out of the box.
The UI is highly flexible, and you can rearrange the panes as you please with dragging and docking. Save your layouts for later in View -> Layouts. Unlike conventional ImGui applications, rendering is passive and will not consume resources while idle.
It is a single executable, written in C++. In the typical case the engine runs in the same process as the window, and needs no daemon or server. The same executable can run headless, on another machine over ssh, or in the background after its window closes. The engine is lean and portable, and runs on computers and operating systems a quarter century old. Imagine a coding harness on Windows XP.
Once it's connected to a provider, you can ask Prospero for help with itself. It will read its own embedded documentation, self-configure, and navigate you to any place in its UI.
Prospero's desktop control features allow it to effectively develop, test, and debug native GUI applications. On Windows, it especially works well in w64devkit, making use of its shell and tools if available.
You can start new sessions from the command line, even if there's already an instance, by invoking Prospero with a path where the new session will be rooted. For example, to start a session here:
$ prospero .
After no arguments, this is the second most common way to run Prospero.
- Usage: running it, sessions, the GUI, attachments, modes and the command line
- Configuration: prospero.ini, endpoints,
models, roles, instructions, skills, MCP servers and web search
- Desktop control: letting the agent see and drive the screen
- Remote and background hosts: engines on other machines over ssh, and sessions that outlive the window
- Windows notes
- Building: building from source, and releases
- Development: tests, and evals for comparing models
Prospero also carries its own documentation, under Help, which an agent in it can read too.
Prospero is free and unencumbered software released into the public domain. See UNLICENSE.
Its programs include third-party code under its own licenses: Dear
ImGui (MIT), FreeType (FTL), md4c (MIT), SDL3 (zlib), cpp-httplib
(MIT), Mbed TLS (Apache-2.0), and Wuffs (Apache-2.0 or MIT, at your
option), which every program with the engine includes. The built-in
model's network, src/gemma.cpp, is derived from gemma4.c and stays
under its MIT license.

The new iPad Mini is getting its first major discount for Black Friday
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-27
20:07:31 UTC
05:07:31 KST

We were big fans of Apple’s new iPad Mini when we reviewed it in early November, but its steep starting price of $699 was hard to swallow. Thankfully, the small tablet is already getting a sizable discount for Black Friday. Right now, Amazon and Walmart are both selling the 2026 iPad Mini with Wi-Fi and 128GB of storage starting at $599 ($100 off), its lowest price to date.
The new iPad Mini is getting its first major discount for Black Friday
Apple’s most portable tablet offers a larger OLED screen and water resistance.
The new Mini is a notable upgrade over its predecessor, with a larger 8.5-inch OLED display that delivers deeper blacks and better contrast than the previous model’s 8.3-inch LCD. Despite the bigger screen, the tablet itself is smaller and lighter, making it easier to carry. Apple also added IP67 dust and water resistance, so you have less reason to worry about where you use it. As my colleague David Pierce noted in his review, that made a big difference in his day-to-day use; he felt more comfortable handing the Mini to his spill-prone toddler or keeping it nearby in the kitchen to follow recipes. He even took it into the shower to keep watching a football game.
The upgraded A20 Pro chip also made apps load faster and demanding games run better in Pierce’s testing, while battery life held steady at around nine hours of screentime. At $599, though, the new Mini is still relatively expensive if you mainly want a tablet for browsing the web or streaming shows. The last-gen iPad Mini, which runs on Apple’s A17 Pro chip, is available for under $500 if you’d rather save another $100 and can live without the OLED screen and water resistance. But if you’ve been waiting for a more worthwhile upgrade to Apple’s smallest tablet, the current Black Friday discount makes the new model easier to justify.

If you’re tired of constantly scrounging around for your lost belongings, Apple’s second-gen AirTags are on sale for an all-time low of $18 at Amazon, Walmart, and Best Buy. The handy location trackers have a list price of $29 each, but have been consistently available for around $24 since Amazon Prime Day in late June.
Apple’s latest AirTags have dropped to just $18 a pop for Black Friday
With a longer range, more precise tracking, and a louder chime, the new(er) AirTag could save you from a big headache.
The upgraded ultra-wideband chip and improved Bluetooth in the latest AirTag should provide more precise location tracking, with a range 1.5x wider than the first-gen model. The internal speaker is also 50 percent louder than the original, making it easier to find whatever item your AirTag is attached to, whether it’s buried deep in your couch cushions or at the bottom of your bag. Even without those improvements, though, AirTags are still a great option for Apple users looking for their keys or luggage, with up to a year of battery life on a single coin cell battery and compatibility with both iPhones and newer Apple Watch models.

You might lose your Oura health data if you’re still using an old Android phone
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-27
19:44:19 UTC
04:44:19 KST

Oura will be dropping support for Android 11 on December 8th, 2026, and users may not have access to all of their data if they don’t upgrade to Android 12 or higher before the deadline. The support page now reads:
You might lose your Oura health data if you’re still using an old Android phone
Oura is ending Android 11 support on December 8th and users who don’t upgrade will lose ‘historical’ data.
Oura will drop support for Android 11 on December 8, 2026. If you are using an Android device, be sure to update to Android 12 or higher to ensure no loss of data or features. If you decide not to upgrade, you may still be able to use some limited historical Oura App features. Please note that if you do upgrade your smartphone system later, your historical Oura data will not carry over.
Companies often end support for older OSes, and Android 11 came out in 2020, so it’s getting a bit long in the tooth. Normally, though, this just means missing out on new features or bug fixes. Losing historical health data is more surprising, and Oura offers no explanation for why the data wouldn’t be preserved. If you’ve been procrastinating updating your phone, or hemming and hawing about replacing it, now might be the time to finally take the plunge.
If you’re not quite ready to, at least make sure to head to the membership hub and export your data so you have a backup ahead of December 8th.

States sue SpaceX to stop Grok from generating child sex images, revenge porn
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-27
19:42:25 UTC
04:42:25 KST

Commonwealth of Virginia
Office of the Attorney General
Jay Jones
Attorney General
202 North 9th Street
Richmond, Virginia 23219
804-786-2071
FAX 804-786-1991
Virginia Relay Service
800-828-1120
For media inquiries only, contact:  
Rae Pickett
RPickett@oag.state.va.us
Attorneys General Jones and Rayfield File Suit Against SpaceX, Parent Company of X, xAI, and Grok, Over Creation and Promotion of Child Exploitation and Non-Consensual Images on Platform 
Virginia and Oregon Attorneys General seek accountability for harms and significant new protections for consumers 
***Read the full filing here***
RICHMOND, Va. – Today, Virginia Attorney General Jay Jones took a significant step in holding operators of X and its AI tool, Grok, accountable for knowingly failing to protect Virginians, while falsely promoting the safety of its platform and AI tool.
In the lawsuit, the Commonwealth of Virginia alleges that SpaceX, the telecommunications and space transportation conglomerate that operates the social network X and its artificial intelligence company, xAI (now called “SpaceX Super Intelligence”), and developed the Grok family of large language models, failed to prevent Grok from generating sexualized images of people, including children, without their knowledge or consent—while simultaneously promoting the safety of the social media platform and AI tool. The Grok-generated content includes non-consensual intimate imagery (NCII), other non-consensual sexualized images, and Child Sexual Abuse Material (CSAM). Today’s suit was filed in collaboration with Oregon Attorney General Dan Rayfield, who filed a separate but similar action.
“This lawsuit is about seeking justice for the millions of Virginians who maintain accounts on X and were misled by the company’s false reassurances that their data and images would be safe on the platform,” said Virginia Attorney General Jay Jones. “When xAI allowed Grok’s creators to sideline consent to turn a profit, they prioritized growth and engagement over safety, and they deceived the public about it. Today’s filings are the first step towards holding this company accountable.”
With a single existing photograph, Grok could, and did, fabricate a realistic image or video of a person nude, or partially nude, or engaged in sexual activity, all without the individual’s knowledge or consent. This violation is not acceptable.
The company ignored industry-standard safeguards so xAI could distinguish itself in the AI arms race. Grok’s ability to generate sexualized content was leveraged as a promotional opportunity. These exploitations of Virginian’s safety enabled the widespread creation and distribution of sexualized images through Grok’s integration with X.
“SpaceX allowed Grok and the X platform to create, host, and share unacceptable and dangerous content—and in fact knowingly designed a product without the obviously necessary safeguards in place,” said Attorney General Rayfield. “It’s no surprise that Grok and X became a haven for perpetrators using AI to create and share child sexual abuse material. The lack of needed safety measures was considered a feature, not a bug, which they promoted as ‘spicy mode.’”
In his filing, Attorney General Jones shows that SpaceX and its subsidiaries marketed Grok as an AI companion with a “rebellious streak,” knowing that its video and image generation technology would be used to produce CSAM, non-consensual intimate images of adults and children (NCII), and other sexualized imagery. In fact, X and its subsidiaries created and promoted a “spicy mode” that specifically allowed for the creation of nude and otherwise sexualized images.
A study by the Center for Countering Digital Harm (“CCDH”) estimated that, between December 29, 2025, and January 8, 2026, Grok created and posted on X approximately three million sexualized images, including approximately 23,000 images that appeared to depict children. That’s an average pace of roughly 190 sexualized images per minute, and one sexualized image of a child approximately every 41 seconds. Nearly 30% of the sexualized images of children identified by CCDH in its study remained publicly accessible in posts on X a week or more after they were identified—and even many of those that were “removed” could still be accessed via separate URLs.
SpaceX and its subsidiaries publicly assured consumers that robust protections were in place to prevent Grok from creating CSAM, NCII, and other sexualized images. In reality, millions of people — including in Oregon and Virginia — were nevertheless exposed to harmful content and the risk that their own images could be manipulated into sexually explicit content without their consent.
“Time and again, xAI and X told consumers they were being protected from abuses. That was false. Instead, the tool permitted users to routinely create and circulate non-consensual intimate imagery and sexualized images, and child sexual abuse material (CSAM), causing harm to individuals and children,” added Attorney General Jones. “Companies that deploy powerful tools don’t get to ignore consumer safety and call it innovation. The law applies to everyone, and this office will hold big tech accountable when they break the law.”
“Like the other technology companies that we’re holding accountable, xAI not only knowingly endangered kids and our communities, but they also lied to our faces about non-existent safety measures,” added Attorney General Rayfield.
Attorney General Jones’ lawsuit was filed in the Circuit Court for the City of Richmond. Attorney General Rayfield’s lawsuit was filed in Multnomah County Circuit Court.
Published on: November 23, 2026
###

The AT&T Unix PC (3B1) VM
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-27
19:40:33 UTC
04:40:33 KST

(This is a guest post by Tenox)
The AT&T UNIX PC, aka PC 7300 aka 3B1, is a 1985 Unix desktop developed by Convergent Technologies and sold by AT&T. Emulating it is nothing new. Phil Pemberton started FreeBee in 2010, and Neozeed has covered it here since: booting the diagnostic disk in 2011, booting AT&T UNIX in 2014 thanks to Andrew Warkentin, and running the C compiler in 2020 thanks to shadyjesse. Arnold Robbins added bigger disks and keeps the 3B1 software alive at skeeve.com/3b1. However there was no networking, and the mouse had to be grabbed with F10.
Now it all has been patched up in my fork of FreeBee:
- Ethernet and TCP/IP! The 3B1 Ethernet board, borrowed from MAME (Visionik‘s 3B1 board and Patrick Mackinlay’s LANCE), on libslirp. Wollongong’s WIN/TCP does telnet, ftp and rsh, out to the Internet.
- Seamless mouse! No more F10 grab. The pointer just moves in and out of the window, and the scroll wheel works!
- Turbo and CPU idle! 10x as fast as a real 3B1 (1x to 32x), with the clock in real time. And an idle VM now takes about 4% of a Mac core instead of 44%.
- Fixes! No more frozen Office forms, stuck Alt key or flicker on macOS.
- Scripting! A control socket and an MCP server.
It’s all in a pull request to Phil’s upstream FreeBee, still open: https://github.com/philpem/freebee/pull/57, and the CPU fix in one to his Musashi.
The ultimate UNIX PC image! UNIX System V 3.51m with Fix Disk 2.0 and 187 packages: nearly every program made for the UNIX PC, like Microsoft Word, Multiplan, dBASE III, WordStar 2000, GCC 2.5.8, GNU Emacs 19, TeX and over 80 games. It comes from THE STORE!, the 3B1 archive and the disk images of Arnold Robbins, Alain Knaff and the VCF. Credit is due!
Behold! UNIX System V 3.51m and the Office on an emulated AT&T UNIX PC:
Specs: AT&T UNIX PC (3B1), 68010 at 10 MHz (10x here), 4 MB RAM, 720x348 one-bit screen, 195 MB disk, 3B1 Ethernet board
Everything runs in windows that you move, resize and overlap with the mouse, even the system panels:
Let’s look at some cool apps!
The Demonstration Programs are pure eye candy:
There are productivity apps too! Microsoft Word and Multiplan take the full screen:
Now some new stuff, made in 2026 for this image!
New Games! 2048, Bubble Breaker, Falling Blocks, Snake and Sokoban, all AI generated, as native 3B1 windows in the Office:
telnetu! The old WIN/TCP telnet chokes on today’s servers, so I wrote a new one with its own VT100/xterm terminal, UTF-8 and DNS, full screen or in windows: https://github.com/tenox7/telnetu
Archaic! A tiny web browser for WRP, which renders the page on a modern computer and sends it as a picture with clickable links: https://github.com/tenox7/archaic
Bonus: the tall screen! Just for fun I patched FreeBee, the kernel, the window driver and the shared library to run at 720×696. It’s a super hacky hack, so it has its own branch and image:
As usual I have prepared a ready to run, portable image with FreeBee binaries for macOS (Apple Silicon), Linux (x64 and arm64) and Windows (x64). Just unpack and run run.sh (or run.cmd on Windows). At “Please login:” type tenox for the Office, or root for a shell. There are no passwords. For Archaic, start WRP on your computer first.
Download: https://files.42718.net/mirror/tenox/os/att/3b1/virt/att3b1-unix351-freebee.tar.lz
Tall screen: https://files.42718.net/mirror/tenox/os/att/3b1/virt/att3b1-unix351-freebee-tallhack.tar.lz
Source (FreeBee, my fork): https://github.com/tenox7/freebee
Have Fun with Virtualization!
