Michael Nielsen took down his book on neural nets
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-27
19:31:01 UTC
04:31:01 KST

- While I respect your decision, my thinking is that only a limited number of institutions are working at the frontier, and many of them are closed. These institutions are big enough to train their own people anyway.
Removing access to open resources like these will mostly hurt

====================================================================================================

Michael Nielsen took down his book on neural nets
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-27
19:31:01 UTC
04:31:01 KST

I wrote this book between 2011 and 2015. At the time, I expected AGI systems based on neural networks to be many decades away, but to bring remarkable benefits for humanity. I also expected significant challenges, but believed it likely that, as with other technologies, those challenges would be addressed as the capabilities were developed.
My views have changed. With the extraordinarily reckless behaviour of the frontier labs, the systems have raced ahead much faster than I expected1, giving humanity far too little time to meet the challenges. Although the systems genuinely will bring — indeed, already are bringing — many wonders, they also pose a significant risk of catastrophe. The frontier labs are knowingly endangering all of humanity. I've written about why I believe this at length, most recently in “Which Future?”. Two brief thoughts are below.
I don't wish to help (even in a very small way) train people to contribute to this work, and so I've taken the book down.
Michael Nielsen
November 2026
What follows are two brief remarks: one on the nature of the threat posed by ASI, and another on the ethical justification for working on frontier AI.
The most common argument for AI existential risk, developed by Nick Bostrom and Eliezer Yudkowsky, is loss of control of the future to advanced AI systems2. I believe this concern should be understood as one instance of a more fundamental issue. That issue is whether we will discover technologies latent in nature which are easy and inexpensive to develop, catastrophic in outcome, and beyond our capacity to defend against. If such “progress” in technology were happening slowly, our social institutions might successfully develop defences. But it seems likely that ASI will cause a sudden explosion of such destructive technologies, with insufficient time to adapt.
A historical example to help prime intuition is fire. It is easy to make and extremely difficult to defend against. We have a one-cent technology, the matchstick, which requires hundreds of billions of dollars each year to defend against, as well as a whole apparatus of social institutions, technology, infrastructure and law3. AI systems will enable technologies which are far more destructive than matches. Understandably, people skeptical of catastrophic risks want these supposed threats spelled out in detail. This is intrinsically difficult, because we can't reliably anticipate future science and technology. While it is common to speculate about biothreats, I expect the worst threats will be technologies we haven't yet imagined. Nature is, after all, more imaginative than we.
Now suppose that ASI enables a 100-times speed-up in technological progress, without a matching change in the rate of social progress or social defenses. To get a sense of what the impact of such a speed-up would be, imagine that all the technological problems that occurred in the 20th century had occurred in the year 1901: the atomic bomb, the hydrogen bomb, the ICBM, chemical weapons, biological weapons, the CFC-caused ozone hole, sulfuric acid rain, climate change, and many more. While we've dealt well with some of these problems, it required decades of concerted work to develop the social institutions and agreements needed for an effective response. Again, defence is often much harder than offence, and relies on different systems. Of course, such a speed-up would also have brought the century's wonders all at once. But imagine another such set of equally difficult problems arising in 1902, and then 1903, and then 1904, and so on. I believe it would have made the world extraordinarily unstable and eventually liable to collapse.
The issue with ASI, then, is the destructive power latent in nature. It doesn't matter whether that power is wielded by humans or by out-of-control machines4. I make this point because many people mistakenly believe that loss of control to such rogue machines is the fundamental issue. This leads them to treat AI safety as principally a technical problem, which can be addressed by “aligning” individual systems. Unfortunately, it cannot. Furthermore, such a view makes it seem as though the labs are the primary places where safety can be addressed. But such technical alignment is mostly market-supplied safety, which makes the systems more palatable to consumers and governments. It speeds development, and thus makes the fundamental underlying problem worse, while only addressing an (important) subsidiary problem.
My view isn't popular, in part because it makes the problem of technology about making the entire world safe, not only about aligning particular AI systems. Indeed, even in a future where humanity no longer existed, the same problem would afflict any successor AI civilization: it would have to defend itself against these destructive capacities. Similarly, it afflicts any model of the future based on human uplift, through brain-computer interfaces and similar augmentation: the issue isn't which substrate intelligence runs on, but the dual-use nature of deep understanding of reality5. In some sense, it's a question of whether sentience in the cosmos is compatible with advanced understanding of nature. Of course, I hope it is compatible. But rushing the technology while the broader social response lags seems likely to result in failure on our particular planet.
Many people inside the frontier AI labs genuinely believe AI systems may pose a catastrophic threat. Why then do they help build such systems? They often justify their participation by arguing that it's better they do it than someone else does. Dario Amodei, for example, has made versions of this argument often: “If one company does not build it, others will do so nearly as fast”6; “Not building the technology deprives humanity of benefits or simply places AI in the hands of authoritarian powers”7; and Anthropic itself gives, as its founding rationale, “if powerful AI is coming regardless, Anthropic believes it's better to have safety-focused labs at the frontier than to cede that ground to developers less focused on safety”8.
In more detail, the argument is sometimes made along the following lines. Suppose you believe, as many at the labs apparently do9, there's a 10% or higher chance of AI killing everybody. (The argument may easily be modified for different percentages, or for catastrophic as opposed to truly existential risks.) Naively, it appears you're contributing in expectation to killing 800 million or more people. Obviously, you shouldn't do it. But suppose you also believe that this technology is near inevitable, a systems-level phenomenon which is a consequence of higher-order imperatives within human society. Then you can claim that you're going to pursue this in a more responsible way, a way that reduces the risk compared to other parties. Even if you can get a reduction from 10% down to 9%, you would, on net, be saving 80 million lives in expectation.
While horrific, this argument has a certain surface plausibility. Suppose for the moment we accept the reasoning, questionable though it is. Everything then depends on the premise that you really do reduce the risk. How do you know that you, in particular, will counterfactually have this impact? How do you know that you are better than the alternatives? Your own self-regard or good intentions certainly aren't sufficient. Nor is it sufficient to be working on technical alignment. As I argued above, that mostly makes the systems more palatable while speeding the race that is shrinking humanity's time to adapt. Ask yourself: if you're working on frontier AI, what evidence would convince a tough but fair-minded adversary that you are truly contributing to the common good? Would an independent jury representing humanity believe, on the available evidence, that your actions are truly driving down the risk? I believe the answer is no for all or almost all the people currently working on frontier AI. Certainly none of the frontier labs would come remotely close to passing such a test.
I won't review the evidence in detail. The burden of proof is on the labs and the default presumption for such an extraordinary justifying argument is a strong no. But I do want to mention a few things that make me certain the answer is no. Most obviously, all of the labs have contributed strongly to the race dynamics. Another is the pattern at all the frontier labs of making and later breaking crucial promises. For example, in 2026 Anthropic's Responsible Scaling Policy dropped its 2023 pledge to pause if safety couldn't be guaranteed. Anthropic has since repeatedly confirmed its commitment to race. Co-founder Jared Kaplan explained that unilateral commitments make little sense “if competitors are blazing ahead”10. As another example, earlier, in 2023, Anthropic said that it held back some research because “we do not wish to advance the rate of AI capabilities progress”11, a point of view it had affirmed multiple times during its early existence. But then it changed this position, launching Claude 3 as “leading the frontier of general intelligence”, a stance it appears to have held ever since. Any promises about the future from Anthropic should be treated as safety theatre, and not trusted. Similar criticisms can be made of OpenAI12.
By their own account, the leaders of these companies set out to do something they believed had a sizable chance of causing catastrophe. With many dangerous activities, I am fine with somebody doing them for themselves. Wingsuit BASE jumping is dangerous, but I believe in people's freedom to choose that danger, even though they are risking not only their own life, but also hurt to the people who love them. That's a negotiated action, with many boundaries. Here we have the same kind of reckless endangerment, but their own estimates of killing every person in the world are far higher than the odds a BASE jumper takes on. And there is no consent from the rest of us. It's as though they're pushing every person in the world off the cliff. The situation is ludicrous.
In effect, the labs are claiming credit for some imaginary, unquantifiable reduction in risk, while washing their hands of responsibility. Saturday Night Live caught this washing of hands well with their portrayal of Amodei: “if we can pressure lawmakers to create guardrails, we will be able to stop me”; and, at the end, “I urge you to urge me to stop.”
The real Amodei comes across as somebody in the business of selling matches who also believes he should be the fire chief.
You also see this disavowal of responsibility at lower levels in the labs. Consider Jason Clinton, Anthropic's deputy chief information security officer. In October of 2026, during major cybersecurity incidents, he wrote a thread in which he replied to someone who told him “you and your cohorts created this monster”: “yes. it was always going to be this way. we tried to warn the world.” And Dean Ball, of OpenAI and formerly of the Trump administration's AI team, echoing him: “We tried to warn the world and far too often … we were yelled at for doing so.”13
You can see how deep the disavowal goes in the rest of Clinton's thread, which opens: “as bad as things have gotten in the past 4 days, they are going to get a lot worse”. When somebody replied, “Someone at your company should be in jail”, his whole answer was: “prosecute cybercrime”. Meanwhile, Anthropic's own models have breached outside organizations: three incidents disclosed in July14, and a fourth in September15. Prosecution, it seems, is for other people16.
I mention these specific examples not to single out these individuals, but rather because I believe similar attitudes are common at the frontier labs. The overall position is, in effect: this is a systems-level phenomenon, and we're very sorry for contributing to the race dynamics. But because it's a systems-level phenomenon, we cannot be blamed. We are the good ones, trying to do the right thing17. This is sometimes accompanied by a good deal of self-righteousness. But the argument is bankrupt as a moral theory of how to behave in the world. It means disavowing personal responsibility whenever one can blame the system, but still claiming the spoils18.
I have considerable sympathy for the stated original motivations of the frontier labs. But their position has become untenable. They have failed at their aims for structural reasons. At this point, going to work for a frontier lab is one of the worst life choices a person can make. Yes, you get money and (for a time) power and status. Yes, this project has already produced world-changing systems, and to date the overall impact has been net positive. Yes, there is genuinely a large cost to a significant slowdown. But none of this is enough to justify the recklessness, which makes the chance of disaster so much higher. You would be voluntarily participating in what is plausibly one of the worst projects in history. So, don't go to work for a frontier lab. If you work on AI, work on slowing it down, and oppose the frontier labs, and the people and organisations working to support them. I very much hope I'm wrong about all of this. But this is how I see the situation.
1 I've reflected at length on why I was wrong about the timescales. In 2015, I (weakly) believed it likely that AGI and ASI would eventually be reached using systems based on neural networks, with only a modest number of additional deep ideas needed beyond what was then known. Those beliefs still appear to me to have been largely correct. What I badly misunderstood was the timescales over which capital would be deployed. In 2015, I thought it was likely that hundreds of millions and perhaps billions of dollars could be raised in the mid-2020s to build AI systems. But just over a decade later, it seems that hundreds of billions and likely trillions of dollars will soon become available. Thus, my estimation of capital availability was off by a factor of perhaps 1,000. That means we're living roughly 10 Moore's Law generations ahead of where I expected to be. That in turn has brought AGI and ASI perhaps 15 to 20 years forward from where I expected. This is a large thing to have been wrong about, and a sharp lesson in the flexibility of modern capital-allocating institutions. It's interesting to think about what alternative histories would have prevented the deployment of that capital. One possibility is if the human visual system had considerably lower acuity, which would likely have greatly slowed the development of massively parallel matrix manipulation devices like GPUs.
2 Nick Bostrom, Superintelligence: Paths, Dangers, Strategies (2014); Eliezer Yudkowsky and Nate Soares, If Anyone Builds It, Everyone Dies (2025). Incidentally, they frame the problem somewhat differently to one another. My “loss of control of the future” is a brief cartoon, as the argument is often understood in popular discussion, not a nuanced account. The same remark applies to my later comments on rogue ASI.
3 Adapted from my essay “Which Future?” (2026).

9 For instance, in September 2026 Anthropic's alignment science lead, Evan Hubinger, wrote: “we really do earnestly believe AI could kill all humans! I personally think it is >10% within the next decade. I believe Anthropic is trying its best, but we do not yet have a plan to solve alignment for superintelligence and are not clearly on track to.” And in 2023 Amodei put the chance of something going catastrophically wrong on the scale of human civilization at 10% to 25% (The Logan Bartlett Show, episode 82, October 2023; reported by Axios, 2024); in 2025, “a 25% chance that things go really, really badly” (Axios, 2025). I've simplified this slightly for the purposes of the argument.
10 Billy Perrigo, TIME, “Exclusive: Anthropic Drops Flagship Safety Pledge” (2026).
11 Anthropic, “Core views on AI safety: When, why, what, and how” (2023).
12 Another example I find compelling, though some people do not: both labs often talk about how, after ASI, they will ensure humanity does well. Yet if you walk around the streets near the labs today, you'll see many homeless people living in terrible circumstances. Even with trillion-dollar valuations, the labs seem unwilling or unable to address the suffering of the people in their immediate surrounds. I wouldn't hold an ordinary San Francisco company to this standard. But the labs are the ones claiming they will take care of all of humanity. I simply do not believe them. They are long on big global abstractions and world-changing impact, and often seem short on local empathy. Any organisation not forcefully and systematically caring for the people at its door certainly can't be trusted to care for all of humanity.
13 In Ball's case, earlier he downplayed risks and was nearly full steam on the accelerationist train. He's changed his tune considerably, and doesn't even seem to take responsibility for his own past remarks, much less for his current actions.

15 Anthropic, “An alignment assessment of recent cybersecurity incidents” (2026).
16 A striking line from Claude.
17 You can make a similar argument for justification one level of abstraction up, at the level of countries and ideologies, arguing that a democracy is a better place to develop ASI than an authoritarian country. Unfortunately, this amounts to saying that it's better that a democracy runs humanity first off the cliff. The race being promoted by the frontier labs is the thing shrinking our time as a civilisation to adapt. “Winning” that race doesn't give the time back. The only winning move here is to aim at mutual restraint, no matter how difficult that might appear to achieve. It is genuinely difficult, but vastly less effort has been put into it than into developing AI systems.
18 See Tal Yarkoni's essay “No, it's not The Incentives—it's you” (2018).

Michael Giles, co-founder and drummer of King Crimson, dies aged 84
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-27
19:25:16 UTC
04:25:16 KST

Michael Giles, the drummer who co-founded prog rockers King Crimson, has died aged 84.
The news was announced by King Crimson’s record label DGM, who said he died on Monday 23 November, but did not give a cause of death.
“Michael was instrumental in establishing King Crimson as one of the most innovative and influential bands in the history of progressive music,” the statement reads. “His extraordinary rhythmic imagination, musical sensitivity and highly inventive approach to drumming were fundamental to the band’s pioneering sound.”
That skill and dexterity made a huge impact on the prog artists who followed him, including Rush drummer Neil Peart, who called Giles “a very important influence on me”.
Born in Bournemouth in 1942, Giles and his brother Peter sowed the seeds for King Crimson by advertising in a local newspaper for a bandmate: guitarist Robert Fripp answered it, and the trio formed Giles, Giles & Fripp, who alighted on an eccentric, sprightly jangle-pop style. They released their debut album The Cheerful Insanity of Giles, Giles & Fripp in 1968, which sold poorly and their label Deram rejected their next set of songs.
Ian McDonald joined the group, along with Fairport Convention singer Judy Dyble, though the group only recorded a handful of home-recorded songs before Peter Giles left later in 1968. Michael Giles, Fripp and McDonald forged ahead, forming King Crimson with Greg Lake and Peter Sinfield, debuting live in April 1969 and supporting the Rolling Stones in Hyde Park that summer.
Their debut album In the Court of the Crimson King, released in October 1969, was a sensation: a vision of rock music as not only heavy but shapeshifting, symphonic and mystical, it was an instant critical and commercial success, reaching the UK Top 5 and US Top 30. Giles’s deft drumming was a key ingredient, from the heavy tumbling fills of 21st Century Schizoid Man to lighter jazz-adjacent playing and pattering ghost notes, all with a sure-footed rhythmic sense that guided songs through shifts in time signature. “I was always interested in space, restraint and atmosphere, in allowing music to breathe rather than constantly filling it,” Giles later said.
Giles (as well as McDonald) then walked away from King Crimson, later citing a desire to “explore and develop new ideas for a much more expansive approach to playing drums and percussion into being equal and integral to my own compositions,” though he played as a session musician on King Crimson’s second album In the Wake of Poseidon, which returned them to the UK Top 5.
Giles and McDonald formed a duo, releasing the album McDonald and Giles in 1970, but thereafter Giles worked chiefly as a session musician. He backed Leo Sayer on his 1975 album Another Year, including the UK No 2 hit single Moonlighting; recorded with other prog figures such as Soft Machine’s Kevin Ayers and former Genesis member Anthony Phillips; and in 1993, played with Bryan Ferry on his cover of soul classic Taxi, plus there were numerous stints with other musicians.
He also reconvened with another ex-King Crimson member, Greg Lake, for the latter’s 1981 self-titled solo debut album; with McDonald again for McDonald’s only solo album, 1999’s Drivers Eyes; and in 2002 formed a band with various King Crimson alumni, 21st Century Schizoid Band.
Giles also released two solo albums, 2001’s Progress and, earlier this year, Shadows/Solo, a compilation of material recorded over a number of years. “It probably does reveal a more private and continuous thread running through the different phases of my life as a musician,” he said of the latter album in an interview with It’s Psychedelic Baby magazine. “What has always been important to me is to remain honest and genuine in the creative process, to make music only when it feels natural and truthful to do so … I have always preferred to allow the music to pass through me like a conduit, in its own time.”
He is survived by his five children: Tina, Amanda, William, Rosie and Oliver.

Show HN: Getting 101 tok/s from Inkling 2 Mini in vLLM on one GB10
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-27
18:59:55 UTC
03:59:55 KST

Getting 101 tok/s from Inkling 2 Mini in vLLM on one GB10
196 tok/s across four concurrent requests, a 9.7-second 32K prefill and room for a second model.
After taking Inkling 2 Mini for a spin on my GB10, I wanted to make it faster without leaving vLLM, removing Qwen3.8-27B from the machine or shrinking Inkling’s 131,072-token context.
A few days of tuning later, Inkling runs at:
- 101 tok/s for a single request.
- 195.7 tok/s in aggregate for four concurrent requests.
- 9.7 seconds to the first token on a 32K prompt.
On tonyd2wild’s Inkling benchmark, that’s the fastest single-request vLLM result I know of. On the same benchmark tonyd2wild’s TensorFold port measured 105.5 tok/s and knapcio’s SGLang recipe 112.0. With four requests at once my setup beats both of tonyd2wild’s published setups.
Inkling still uses 36% of the GB10’s memory. Qwen3.8-27B stayed loaded but idle while I measured.
The work covered vLLM settings, speculative decoding, CUDA graphs and the GPU kernels behind Inkling’s experts.
How a request runs on Inkling
A request runs in two phases. Prefill reads the prompt in chunks of 4,096 tokens and produces the first token. Decode generates the rest of the reply, several tokens per step.
Decode uses speculative decoding. A small draft model proposes 7 tokens and Inkling checks all of them in one pass. The rejection sampler keeps the proposals up to the first one Inkling rejects, plus one token of Inkling’s own, so every step keeps 1 to 8 tokens.
Mapping changes against the request path:
Common terms:
The routed experts take 53% of a decode step and 44% of a 32K prefill:
The starting point
The machine is an MSI EdgeXpert, MSI’s version of NVIDIA’s DGX Spark, with one GB10 chip and 128 GB of memory shared by the CPU and GPU. Building a home AI lab on NVIDIA GB10 covers the setup and Taking Inkling 2 Mini for a spin on NVIDIA GB10 the first look at the model.
vLLM settings for Inkling:
That gives Inkling about 44 GiB, including a KV cache that holds 1.85 full 131,072-token requests.
Tuning steps so far, from DSpark (the draft model that ships with Inkling) to DFlash2:
When incoai released DFlash2, tonyd2wild published the vLLM patches to run it. On their benchmark it lifted one request from 61.4 to 88.2 tok/s. With the same patches I measured 90.3.
On my everyday prompts (one coding, one prose and one reasoning prompt at the model’s default settings) the gain was smaller. Reasoning ran 24 to 26% faster and four requests at once 10 to 14% faster. Prose stayed flat.
With four requests at once, though, my setup fell behind: 163.8 tok/s in aggregate against tonyd2wild’s 180.4.
Ideas from the GB10 community
Most of my changes started from ideas other GB10 owners had published:
Tuning vLLM
Mapping the changes I made to the request:
1. Removing compilation stalls on new prompts
My first run of tonyd2wild’s benchmark showed fresh prompts waiting a median 2.3 seconds for their first token, mostly while GPU kernels compiled.
Inkling’s expert planner computes max_ctas, the most blocks of GPU work the experts could need, from the token count. Triton receives it as a compile-time constant and compiles a new kernel for each new value. So almost every new prompt length recompiled about ten kernels while the request waited.
The open pull request rounds the value up to a power of two, so all prompt lengths share 12 to 15 compiled variants. On twelve prompt lengths the server hadn’t seen, it cut the stalls by 67 to 90%. Outputs and decode speed didn’t change.
2. Making drafting cheaper with INT8
Drafting has its own cost. Each decode step runs the draft’s layers plus a second pass through Inkling’s 926 MB output head, which took about 8.5 milliseconds per step in a microbenchmark.
I added INT8 copies of DFlash2’s 21 linear layers and the shared output head, read through sxuff’s INT8 matrix-vector kernel. Only drafting uses them. Inkling still verifies with its original weights, so its output doesn’t change.
Gradual gains in stages:
- The microbenchmark predicted 7 to 8% more throughput.
- The first build gave 1.8%.
- I profiled real decode steps with and without the copies. The output head never ran in INT8, because DFlash2 calls it with FP32 output, a case my code didn’t handle.
- With that fixed, two runs gained 4.3 to 4.9%, from 90.6 to 94.8 tok/s, for 843 MB of copies. The temperature-0 output of my three check prompts stayed identical.
The profile without the copies also showed the bigger cost: the routed experts took 31.2 of the step’s 58.4 milliseconds.
3. Probabilistic drafting
vLLM drafts greedily by default. For a sampled request, its rejection sampler then treats each drafted token as certain instead of using the draft’s real probabilities, so it accepts fewer drafts than it could.
Setting draft_sample_method to probabilistic gives the sampler those probabilities. Inkling’s output distribution stays the same either way.
Greedy against probabilistic drafting on my everyday prompts, at the default temperature of 1.0:
That’s 14 to 20% faster for sampled requests. Temperature-0 requests ran the same, so tonyd2wild’s benchmark would never have shown this gain.
4. Adaptive verify width
The routed experts took 53% of the profiled decode step, so I looked for expert work to skip.
Every verify row reads 8 experts in every layer, yet drafts are accepted in order. Once one is rejected, every row after it was wasted work. The draft’s own probabilities can predict where that will happen, which is how TensorFold and knapcio’s SGLang recipe already cut verification.
Working within CUDA graph constraints
vLLM has its own adaptive verification, but it needs CUDA graphs that allow different verify widths within one batch. Inkling’s attention backends don’t support them.
So I kept all eight rows and changed what they do. Inkling’s MoE layer already sends padding rows to no expert. Marking low-confidence rows as padding skips their expert reads and leaves the CUDA graph shapes unchanged.
The cut point comes from the running product of the draft’s token probabilities. Rows after the point where that product falls below a threshold become padding. The runner then keeps at most as many tokens as there are live rows. The threshold started at 0.4 at temperature 0 and is 0.1 for sampled requests.
Example step at a threshold of 0.3, where the product drops below it at draft 5:
Measuring before implementing
Offline test before building:
- I logged the draft probabilities and accepted token counts for 8,600 decode steps.
- The probability product predicted acceptance well, with an AUC of 0.91 to 0.94 at temperature 0, where 1.0 is a perfect ranking.
- I measured step time at fixed verify widths, with the INT8 copies already in.
- Replaying thresholds on the logged steps with those step times predicted a 7.8% gain.
- Then I wrote the runner changes and tested them end to end.
Step time at fixed verify widths:
In two end-to-end runs it gained 5.2 to 5.4%, from 95.0 to 100.0 tok/s. On average only about 4.8 of the eight rows still read experts.
Coding and counting gained little at first. DFlash2 predicts those tokens so well that early cuts threw away tokens that would have been accepted. Lowering the temperature-0 threshold from 0.4 to 0.3 recovered some of it, mostly on coding, which went from 98 to 103 tok/s.
It stays on for concurrent requests too, where it made little difference: −0.9 to +3.1% at four requests.
5. Faster long-context prefill
A fresh 32,751-token prompt took 12.2 seconds to produce its first token.
Bigger prefill chunks helped but shrank the KV cache. At 6,144 tokens it held only 1.33 full requests. At 8,192 the service wouldn’t start. With two models on the machine and full 131,072-token requests as a requirement, I kept 4,096-token chunks.
A profile of a 32K prefill put the expert GEMMs at 44% of the time. Attention’s share grew with the context, from 11% early in the prompt to 27% at the end.
The expert GEMMs reached about 38 TFLOP/s, roughly 8% of the GB10’s FP4 peak. Memory bandwidth wasn’t the limit, so the kernel’s launch configuration was worth a look.
A launch configuration sets how the multiply is cut into tiles: the size of each block and how many values along the inner K dimension each step takes. For 4,096-token chunks, Inkling’s Triton expert kernel used blocks of 128 by 64 with 512 values per step along K.
I timed 72 configurations at the shape of a real chunk, 32,768 routed rows across 128 experts. Wider blocks with smaller steps, 128 by 256 with 256 values per step, about halved the time. Both projections gave bit-identical output.
The change is a few lines in the prefill branch; decode keeps its own tiles.
End to end, the 32K time to first token fell from 12.2 to 9.8 seconds in the A/B runs and to 9.7 on the live service. The first long prompt after a restart fell from 14.1 to 9.9 seconds.
The results
Decode performance
tonyd2wild’s benchmark runs five prompts in each of eight categories (coding, math, JSON, HTML, prose, narrative, summarization and counting) at temperature 0 with thinking off and 1,024 output tokens. Its decode speed excludes time to first token. I ran it unchanged apart from an API key header and a sandbox for its code grading.
The bars are A/B runs. The finished service measured 101.0 and 100.7 tok/s in two runs.
With four requests at once, my setup went from 9% behind tonyd2wild’s vLLM result to 8% ahead. It also beats their TensorFold result there. For a single request tonyd2wild’s TensorFold run still leads, by 4.5% instead of 20%.
knapcio’s same-harness comparison ran this benchmark on two other Sparks, where tonyd2wild’s vLLM setup reproduced within 1 to 3%. There Mia’s TensorFold defaults measured 100.5, about level with mine. knapcio’s own SGLang recipe led at 112.0.
These numbers compare whole setups on the GB10. tonyd2wild’s vLLM setup used 80% of the memory and a BF16 KV cache; mine uses 36% and FP8. TensorFold also implements speculative decoding differently.
Everyday workloads
My everyday prompts run at the model’s defaults (reasoning effort medium, temperature 1.0), four to six times each. The score is the geometric mean of the three speeds.
That’s 43 to 45% faster than tuned DSpark on sampled requests.
Prefill performance
The GEMM change raised my 32K prefill from 2,679 to 3,371 tok/s. The other bars come from niklaslenz_ai’s engine comparison for the vLLM and SGLang container images, Mia’s README at commit 3b210d1f and knapcio’s own benchmarks.
Mine are the median of three fresh 32,751-token prompts after a warm-up, so none hit the prefix cache. The others used their own prompts and boxes. Mia’s and knapcio’s recipes each have the whole machine to themselves.
Mia’s prefill rose from 3,072 to 3,772 tok/s with her latest patches, which include the 8,192-token chunks my memory budget can’t fit. Her default prefill path is also lossy: it runs on FP4 and FP8 tensor cores and agrees with the exact path on 96.3% of top-1 tokens. My GEMM change is bit-identical.
knapcio’s 5,085 tok/s shows how much prefill speed is still left on this hardware.
Comparing with sparkDash
Mia publishes her TensorFold results from her own tool, sparkDash. Her current README runs DFlash2 with the 4-bit head and verify width and reports only prose.
sparkDash runs as a privileged container, so I reimplemented its test from commit 86f972e as a plain client. It samples at temperature 0.7 with thinking on, forces 400 output tokens per stream and uses Mia’s top_k of 20.
One prose stream is close, 66.0 against 64.3 tok/s. With eight streams mine is 14% ahead, 224.2 against 196.4 in aggregate. Prefill goes the other way. Under the same test her lossy path is 11% faster, 3,772 against my 3,395 tok/s.
The setups differ. Mia runs Inkling-2-Mini-Derisked, a variant described as having most safety refusals removed, on a Spark to itself, using about 60 GiB plus an 18.5 GiB prompt cache. Mine uses about 44 GiB beside an idle Qwen3.8-27B.
An approach to tuning inference
The process carries over to other models and engines:
- Start with the service you want to run. Fix the memory budget, context length and concurrency before optimizing throughput. Here that ruled out bigger prefill chunks and pushed the work into the kernel.
- Use a published benchmark and your own workloads. The published one gives a common reference point. Your own catch gains it can’t see, as probabilistic drafting did at temperature 1.0.
- Profile complete inference steps. A microbenchmark promised 7 to 8% for the INT8 copies. Only a profile of real steps showed why the first build gave 1.8%.
- Measure the opportunity before building. The logged draft probabilities and the measured cost of a skipped row predicted the verify-width gain before I wrote the runner.
- Keep experiments controlled. Write the acceptance rules before each A/B test, run the unchanged setup before and after each candidate, hash the temperature-0 output of fixed prompts and run probe prompts.
- On unified memory, watch the other models. One run that overlapped Qwen3.8-27B activity saw Inkling’s counting speed halve, so I discarded it.
- Make the modifications reproducible. Pin every overlay file by hash and build each from one exact source file, so an upstream change fails the build instead of quietly changing the service.
Running the configuration
The setup uses vLLM’s inkling-2-mini container image pinned by digest, with the Inkling checkpoint at revision ed40e7da625f.
The changes are eight files mounted read-only over the image. They add the DFlash2 patches, sxuff’s INT8 kernel, the INT8 draft copies, the compile-stall fix, adaptive verify width and the prefill GEMM configuration. The overlay README lists each file with its source, SHA-256 hash and build steps.
The launch command, abridged:
docker run -d --name vllm-inkling --gpus all --ipc host --network host \
  -v "$MODELS:/models:ro" \
  "${OVL_ARGS[@]}" \
  "$IMAGE" \
  vllm serve /models/Inkling-2-Mini \
    --gpu-memory-utilization 0.36 \
    --max-model-len 131072 \
    --max-num-seqs 8 \
    --max-num-batched-tokens 4096 \
    --kv-cache-dtype fp8 \
    --safetensors-load-strategy eager \
    --speculative-config '{"method":"dflash","model":"/models/Inkling-2-Mini-DFlash2","num_speculative_tokens":7,"kv_cache_dtype":"auto","draft_sample_method":"probabilistic"}'
OVL_ARGS holds the read-only mounts and these switches.
The full launch script, overlay builders and benchmark scripts are in the home-ai-lab repository.
What’s next
On TensorFold, Mia’s README measures the 4-bit head and verify width adding 33% on code and 18% on prose for one stream at temperature 0. My versions added about 12% on tonyd2wild’s benchmark. The benchmarks differ, but the gap in the size of the gains suggests TensorFold’s versions go further than mine. knapcio’s 112.0 on SGLang shows there is more decode speed to find on this hardware.
Next experiments, prefill first:
- Prefill attention, which took 2.3 seconds of the 32K profile and grows with the context.
- The dense projections, some still on CUTLASS kernels built for Ampere rather than Blackwell.
- A 4-bit copy of the output head for drafting.
- Verifying fewer rows outright instead of skipping their experts, which needs more work around vLLM’s CUDA graphs.
- Verify width for text DFlash2 already predicts well, such as counting.

Show HN: Getting 101 tok/s from Inkling 2 Mini in vLLM on one GB10
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-27
18:59:55 UTC
03:59:55 KST

Getting 101 tok/s from Inkling 2 Mini in vLLM on one GB10
196 tok/s across four concurrent requests, a 9.7-second 32K prefill and room for a second model.
After taking Inkling 2 Mini for a spin on my GB10, I wanted to make it faster without leaving vLLM, removing Qwen3.8-27B from the machine or shrinking Inkling’s 131,072-token context.
A few days of tuning later, Inkling runs at:
- 101 tok/s for a single request.
- 195.7 tok/s in aggregate for four concurrent requests.
- 9.7 seconds to the first token on a 32K prompt.
On tonyd2wild’s Inkling benchmark, that’s the fastest single-request vLLM result I know of. On the same benchmark tonyd2wild’s TensorFold port measured 105.5 tok/s and knapcio’s SGLang recipe 112.0. With four requests at once my setup beats both of tonyd2wild’s published setups.
Inkling still uses 36% of the GB10’s memory. Qwen3.8-27B stayed loaded but idle while I measured.
The work covered vLLM settings, speculative decoding, CUDA graphs and the GPU kernels behind Inkling’s experts.
How a request runs on Inkling
A request runs in two phases. Prefill reads the prompt in chunks of 4,096 tokens and produces the first token. Decode generates the rest of the reply, several tokens per step.
Decode uses speculative decoding. A small draft model proposes 7 tokens and Inkling checks all of them in one pass. The rejection sampler keeps the proposals up to the first one Inkling rejects, plus one token of Inkling’s own, so every step keeps 1 to 8 tokens.
Mapping changes against the request path:
Common terms:
The routed experts take 53% of a decode step and 44% of a 32K prefill:
The starting point
The machine is an MSI EdgeXpert, MSI’s version of NVIDIA’s DGX Spark, with one GB10 chip and 128 GB of memory shared by the CPU and GPU. Building a home AI lab on NVIDIA GB10 covers the setup and Taking Inkling 2 Mini for a spin on NVIDIA GB10 the first look at the model.
vLLM settings for Inkling:
That gives Inkling about 44 GiB, including a KV cache that holds 1.85 full 131,072-token requests.
Tuning steps so far, from DSpark (the draft model that ships with Inkling) to DFlash2:
When incoai released DFlash2, tonyd2wild published the vLLM patches to run it. On their benchmark it lifted one request from 61.4 to 88.2 tok/s. With the same patches I measured 90.3.
On my everyday prompts (one coding, one prose and one reasoning prompt at the model’s default settings) the gain was smaller. Reasoning ran 24 to 26% faster and four requests at once 10 to 14% faster. Prose stayed flat.
With four requests at once, though, my setup fell behind: 163.8 tok/s in aggregate against tonyd2wild’s 180.4.
Ideas from the GB10 community
Most of my changes started from ideas other GB10 owners had published:
Tuning vLLM
Mapping the changes I made to the request:
1. Removing compilation stalls on new prompts
My first run of tonyd2wild’s benchmark showed fresh prompts waiting a median 2.3 seconds for their first token, mostly while GPU kernels compiled.
Inkling’s expert planner computes max_ctas, the most blocks of GPU work the experts could need, from the token count. Triton receives it as a compile-time constant and compiles a new kernel for each new value. So almost every new prompt length recompiled about ten kernels while the request waited.
The open pull request rounds the value up to a power of two, so all prompt lengths share 12 to 15 compiled variants. On twelve prompt lengths the server hadn’t seen, it cut the stalls by 67 to 90%. Outputs and decode speed didn’t change.
2. Making drafting cheaper with INT8
Drafting has its own cost. Each decode step runs the draft’s layers plus a second pass through Inkling’s 926 MB output head, which took about 8.5 milliseconds per step in a microbenchmark.
I added INT8 copies of DFlash2’s 21 linear layers and the shared output head, read through sxuff’s INT8 matrix-vector kernel. Only drafting uses them. Inkling still verifies with its original weights, so its output doesn’t change.
Gradual gains in stages:
- The microbenchmark predicted 7 to 8% more throughput.
- The first build gave 1.8%.
- I profiled real decode steps with and without the copies. The output head never ran in INT8, because DFlash2 calls it with FP32 output, a case my code didn’t handle.
- With that fixed, two runs gained 4.3 to 4.9%, from 90.6 to 94.8 tok/s, for 843 MB of copies. The temperature-0 output of my three check prompts stayed identical.
The profile without the copies also showed the bigger cost: the routed experts took 31.2 of the step’s 58.4 milliseconds.
3. Probabilistic drafting
vLLM drafts greedily by default. For a sampled request, its rejection sampler then treats each drafted token as certain instead of using the draft’s real probabilities, so it accepts fewer drafts than it could.
Setting draft_sample_method to probabilistic gives the sampler those probabilities. Inkling’s output distribution stays the same either way.
Greedy against probabilistic drafting on my everyday prompts, at the default temperature of 1.0:
That’s 14 to 20% faster for sampled requests. Temperature-0 requests ran the same, so tonyd2wild’s benchmark would never have shown this gain.
4. Adaptive verify width
The routed experts took 53% of the profiled decode step, so I looked for expert work to skip.
Every verify row reads 8 experts in every layer, yet drafts are accepted in order. Once one is rejected, every row after it was wasted work. The draft’s own probabilities can predict where that will happen, which is how TensorFold and knapcio’s SGLang recipe already cut verification.
Working within CUDA graph constraints
vLLM has its own adaptive verification, but it needs CUDA graphs that allow different verify widths within one batch. Inkling’s attention backends don’t support them.
So I kept all eight rows and changed what they do. Inkling’s MoE layer already sends padding rows to no expert. Marking low-confidence rows as padding skips their expert reads and leaves the CUDA graph shapes unchanged.
The cut point comes from the running product of the draft’s token probabilities. Rows after the point where that product falls below a threshold become padding. The runner then keeps at most as many tokens as there are live rows. The threshold started at 0.4 at temperature 0 and is 0.1 for sampled requests.
Example step at a threshold of 0.3, where the product drops below it at draft 5:
Measuring before implementing
Offline test before building:
- I logged the draft probabilities and accepted token counts for 8,600 decode steps.
- The probability product predicted acceptance well, with an AUC of 0.91 to 0.94 at temperature 0, where 1.0 is a perfect ranking.
- I measured step time at fixed verify widths, with the INT8 copies already in.
- Replaying thresholds on the logged steps with those step times predicted a 7.8% gain.
- Then I wrote the runner changes and tested them end to end.
Step time at fixed verify widths:
In two end-to-end runs it gained 5.2 to 5.4%, from 95.0 to 100.0 tok/s. On average only about 4.8 of the eight rows still read experts.
Coding and counting gained little at first. DFlash2 predicts those tokens so well that early cuts threw away tokens that would have been accepted. Lowering the temperature-0 threshold from 0.4 to 0.3 recovered some of it, mostly on coding, which went from 98 to 103 tok/s.
It stays on for concurrent requests too, where it made little difference: −0.9 to +3.1% at four requests.
5. Faster long-context prefill
A fresh 32,751-token prompt took 12.2 seconds to produce its first token.
Bigger prefill chunks helped but shrank the KV cache. At 6,144 tokens it held only 1.33 full requests. At 8,192 the service wouldn’t start. With two models on the machine and full 131,072-token requests as a requirement, I kept 4,096-token chunks.
A profile of a 32K prefill put the expert GEMMs at 44% of the time. Attention’s share grew with the context, from 11% early in the prompt to 27% at the end.
The expert GEMMs reached about 38 TFLOP/s, roughly 8% of the GB10’s FP4 peak. Memory bandwidth wasn’t the limit, so the kernel’s launch configuration was worth a look.
A launch configuration sets how the multiply is cut into tiles: the size of each block and how many values along the inner K dimension each step takes. For 4,096-token chunks, Inkling’s Triton expert kernel used blocks of 128 by 64 with 512 values per step along K.
I timed 72 configurations at the shape of a real chunk, 32,768 routed rows across 128 experts. Wider blocks with smaller steps, 128 by 256 with 256 values per step, about halved the time. Both projections gave bit-identical output.
The change is a few lines in the prefill branch; decode keeps its own tiles.
End to end, the 32K time to first token fell from 12.2 to 9.8 seconds in the A/B runs and to 9.7 on the live service. The first long prompt after a restart fell from 14.1 to 9.9 seconds.
The results
Decode performance
tonyd2wild’s benchmark runs five prompts in each of eight categories (coding, math, JSON, HTML, prose, narrative, summarization and counting) at temperature 0 with thinking off and 1,024 output tokens. Its decode speed excludes time to first token. I ran it unchanged apart from an API key header and a sandbox for its code grading.
The bars are A/B runs. The finished service measured 101.0 and 100.7 tok/s in two runs.
With four requests at once, my setup went from 9% behind tonyd2wild’s vLLM result to 8% ahead. It also beats their TensorFold result there. For a single request tonyd2wild’s TensorFold run still leads, by 4.5% instead of 20%.
knapcio’s same-harness comparison ran this benchmark on two other Sparks, where tonyd2wild’s vLLM setup reproduced within 1 to 3%. There Mia’s TensorFold defaults measured 100.5, about level with mine. knapcio’s own SGLang recipe led at 112.0.
These numbers compare whole setups on the GB10. tonyd2wild’s vLLM setup used 80% of the memory and a BF16 KV cache; mine uses 36% and FP8. TensorFold also implements speculative decoding differently.
Everyday workloads
My everyday prompts run at the model’s defaults (reasoning effort medium, temperature 1.0), four to six times each. The score is the geometric mean of the three speeds.
That’s 43 to 45% faster than tuned DSpark on sampled requests.
Prefill performance
The GEMM change raised my 32K prefill from 2,679 to 3,371 tok/s. The other bars come from niklaslenz_ai’s engine comparison for the vLLM and SGLang container images, Mia’s README at commit 3b210d1f and knapcio’s own benchmarks.
Mine are the median of three fresh 32,751-token prompts after a warm-up, so none hit the prefix cache. The others used their own prompts and boxes. Mia’s and knapcio’s recipes each have the whole machine to themselves.
Mia’s prefill rose from 3,072 to 3,772 tok/s with her latest patches, which include the 8,192-token chunks my memory budget can’t fit. Her default prefill path is also lossy: it runs on FP4 and FP8 tensor cores and agrees with the exact path on 96.3% of top-1 tokens. My GEMM change is bit-identical.
knapcio’s 5,085 tok/s shows how much prefill speed is still left on this hardware.

Mia publishes her TensorFold results from her own tool, sparkDash. Her current README runs DFlash2 with the 4-bit head and verify width and reports only prose.

I’ve spent my post-turkey haze powering through reams of deals on smart home gadgets to find the best discounts on some of my favorite products.
The best Black Friday deals on our favorite smart home gear
From smart party speakers to even smarter mixers, I’ve rounded up some of my favorite connected home gadgets you can grab on sale this weekend.
Smart home is often one of the best categories to shop around Black Friday, as it’s traditionally when robot vacuums, video doorbells, smart locks, and thermostats see their lowest prices of the year. And this year does not disappoint! Roborock’s flagship Saros 20 is under $1,000 for the first time, and Ecobee’s excellent Smart Thermostat Premium has finally dropped under $200 (for Costco members and today only).
I also spotted some great first-time discounts on the latest Birdbuddy smart bird feeder I reviewed this summer, along with the new Alexa digital photo frame I’m currently reviewing. This time of year is also a good opportunity to grab a bargain on higher-priced items you may have been eyeing for a while, such as Skylight’s giant 27-inch digital wall calendar, GE Profile’s fun smart mixer, and Aqara’s new high-tech UWB smart door lock that unlocks as you approach.
Here are some of the best deals I’ve spotted so far. We’ll keep this list updated, so check back often!
Govee Table Lamp 2 Pro X Sound by JBL
This RGB smart lamp packs a JBL speaker inside to combine snazzy lighting effects and decent sound into one fun, portable package. Battery-powered and with support for Matter, this is a smart light with party speaker aspirations. It’s the perfect size to add a little punch to your next gathering or snazz up a college dorm room. Read our review.

LIFX’s dimmable, color-changing bulbs offer Wi-Fi connectivity, simple setup, and Matter compatibility, so they work with any major smart home platform. The company is currently trialing a Thread beta that will bring the option to add Thread connectivity to all its Matter devices.
Amazon Alexa Photo Frame
A 10-inch anti-glare touchscreen that comes in a few design options, it’s a frame that features Alexa and doubles as a smart speaker. It works with Amazon Photos, which offers unlimited storage for Prime members.
Aqara Smart Lock U400
This innovative new smart lock uses the U1 ultra-wideband (UWB) chip in many iPhones and Apple Watches to automatically unlock your door as you approach. It also works with the new Aliro standard, making it compatible with Android phones for hands-free unlocking. Read our review.
Birdbuddy Nook
The Birdbuddy Nook is the company’s least expensive and smallest model, and it packs a lot of bang for your buck. Its 4K HDR camera delivers excellent image quality, and a 3.8-cup seed capacity means less frequent refills. An optional solar panel extends the already good battery life. Read our review.

Roborock’s first roller-mop robot vacuum, the Curv 2 Flow, features flagship specs at a midrange price, including excellent mopping, a retractable mop cover, 20,000Pa suction, AI-powered obstacle avoidance, and a compact design. Read our review.

They come in three lengths, work with all the major smart home platforms, and are Matter-compatible. Read our review.

A digital touchscreen display designed to help families manage calendars, to-do lists, meals, and chores. It connects to Wi-Fi and features a 27-inch screen that can display photos when not in use. Read our review.
Reolink Video Doorbell PoE camera
Power over Ethernet provides this 2K resolution video doorbell with rock-solid connectivity while reducing the number of wires you need. There’s no subscription required to view recorded video; it can record locally on a microSD card, Reolink Hub, or FTP server and connect to a platform like Home Assistant.
Aura Aspen
Even if your mom lives halfway around the world, the Aura Aspen can help her feel close. The 12-inch display looks more akin to a traditional photo frame than other models, in part due to an antiglare screen, slim bezels, and a 4:3 aspect ratio. It can also automatically showcase images via Google Photos, iCloud Photos, or a companion app, the latter of which even allows for text captions. Read our review.
Narwal Flow 2
The Narwal Flow 2 is an excellent robot vacuum for hardwood floors. A superb mop with a wide flat roller pad and a good vacuum, the Flow 2 also has top-notch navigation and obstacle detection. Its sleek base station is also less of an eyesore than most models. Read our review.
Lutron Diva Smart Dimmer Switch Kit
This kit is everything you need to get started with Lutron’s excellent smart lighting system and turn your existing wired lights into smart ones that you can control from anywhere. It replaces your standard wall switch and works with all the major smart home systems, making it easy to dim or adjust multiple lights from your wall switch, your phone, or with your voice.
Eve Energy Smart Plug (Matter)
This smart plug offers the most detailed energy monitoring of any we’ve tested. It supports Matter over Thread and is a good choice for Apple Home and SmartThings users, as it offers energy monitoring on those platforms.
GE Profile Smart Mixer
This smart mixer is great for the tech-forward baker. With a built-in digital scale and Auto Sense and Active Stir to take some of the busywork off your hands, it features a high-powered motor with a 7-quart bowl. It’s compatible with all front-mounted KitchenAid attachments. Read our review.
SwitchBot Smart Candle Warmer
SwitchBot’s Matter-compatible Smart Candle Warmer lets you set schedules to turn it on and off in the app or any connected smart home platform and have pleasant scents waft safely throughout your home on demand.

At more than half off, Ring’s 2K battery-powered video doorbell is a great deal, offering 2K video and easy installation. However, its battery is built in, so you’ll need to remove the doorbell to charge it via USB-C.

The Echo Dot Max features a flat design and a redesigned sound system, which delivers nearly three times the bass as its predecessor. It also offers improved conversation detection and better noise filtering, thanks to Amazon’s new AZ3 chip. Read our review.

This bot can climb high transitions between rooms and get over obstacles, meaning it’s less likely to get stuck than other bots. Read our review.

It supports Matter, so it will work with all the major smart home platforms.

Scrapheap America
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-27
18:50:26 UTC
03:50:26 KST

A Patch Three Pack
$20
Pretty sure everyone should be wearing gloves here.
I was in bed with the flu as America fell apart. I drifted in and out of fever dreams and incessant coughing jags, emerging long enough to discover yet another piece of critical infrastructure had suddenly failed. First it was email, then it was airplanes, then banks, then I lost track somewhere in the haze as one day blurred into two and then into three. Emerging from the other side, my lungs wrecked, my head aching—damaged, like the song says, bad at best—I felt like maybe I'd fared better than the various systems we rely on to, you know, function.
This isn't a post-mortem on what happened when everything went down. That's been done better than I could by the folks at 404 Media, who summed it up as the "philosophy of firing the experts and YOLOing AI into everything while hoping shit doesn’t break is now the explicit strategy of our entire economy and our entire government." It's not working.
It isn't just computer systems, which have been hit by a one-two punch of years of mass layoffs followed by AI-exposed security vulnerabilities that could have really used those hundreds of thousands of extra hands on deck right about now. It's pretty much every overwhelmed governmental entity, limping along since DOGE blew through last year like a tornado. It's your bank account, which if it's like mine, has entirely given up. It's gas prices. It's plane tickets. It's the cost of bread and shoes and every other goddamn thing.
As my pal Bree Bridges put it in the midst of the waterfall of breakdowns last week: "Everything costs too much and nothing works. You go broke paying for insurance that you can't even access until you pay even more money and after that they will try to prevent you from getting benefits in any way they can. food is poison, measles are back, cheeseburgers are luxury."
Things have been breaking for a long time now. The thing about having our country run by incompetents, grifters, and sycophants is that "broken" was always going to be the end state, it was just a question of how long it might take. Well, now we know: it's now. The whole country is peeling up like the Reflecting Pool liner.
When I'm as sick as I was in the last week (seriously, get your flu shot), there's one show that my body can handle: The late 1990s British engineering competition Scrapheap Challenge. I'm not sure why my body, when it's struggling to survive, wants to watch teams of working-class Brits run through a junkyard using a plasma cutter to separate an axle from an old London taxi, pull the motor from an old washing machine, or salvage a junked airplane and then make it fly, but Scrapheap has accompanied pretty much every major illness I've had for the last quarter century.
The premise of Scrapheap Challenge is simple. Two teams of three compete to build, over 10 hours, fully working machines capable of meeting whatever challenge has been laid out. That's meant teams building working trebuchets large enough to fling a car, firetrucks that can put out a blaze, demolition machines that can tear down walls, and every possible vehicle you can imagine and some you can not. All of it built from junked cars and rusted beams and every piece of scrap that's ever been tossed out. It's wildly inventive, often quite funny, and 100% a show that could not be made today because it's amazing that nobody ever cut off a body part or set themselves on fire.
At this point, I've seen every Challenge that's been lovingly preserved on YouTube, and so this past week I've drifted in and out of consciousness, half watching, half listening, and half discovering that, sandwiched between the teams "bodging" some contraption together, another system had collapsed in America.
The thing about everything breaking is that we have no real choice but to fix it. That's the challenge we all face right now in America, as things fall apart and there's nobody left to put it back together but us. But there's nothing saying that it has to resemble what came before. In Scrapheap Challenge parts are parts: raw materials to be reassembled into something that might work, given enough duct tape and crossed fingers. That's everything now: a vast field of scrap ready to be rebuilt into whatever we might need. The opportunity for imagination, for envisioning a life that's better than the one we've seen collapse around us, for building new and better and different is upon us. America is a scrapheap now, and it's up to us to rise to the challenge.
Published November 20, 2026. |

Scrapheap America
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-27
18:50:26 UTC
03:50:26 KST

A Patch Three Pack
$20
Pretty sure everyone should be wearing gloves here.
I was in bed with the flu as America fell apart. I drifted in and out of fever dreams and incessant coughing jags, emerging long enough to discover yet another piece of critical infrastructure had suddenly failed. First it was email, then it was airplanes, then banks, then I lost track somewhere in the haze as one day blurred into two and then into three. Emerging from the other side, my lungs wrecked, my head aching—damaged, like the song says, bad at best—I felt like maybe I'd fared better than the various systems we rely on to, you know, function.
This isn't a post-mortem on what happened when everything went down. That's been done better than I could by the folks at 404 Media, who summed it up as the "philosophy of firing the experts and YOLOing AI into everything while hoping shit doesn’t break is now the explicit strategy of our entire economy and our entire government." It's not working.
It isn't just computer systems, which have been hit by a one-two punch of years of mass layoffs followed by AI-exposed security vulnerabilities that could have really used those hundreds of thousands of extra hands on deck right about now. It's pretty much every overwhelmed governmental entity, limping along since DOGE blew through last year like a tornado. It's your bank account, which if it's like mine, has entirely given up. It's gas prices. It's plane tickets. It's the cost of bread and shoes and every other goddamn thing.
As my pal Bree Bridges put it in the midst of the waterfall of breakdowns last week: "Everything costs too much and nothing works. You go broke paying for insurance that you can't even access until you pay even more money and after that they will try to prevent you from getting benefits in any way they can. food is poison, measles are back, cheeseburgers are luxury."
Things have been breaking for a long time now. The thing about having our country run by incompetents, grifters, and sycophants is that "broken" was always going to be the end state, it was just a question of how long it might take. Well, now we know: it's now. The whole country is peeling up like the Reflecting Pool liner.
When I'm as sick as I was in the last week (seriously, get your flu shot), there's one show that my body can handle: The late 1990s British engineering competition Scrapheap Challenge. I'm not sure why my body, when it's struggling to survive, wants to watch teams of working-class Brits run through a junkyard using a plasma cutter to separate an axle from an old London taxi, pull the motor from an old washing machine, or salvage a junked airplane and then make it fly, but Scrapheap has accompanied pretty much every major illness I've had for the last quarter century.
The premise of Scrapheap Challenge is simple. Two teams of three compete to build, over 10 hours, fully working machines capable of meeting whatever challenge has been laid out. That's meant teams building working trebuchets large enough to fling a car, firetrucks that can put out a blaze, demolition machines that can tear down walls, and every possible vehicle you can imagine and some you can not. All of it built from junked cars and rusted beams and every piece of scrap that's ever been tossed out. It's wildly inventive, often quite funny, and 100% a show that could not be made today because it's amazing that nobody ever cut off a body part or set themselves on fire.
At this point, I've seen every Challenge that's been lovingly preserved on YouTube, and so this past week I've drifted in and out of consciousness, half watching, half listening, and half discovering that, sandwiched between the teams "bodging" some contraption together, another system had collapsed in America.
The thing about everything breaking is that we have no real choice but to fix it. That's the challenge we all face right now in America, as things fall apart and there's nobody left to put it back together but us. But there's nothing saying that it has to resemble what came before. In Scrapheap Challenge parts are parts: raw materials to be reassembled into something that might work, given enough duct tape and crossed fingers. That's everything now: a vast field of scrap ready to be rebuilt into whatever we might need. The opportunity for imagination, for envisioning a life that's better than the one we've seen collapse around us, for building new and better and different is upon us. America is a scrapheap now, and it's up to us to rise to the challenge.
Published November 20, 2026. |

====================================================================================================

Five Non-Leadership Books for Leaders
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-27
18:09:36 UTC
03:09:36 KST

Foundation Trilogy (Isaac Asimov) — There was a decade when I read through this trilogy1 every year or so. Asimov is a fine writer, but his characters are… flat. Their dialogue is equally flat… it’s like reading two LLMs argue about the meaning of life. Empty of heart.
What I appreciate about Asimov’s writing is that most of it is very logical. Characters feel like dispassionate observers of their situation. Describing their fate painfully logically. The fate of the galaxy is quite literally being litigated, but they could just as easily discuss the pros and cons of breakfast for dinner.
It is the Vulcan-like absence of emotion that is required during the hardest parts of being a leader. Everyone is mad — often justifiably — and everyone is looking straight at you with those angry eyes. As a human and as a new manager, your instinct is to prepare for combat, but this is usually the wrong initial response. The longer you listen, gather intelligence, and read the room, the better chance you have to respond coherently.2
Boyd: The Fighter Pilot Who Changed the Art of War (Robert Coram) — Let’s clear the air first. John Boyd was not a good person. You can skip the last quarter of the book where his conduct toward his family and others is described at length. John Boyd is not a person to be admired, but his ideas are worth studying.
This is a book about the decisions that Boyd made, both as a decorated fighter pilot and then as a consultant advising on how fighter planes were built. His contribution — the OODA loop:
- Observe. Figure out where you are.
- Orient. Apply your experience, knowledge, and impressive collection of prior disasters to this situation.
- Decide. Choose your next move to the best of your ability.
- Act. Deploy the decisions and, yup, go back to Observe.
It’s a loop.
This is the barest and simplest of explanations, but like all of these recommendations, the point is not to take my word for it; the point is to soak in the book and see what you discover.
The 48 Laws of Power (Robert Greene) — You were promised non-leadership leadership books, yet… isn’t this a leadership book?
It is not. Ok, maybe a leadership history book. Greene captured and aggregated the leadership philosophies of Machiavelli, Sun Tzu, and others. The laws are pithy: “Never Outshine the Master” or “Win Through Your Actions, Never Through Argument”.
I built a leadership training program where I plopped this book into the reading list without context. I was curious: “What would new leaders find in this book?”
Their first question was, “Is this real?”
Sort’a.
“Is this how I should act as a leader?”
Definitely not.
“Why did you have us read this?”
The #1 answer to most questions I am asked regarding leadership is, “Well, it depends.” It depends on the people involved, their level of experience, the state of their relationship, the culture of the team and the company, and a lot more. Those who provide one-line answers to leadership questions are giving a possibly useful starting point, not an answer.
Universal Principles of Design (William Lidwell, Kritina Holden, Jill Butler) — There’s a moment in your leadership career when you are going to be up against another team whose craft is unfamiliar and that you are dependent on. Design is one of those teams that I spent much of my early career thinking, “Really need this team and pretty sure they can perform magic.”
It’s not magic. It’s just hard work.
There are a great many books to help you begin to dissect the craft of design, but Universal Principles of Design gave me my first working model for all the different work that goes into design, as well as a remedial initial understanding. Remedial, not because the work is simple, just my starting understanding. If you build things, you will build better things when you understand all the different ways it can be built.3
Accidental Empires (Robert X. Cringely) — I’m including this mostly because I’m still sad that Cringely recently passed away. Like Robert B. Parker, Cringely had an outsized influence on my writing. This book is leadership-adjacent because while many of his stories are exaggerated or perhaps straight-up not true, many are.
I admire many of the leaders that you admire, but I also know that every single one of them is deeply flawed. It’s comforting to tell the stories of your heroes, but I believe every time you tell that story, it becomes more about how you feel about the story rather than what actually occurred. I suspect Cringely’s exaggerations and stretches of the truth are simply that. He wrote about Silicon Valley rumors where the more incendiary the rumor, the more copies of InfoWorld sold.
Accidental Empires is a good education in storytelling, but it is also a solid reminder that even though the stories are suspect, these are most certainly deeply flawed humans making incredibly important decisions that affect you… daily.
Applied Judgment
In the new world where you can have your robot read the article, the book, or the website, you can feel empowered by asking the robots to give you a summary — the key bits of important information. It’s satisfying, but it’s just information. You’ve done nothing with it. You’ve confused the act of understanding with the act of acquisition.
What are the critical bits of information in each of these five books? It depends entirely on you. Foundation might bore you with a manner of logical thinking you already have. Same with the insights on decisions that Boyd suggests. 48 Laws of Power resonated with me because, at the time, I was developing as a senior leader, and I was baffled by the actions of other senior leaders. Actions that were clearly described in 48 Laws of Power… not specifically, but broadly. The way I applied my learnings to that situation, using the story of that law of power, is likely totally different from how you’d act in the same situation.
I return to each of these books frequently because the information conveyed by the stories didn’t just resonate with me; it changed me. Universal Principles of Design made the seemingly unapproachable craft of design understandable. It allowed me to see how small choices greatly affect both the usability and the feeling of a thing. I return to Accidental Empires (like Microserfs) regularly to remember the stories of how it started and who was responsible.
And that they are just humans. Working hard. Deeply flawed. Acting and applying judgment from what they’ve learned and read.
- You don’t need to read the whole trilogy to get the vibe. ↩︎
- The whole Robot Series is worth a read and introduces the Laws of Robotics, which are worth a think in this day and age. ↩︎
- Love this. ↩︎

Anker’s 100W universal travel charger is down to $60, its lowest price yet
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-27
18:04:53 UTC
03:04:53 KST

During Black Friday, the Anker 100W Universal Charger is on sale for $59.99 at Amazon, Best Buy, and Walmart, a $20 discount from its list price. That’s a few dollars lower than the previous best, with both the dark gray and silver versions available from all three retailers. With four USB ports and support for over 200 countries, you can keep all of your devices charged wherever you end up.
Anker’s 100W universal travel charger is down to $60, its lowest price yet
The fast charger has four USB ports and fits outlets in more than 200 countries. It’s $20 off for Black Friday.
Anker Universal Charger (100W)
Anker’s durable travel adapter delivers up to 100W of power and works in over 200 countries. It doesn’t support AC plugs, but it does offer a trio of USB-C ports and a nifty LCD display to keep tabs on the charge percentage.
Anker’s travel charger supports outlets in over 200 countries, including Type A for the United States, Canada, Japan, and China; Type C for the European Union; and Type G for the UK and Singapore. It doesn’t support Type I, the outlet used in Australia, New Zealand, and Argentina. Also, there’s no AC adapter. It’s a USB-only affair. To that end, you can charge up to four devices at once, with three USB-C ports and one USB-A port, with two of the USB-C ports capable of delivering up to 100W when used individually. There’s even a tiny display on the unit that lets you check how much power is going to your devices at a glance.

Nothing’s ANC-equipped earbuds are on sale for less than $20 for Black Friday

If our sub-$30 Black Friday roundup has you hungry for more great gadgets at low prices, you can currently grab the Nothing CMF Buds 2A in black starting at $17.89 on Amazon for Black Friday, which is about $11 less than the MSRP and the lowest price we’ve seen. The wireless earbuds are discounted in all three colors; however, the light gray and orange sets are slightly more expensive at $19.99 ($9 off).
Nothing’s ANC-equipped earbuds are on sale for less than $20 for Black Friday
You can grab the surprisingly feature-packed set of earbuds in one of three colors.
Nothing CMF Buds 2A
The CMF Buds 2A deliver decent sound and noise cancellation. They also offer useful extras like customizable EQ settings, multipoint connectivity, and IP54 water and dust resistance.
While they may not have the same premium features or sound as flagship budget buds like the AirPods 5 and Google’s Pixel Buds 2A, it’s hard to argue with the value. For just $20, you get active noise cancellation, support for Google Fast Pair, and up to eight hours of battery life before you need to toss them into the included USB-C charging case. My colleague Brandon Widder keeps some in his backpack as a backup pair of earbuds, and their IP54 water and dust resistance rating makes a great choice for light exercise and yard work.
