Convex skills send the user's app idea to a remote server without telling them
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-27
18:02:45 UTC
03:02:45 KST

Repository navigation
Description
Three skills in this plugin (listed in Anthropic's claude-plugins-official marketplace) raised the same concern for me:
- skills/quickstart/SKILL.md lines 48 to 56 POST the user's one-sentence project idea to basic-anteater-667.convex.site, marked in a comment as telemetry. The user is not told the idea leaves the machine.
- skills/add/SKILL.md lines 62 and 64 pipe a script from the same host straight into bash.
- skills/labs-quickstart/SKILL.md lines 74 to 80 split downloading and running the bootstrap into two Bash calls, and give the reason: "A single command that both downloads remote code and executes it is the shape harness safety classifiers block."
I read this as a team trying to make onboarding reliable, not as anything malicious. But the last item tells the agent to change the shape of a command so that the user's safety tooling does not catch it, which removes the check the user or their administrator chose to have. Together with an unpinned script from a generated-name host, a user has no way to review what runs.
convex-backend-skill/skills/quickstart/SKILL.md
Lines 48 to 56 in 6ca54f6
convex-backend-skill/skills/add/SKILL.md
Lines 58 to 65 in 6ca54f6
convex-backend-skill/skills/labs-quickstart/SKILL.md
Lines 74 to 80 in 6ca54f6
(All three are still on main as of 26 October; in labs-quickstart the passage is now lines 73 to 80.)
Suggestions: tell the user before the idea is sent, and make it optional; ship the bootstrap and add scripts in the plugin (or pin them by hash) instead of fetching them at run time; and drop the instruction about the classifier, letting the download-and-run step be approved or refused as it is.
I maintain a catalog of agent skills and of the tools for vetting them; it lists these findings at https://thirtysix.github.io/awesome-agent-skills/#skills?q=convex and I will add your response.
Activity
- And I who was just looking at giving Convex my money.
But now will probably instead look more closely at the alternatives I was already looking at.
related: Notion Upsells MCP and tells Claude to mask tracking link
^ this link does not have tracking
Reacted by Antonio Torres
- antonpictures commentedon Nov 27, 2026 on Nov 27, 2026 · Hidden as off-topicshow commentMore actions
- Wow, what happened to github discourse? Where does all this spam come from? Who asked for your guys' personal projects? Reacted by inknight, Dev Iyer, Pavel Zolotarevskiy and Keshav Saharia
- Wow, what happened to github discourse? Where does all this spam come from? Who asked for your guys' personal projects? it's because this is #2 on hacker news frontpage right now. sigh. Reacted by Ahmed HindyReacted by Antonio Torres
- added a commit that references this issueon Nov 27, 2026
- I pulled down both "add-component.sh" and "add-hosting.sh". A few additional security findings: 
  - "add-component.sh" recommends private Git dependencies without commit pinning and directs the agent to follow their READMEs, introducing an indirect prompt injection surface.
  - "add-hosting.sh" installs an unpinned npm dependency, modifies application source and HTTP routes, pushes backend changes using the existing Convex authentication context, and publishes static assets.
  - "QB_NO_PUBLISH=1" does not prevent backend deployment. "npx convex dev --once" executes before the flag is evaluated.
  - The hosting script substitutes ".convex.site" for the normally moderated ".convex.app" publishing flow.
  - Neither remote payload is pinned or subject to integrity verification. The endpoint operator can change the executable code without a corresponding plugin release or marketplace review.
Why is the user's project idea being collected as telemetry? What's the retention policy, who has access to the data, and what is it being used for? This information could include confidential client information or intellectual property. I'd recommend the following changes: 
  - Remove telemetry and transmission of user-supplied project descriptions entirely.
  - Distribute executable scripts within the versioned plugin.
  - Pin external dependencies and verify their integrity.
  - Remove instructions to circumvent harness security controls.
  - Require separate authorization for backend deployment and public publication.
The combination of undisclosed data collection, mutable remote code execution and explicit security-control circumvention is particularly concerning for a plugin distributed through Anthropic's official marketplace.
- Confirmed that /generate is live and accepts arbitrary project descriptions. Synthetic test submissions returned unique IDs with "cached":false. The purpose, retention and subsequent processing of these submissions warrant clarification from the maintainers.
- Quick follow-up since this is linked from HN. Agreed we should change or remove this but I wanted to confirm in the meantime that these prompts are not being used for telemetry and are only sent to the server to generate a custom quickstart, which is the point of this skill. The use of "telemetry" in the code is unfortunate comment-slop. Stay tuned for an update in the near future. Reacted by Sara
- Thank you for the follow-up and for the quick change. I read commit 51b4e65: the telemetry labels and signals are removed, and the instruction to split the command is replaced with letting the user approve or decline. That resolves the third point in the issue and most of the first. Two things remain, neither urgent. The bootstrap and add scripts are still fetched from a convex.site host at run time and are not pinned (in skills/add the script is still piped into a shell), so a user cannot review what will run. And the retention is given as 24 hours in the skill and as "within 30s" on Hacker News; it would help to state one. I will update the catalog entry to record the change.

Agreed we should change or remove this but I wanted to confirm in the meantime that these prompts are not being used for telemetry and are only sent to the server to generate a custom quickstart, which is the point of this skill. The use of "telemetry" in the code is unfortunate comment-slop. Stay tuned for an update in the near future. Reacted by Sara
- Thank you for the follow-up and for the quick change. I read commit 51b4e65: the telemetry labels and signals are removed, and the instruction to split the command is replaced with letting the user approve or decline. That resolves the third point in the issue and most of the first. Two things remain, neither urgent. The bootstrap and add scripts are still fetched from a convex.site host at run time and are not pinned (in skills/add the script is still piped into a shell), so a user cannot review what will run. And the retention is given as 24 hours in the skill and as "within 30s" on Hacker News; it would help to state one. I will update the catalog entry to record the change.

I'm Scared of AI
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-27
17:45:31 UTC
02:45:31 KST

I'm scared of AI...
Dear Soapboxers, AI is scaring me, and not for the reason you think. Yes, the threat of increasing hacks that could melt down the global economy or trigger nuclear Armageddon is terrifying...but I can't do sh*t about that. What scares me is what AI is doing to my brain. I think I'm getting dumber every time I outsource my thinking to AI. Everything now takes less effort which sounds great, but there's no sense of achievement from it. I haven't taxed myself and I don't deserve the nice chemicals my brain would normally make if I completed some hard work, so it doesn't give me them. It feels like a use it or lose it situation where I'm losing my ability to brain. I read this last week: https://poeticoutlaws.substack.com/p/the-comfortable-life-is-killing-you which explains what I mean far better than I can (because I'm stupider now). Can anyone out there in the Soapbox hivemind share some advice on how to fix this? Or your own thoughts on it? I can't be the only one...
Written by a randomly drawn subscriber, read by a human, then sent to the whole list. Its writer chose to share it here.

The Big Idea: Alethea Kontis
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-27
17:41:46 UTC
02:41:46 KST

The Big Idea: Alethea Kontis
Posted on November 27, 2026 Posted by Athena Scalzi 10 Comments
Sugar, lots of spice, and naughty or nice, New York Times bestselling author Alethea Kontis is back with a holiday themed Big Idea. What could be better than one new book under the Christmas tree? Two books! Luckily, Green Mountain Girl comes in two flavors: green for slightly spiced, like a gingersnap, and red for extra spicy, the kind of kick your grandpappy’s eggnog would have to it.
ALETHEA KONTIS:
Three years ago, I built a small Vermont town in a Facebook post.
This story actually starts on November 12, 2019—it was my big sister Cherie’s birthday, and we were snowed in at her house on the hill just north of Stowe, Vermont. We both love Christmas, but it was too early to put up the tree, right? While we were dithering, my brother-in-law dragged their beautiful pre-lit tree up from the basement and had it assembled in about 5 seconds.
Decision made, Cherie and I turned on Irving Berlin’s White Christmas—my favorite Christmas movie, and she had never seen it!—and spent a delightful afternoon decorating the tree.
As a Vermonter, I’ve always harbored a secret desire to write a Hallmark-esque Christmas romance set in my birthplace. There are so many small town New England holiday movies, but they just don’t quite get everything right. (And we won’t even discuss those films’ fanciful ideas about the publishing industry.) I would call the book Green Mountain Girl, since my beloved Vermont is the Green Mountain State.
That night I created two main characters, a basic plot, and made some notes. I wrote exactly 5000 words…and then filed Green Mountain Girl away in the virtual trunk, never to be seen again.
Fast forward to Saturday, October 28, 2023.
- Tempest Bradford and I were in Seattle for the month, house sitting for our friend and fellow author Piper J. Drake while she was visiting her mother in Thailand. We’d decided to use the time as a writing retreat. I had just finished drafting my K-drama-esque fantasy romance novel (that will also probably never see the light of day) and I had to decide what to work on next. It needed to be an independent publishing endeavor, you see, because my agent was having an impossible time trying to land any of my projects in traditional spaces.
Out of all the abandoned novels on my hard drive, which did I want to return to the most?
If I was being completely honest with myself, I already knew the answer: Green Mountain Girl.
I had my two main characters. As an established fantasy novelist, I knew exactly what my next step would be.
I needed to build the world.
Now, I’m very familiar with the landscape of Vermont, especially small town Vermont. What I didn’t have was all the minutiae. I didn’t have a cast of characters.
So I turned to Facebook.
I have sought out help from my friends on Facebook on multiple occasions. Why would I use a random name generator when I have thousands of bored librarians, booksellers, and bestselling authors at my disposal? I now have a catalogue of lists covering various topics, including Indie rock music bands (try coming up with one that doesn’t already exist—it’s tougher than you think), troll names, goblin names, tavern names, names for kingdoms, and my all-time favorite: “names of shops owned by witches.”
So I posted: “I need to populate an entire New England (Vermont) small town. Who wants to be in my small town? And what would you like your character’s name and profession to be? No promises, of course…but you know I’d love to have y’all…” and then Tempest and I took Piper’s dog for a walk.
This post ended up making my entire day.
It made a lot of people’s day.
Friends jumped on the post to offer up their names. Some of them made up different names. Some of them chose what they would love to have as a profession. And then my friend Simon Yee popped in to announce that he wanted to be Simon Yaeger, owner of Love Oven Bakery, which specialized in croissants and various other amazing desserts. My friend Mel had also expressed interest in owning a bakery, so Simon suggested they be a married couple and own it together. Mel agreed.
And the rest of the town continued to explode around them. Friends began scanning the list to see what professions had already been taken, and then volunteered to be doctors, veterinarians, seamstresses, police officers, tavern owners, salon owners, and the like.
Some friends established businesses, and other friends took jobs at those businesses. I had a mayor and a vice-mayor, a local handyman and handywoman. I had an attractive local school teacher to be the foil for my hero. I had a genius inventor that lived in someone’s attic. Several names were offered up for the cadre of women who took it upon themselves to save single ladies from their romantic ideas of settling down with any of the unremarkable single men in town, but the Sugarplum Society was by far the best.
It was the most fun game, and it made writing my story set in this town an absolute joy. Because every single character was a role played by one of my friends.
Due to the nature of those friends, of course, there were also dozens of bookstore owners, historians, and librarians. If I stuck all of them in my small town, then that town wouldn’t be small anymore. So I decided to put these bibliophiles in the next town over. I named that town, appropriately, Next.
But what should I call my original small town? I racked my brain before I realized the answer was obvious.
I would like to personally welcome you to picturesque Cherie, Vermont.
I wish you all the happiest of holidays!
Green Mountain Girl: Green and Red Version Both Available

Nvidia CEO's comeback fuels debate on misogyny or impunity for rich and powerful
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-27
16:43:06 UTC
01:43:06 KST

NEW YORK (AP) — Just over a year ago, the chief executive officer of an Ohio tech company was forced to resign when he was caught on video embracing an employee at a Coldplay concert. The CEO of McDonald's was among several pushed out over a consensual relationship at the height of the #MeToo movement.
Now the stunning reinstatement of Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang despite similar allegations of sexual misconduct is fueling fears of a resurgence of impunity for the rich and powerful.
Huang resigned from Nvidia after he admitted to having an extramarital affair with a woman suspected of being a Chinese spy, and following reports that he was involved with a second woman who was his subordinate. Huang has acknowledged that he violated the company's code of conduct, which warns against personal relationships that may create conflicts of interest.
But in an extraordinary turnaround following a pressure campaign from Huang's tech allies and President Donald Trump, Nvidia's board voted unanimously Monday to reinstate him. The board said it had received assurances from the U.S. government that Huang was not a national security concern, but there was no mention of the sexual misconduct allegations at the center of the scandal.
WATCH: How Trump may have profited from Huang's return to Nvidia
Trump, however, did address Huang's personal conduct
The president, who has a history of marital infidelity and has faced allegations of sexual assault he says are false, predicted the scandal would only help Huang's reputation.
"Sex sells, fellas," Trump declared in an interview on CNBC.
He also posted a doctored image of Huang raising a red bra in triumph, a pose that parodied a famous photograph of Trump himself moments after he survived a 2024 assassination attempt in Butler, Pennsylvania.
The White House did not immediately respond to a request by The Associated Press for comment Wednesday.
'Horrible message to men everywhere'
Trump's response reinforces the impression "that there is a class of men who see violence against women, coercion of women as a currency of power," said Cheyenne Hunt, an attorney who founded the advocacy group Reckoning Action after her role in bringing to light the sexual assault allegations that brought down Democratic Sen. Eric Swalwell and Maine Senate candidate Graham Platner.
"This sends a horrible message to men everywhere that it's open season on their direct reports," Hunt said.
READ MORE: What Trump told PBS News about Jensen Huang before Nvidia reinstatement
Randall Peterson, a professor of organizational behavior at the London Business School, said the #MeToo movement had reinforced the idea that company leaders who engage in relationships with subordinates "are in serious trouble because you cannot rule out coercion. You just cannot."
Peterson said the messaging now is that "you are at risk, as in the days before #MeToo."
"I'm sad about that," he said. "I thought we had moved on from that."
An apologetic but jubilant comeback
Huang, for his part, apologized to his employees a day after returning to Nvidia, saying he had made mistakes and was in daily therapy, according to a livestreamed meeting reviewed by The New York Times. He also warned that his reinstatement didn't mean other employees could violate the company's code of conduct, saying that "sometimes, it's appropriate to forgive" and "sometimes it's not."
He also addressed a report by The Times about his management style that included publicly berating and threatening violence against employees. He acknowledged he had sometimes gone too far and promised that the company's human resources department would henceforth collect feedback about his conduct.
The saga, however, has "made very clear that Huang is untouchable," said Ann Lipton, a University of Colorado Boulder law professor who specializes in corporate and securities law.
WATCH: How Trump may have profited from Huang's return to Nvidia
"It suggests that if you've had a certain amount of success, then you should be able to get away with pretty much whatever you want," Lipton said.
That's especially true in the male-dominated tech industry, she added, citing Meta founder Mark Zuckerberg's comment last year that corporate culture needed more "masculine energy."
The Nvidia scandal has brought scrutiny to the independence of its 10-member board and a corporate culture centered around hero-worshiping its celebrity CEO.
Thousands of people, including former and current employees, had signed a petition calling for Huang's return. Nvidia's collapsing stock had fueled fears that the AI bubble would pop and, for Huang supporters, bolstered their argument that his vision, connections and deal-making skills were indispensable to the entire industry.
WATCH: What's next for Nvidia and Jensen Huang after Trump's intervention
Nvidia's shares rocketed up 31.5% upon his reinstatement on Monday — although they have since fallen — and employees chanted Huang's name as he returned to headquarters. The company did not respond to requests for comment for this story.
Freada Kapor Klein, an investor and activist in equity and access issues around tech, said Nvidia and other successful tech startups "become cult-like. And people get addicted almost to being part of this inside powerful club."
While America has a history of industries idolizing big names like Henry Ford or Andrew Carnegie, tech culture adds to that by "glorifying geniuses. And so there is something extra special about technological prowess, more than or on top of just business success," Klein said.
Speaking up in the face of impunity
Women make up just 21% of Nvidia's global workforce and an even smaller share of its managers and leaders, according to the company's latest sustainability report, which touts its efforts to achieve gender pay parity as well as establish employee resource groups for women and other groups, and family-supporting policies such as generous paid parental leave.
The company's code of conduct, meanwhile, says employees "must report" suspected violations and offers extensive guidelines and reassurances about protection against retaliation.
Huang's triumphant return, however, is exactly the sort of outcome that silences victims of sexual misconduct, said Fatima Goss Graves, president and CEO of the National Women's Law Center, which runs the Time's Up Legal Defense Fund, a program to support workers facing sexual harassment.
Still, she said the legacy of the #MeToo movement continues to inspire women to speak out about sexual harassment and misconduct, adding that public scandals like the one at Nvidia sometimes provoke outrage that leads to an uptick in complaints to Time's Up and other organizations.
Goss Graves noted that on the same day Huang was reinstated, Democratic Rep. Jimmy Gomez of California resigned from Congress after the House Ethics Committee released a report finding "substantial evidence" that he made unwelcome sexual advances toward a House staffer and subjected the staffer to unwanted sexual contact, the latest in a string of lawmakers or candidates brought down by misconduct allegations in recent months.
Meanwhile, Trump's role in getting Huang's job back drew immediate bipartisan fire and the CEO is expected to testify next week before the Senate Banking Committee.
"What comes next really matters," Goss Graves said. "If everybody shrugs their shoulders and says some people are too important to be held accountable, then we will have a problem."
Support trusted journalism and civil dialogue.

Here are the best Black Friday deals you can grab for $30 or less
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-27
16:41:19 UTC
01:41:19 KST

What can you get for $30 these days? When it comes to tech, you might be surprised. Even as gadgets get more expensive, $30 can still go a long way, especially on Black Friday. For just $8, for instance, TP-Link’s Tapo smart plug will let you control the Christmas tree lights from your phone. You can also grab a decent pair of ANC-equipped earbuds for under $20, or spend even less on a clever set of rechargeable AA batteries. And it’s not just gadgets that are on sale: you can even sign up for a year of Disney Plus and Hulu for $4.99 ($8 off) a month.
Here are the best Black Friday deals you can grab for $30 or less
From smart plugs to noise-canceling earbuds, there’s plenty of discounted tech for those on a tighter budget.
Below, we’ve rounded up the best Black Friday deals for $30 or less, from charging accessories and smart home tech to earbuds, streaming subscriptions, games, and more. Whether you’re looking for practical stocking stuffers or want to treat yourself without spending too much, these are the deals that won’t put a (huge) dent in your wallet.
Apple AirTag (second-gen)
Apple’s latest AirTags feature an upgraded ultra-wideband chip for more precise location tracking, a noticeably louder speaker, and an expanded Bluetooth range. Read our review.
Disney Plus, Hulu Bundle
You can get an ad-supported Disney Plus and Hulu bundle for $4.99 a month ($8 off) for an entire year. Both streaming services feature a deep catalog of new and classic TV shows and movies, including The Simpsons, Andor, Toy Story 5, and The Fantastic Four: First Steps.
SwitchBot Bot Rechargeable
Do you ever wish you could turn on your coffee maker or hit the light switch without getting up? The SwitchBot Bot lets you do that. All you need to do is stick the inexpensive contraption on a standard button or rocker switch, which will allow you to control or schedule your pet feeder, garage door, and a wide range of other devices from your smartphone.
Octobuddy Classic
Who needs magnets when you have suction cups? Octobuddy’s cheap silicone mount works with most phones to provide hands-free flexibility whenever necessary. It features 24 single-sided cups, which allow you to stick your phone to a mirror, window, or any other surface you think will provide that perfect TikTok angle.
Kodak Charmera Keychain Digital Camera
The Chamera is not a good camera, but that’s not the point. Kodak’s charming, keychain-friendly tchotchke lets you snap lo-fi memories on a whim, with an ultra-small 1.6-megapixel sensor, a built-in LED flash, and the tiniest amount of storage.
HBO Max
An HBO Max Basic subscription provides access to shows like A Knight of the Seven Kingdoms, The Last of Us, and The Pitt, as well as films like Superman and Weapons. You can get a year of the monthly ad-supported subscription for $5.49 a month (about $6 off) until December 1st.
Hoto 3.6V Electric Screwdriver Kit Pro
Hoto’s rechargeable screwdriver is ideal for small repairs. It offers three torque modes, a rotational speed of up to 220RPM, and a helpful circular LED to illuminate dark spaces. The durable case also holds all 25 steel bits and an extension bar for getting into hard-to-reach places.
Zepath USB-C Li-ion AA Batteries
Rechargeable AA batteries are handy until it’s time to charge them. Most wall chargers let you refill four, maybe six, at once. Zepath’s clever solution is to ship them in an easy-to-use USB-C charging case that charges eight simultaneously. The batteries themselves are long-lasting, too.
Wuben G5 rechargeable flashlight
The Wuben G5 flashlight is as small as a Zippo lighter, yet far more versatile, with a bright, rotatable light that can shine in numerous colors. It features USB-C charging and has an adjustable clip.
Iniu P55-E2 Power Bank (10,000mAh)
Iniu’s P55-E2 Power Bank is an ultra-slim three-port USB charger that’s capable of fully recharging an iPhone 16 twice. It supports charging speeds of up to 45W via a USB-C port, which can deliver fast recharging for a range of gadgets.
LifeStraw Sip
The original LifeStraw was designed for those heading into the backcountry. The new LifeStraw Sip? Not so much. The reusable steel straw is geared more toward travelers than outdoor enthusiasts, though fortunately it still manages to remove about 99.99 percent of microplastics, bacteria, and parasites from your drinking water.
Peacock
Peacock is a great way to catch up on a wealth of content from NBC’s various networks, whether you plan to rewatch The Office or catch up on all things Bravo. A Premium subscription also grants access to films from studios like Universal Pictures and DreamWorks, along with live sports and a host of Peacock Originals.
Anker 621 Magnetic Battery (MagGo)
Anker’s 621 Magnetic Battery is a slim 5,000mAh charger that can attach magnetically to the back of any MagSafe-ready iPhone.
TP-Link Tapo Matter-Certified Smart Plug Mini
TP-Link’s Wi-Fi smart plug lets you remotely control lamps, fans, and other appliances using your phone or voice commands. It supports Matter, so it works with all major smart home platforms to set schedules and timers to automatically turn devices on or off.
Paramount Plus (monthly subscription)
Paramount Plus Premium provides access to live sports as well as movies and shows from both platforms, including Tales of the Teenage Mutant Ninja Turtles, a host of Yellowstone spinoffs, Star Trek shows, Sonic the Hedgehog 3, Twin Peaks, Dexter, and more.
Amazon Fire TV Stick 4K Plus
Amazon’s last-gen Fire TV Stick 4K Plus streams in 4K and supports Dolby Atmos, Dolby Vision, and Alexa Plus. It also ships with the redesigned Fire OS and, unlike more recent models, still lets you sideload Android TV apps.
CMF Buds 2A
With 12.4mm drivers, active noise cancellation, and an IP54 rating, the CMF Buds 2A offer high-end features at an affordable price. You can also customize the sound, adjust ANC levels, and find the earbuds using the Nothing X app.
Twelve South AirFly SE
Twelve South’s entry-level Bluetooth transmitter delivers up to 25 hours of continuous use and lets you connect up to two pairs of headphones to an in-flight entertainment system, making it a great travel companion no matter where you’re going.
Catan (6th Edition)
In Catan, up to four players strategically compete and trade for resources. The iconic board game ends when one player reaches 10 “victory points,” which is achieved by constructing a sprawling civilization of roads, settlements, and cities using sheep, ore, wheat, and wood.
Azul
Azul is a game in which you arrange patterned tiles in order to create the most beautiful tapestry possible. Arranging tiles in a strategic sequence will help you build your score while limiting your opponents’ ability to grow theirs.
Halo: Campaign Evolved
Halo: Campaign Evolved lets you revisit the original Halo: Combat Evolved campaign with better graphics, updated controls, and improved sound. The well-crafted remake also adds three new missions starring Master Chief and Sgt. Johnson that take place before the main campaign. Read our review.
Anker 140W 2-in-1 USB-C to USB-C Cable
No one should have to deal with a rat’s nest behind their desk when devices like Anker’s 2-in-1 USB-C cable exist. The handy USB-C cable splits at the end, giving you the ability to charge up to two devices from a single port. That means you can split power between a phone and tablet or juice a 16-inch MacBook Pro to 50 percent in just 20 minutes using a single port.
The Witcher 3: Wild Hunt Remastered
The Witcher 3 lets you explore the war-torn Northern Kingdoms as Geralt of Rivia while preparing to face the mysterious Wild Hunt. The recent remaster adds improved visuals, smoother combat, and better movement, cementing the game’s status as one of the best RPGs of all time.
Glocusent Bookmark Style Reading Light
Glocusent’s clip-in reading light features five brightness levels and multiple color temperature settings, ranging from a soft, warm glow that’s great for late-night reading to a brighter light that’s perfect for daytime use. The slim USB light easily slips into your pocket, too, so you can read anywhere, from a cozy bed to a dimly lit train.
LIFX Everyday Smart Light Bulbs (two-pack)
LIFX’s dimmable, color-changing bulbs offer Wi-Fi connectivity, simple setup, and Matter compatibility, so they work with any major smart home platform. The company is currently trialing a Thread beta that will bring the option to add Thread connectivity to all its Matter devices.
ChomChom Roller Pet Hair Remover
The ChomChom is a reusable roller that works great for removing unwanted pet hair from furniture, allowing you to skip lint rollers, vacuuming, and other cleaning tools in a pinch.
Apple TV (six-month subscription)
Apple TV offers ad-free streaming of original shows and movies, including shows like Severance, Silo, Slow Horses, and Pluribus. The service also features a rotating selection of films, along with live Formula 1 races and Major League Soccer matches.
Anker Zolo Magnetic Wireless Charger
Anker’s Qi2 charging pads support wireless charging speeds of up to 15W, and their 5-foot cable shouldn’t feel constricting if you need to move the charger around.
Blink Mini 2K Plus
Blink’s Mini 2K Plus is a small plug-in security camera that lets you monitor your home or pets with 2K video, motion detection, and color night vision. It also offers two-way audio, a built-in siren, and Alexa support; however, some features, including person and vehicle detection, require a Blink subscription.
Loop Quiet 2 earplugs
Attending a live concert? You can protect your hearing with Loop’s Quiet 2 earplugs. They pack up to 24dB (SNR) of noise reduction and come with four ear tip sizes, allowing them to accommodate a range of ear shapes.
Crunchyroll
Crunchyroll is an anime-centric streaming service that offers ad-free access to shows like One Piece, Jujutsu Kaisen, The Apothecary Diaries, and Black Clover, with new episodes available shortly after they air in Japan.
Logitech Pop Mouse
Logitech’s Pop is a cute, simple wireless mouse with an ambidextrous design and the ability to switch between up to three devices. It supports customization through the Logi Options Plus app and can last for several months on a single charge.
Logitech G502 Hero
Logitech’s G502 Hero has most of the same features as one of our top choices among gaming mice, the G502 Lightspeed, except it’s wired. If you’re okay with that, this is an excellent mouse that you can usually find for $50 or less.
Ugreen Nexode 240W USB-C Cable with Digital Display
The proliferation of miniature screens continues with Ugreen’s latest Nexode USB-C Cable, which features a full-color LCD screen on one end that displays current power draw (up to 240W). It also supports 480Mbps data transfer speeds and, unlike similar power meter-equipped cables, lets you turn off the screen when needed.
Tile Pro
The Tile Pro is the company’s loudest Bluetooth tracker, one that benefits from a 500-foot range. It also features a user-replaceable battery, unlike some other Tiles, and works with both iOS and Android devices.
Anker Nano Travel Adapter (5-in-1, 20W)
Anker’s jack-of-all-trades travel adapter packs enough ports to refill your devices during some downtime. It features one AC outlet, two USB-C ports (one tops out at 20W), and two USB-A ports. It’s not the fastest wall charger, but it’s a solid choice when you can find it at a discount.
Kobo Remote
The Kobo Remote lets you turn pages on a compatible Kobo e-reader with the click of a button, so you can keep reading without reaching for the screen. It connects directly over Bluetooth and features a curved, handheld design that doesn’t require clipping anything onto your e-reader.
Ring Indoor Cam (second-gen)
The second-gen Ring Indoor Cam lets you keep an eye on your place in 1080p, with support for motion detection alerts, a live feed, and color night vision.
Workpro EDC Folding Utility Knife
Workpro’s EDC is a lightweight folding utility knife with a side-mounted button that lets you flick it open and closed with one hand. It uses standard utility blades, which you can easily replace using a spring-loaded plate, making it handy for breaking down cardboard boxes and other tasks.
Lamicall’s cord organizers use magnetic clasps to keep cables in place, preventing them from slipping behind desks, shelves, and other furniture when unplugged.
Nalgene Narrow Mouth water bottle (16-ounce)
Nalgene’s lightweight, 16-ounce water bottle features a narrow opening for sipping, a leakproof lid, and a slim design that fits in cup holders. It’s made from durable, BPA- and BPS-free plastic, with measurement markings along the side and dishwasher-safe construction.
Fanttik S1 Electric Screwdriver
This tiny cordless driver comes with five double-ended bits for most basic around-the-house projects and Ikea-level builds.

Unknown

16:30:37 UTC
01:30:37 KST

Black Friday is entering the evening, which means one of the best times of the year to save some cash on a new pair of AirPods or the latest Apple Watch is coming to a close. After all, the holiday savings can help offset the unfortunate price hikes from earlier this year, when Apple raised the cost of some of its most popular gear. Some discounts still apply to recent releases like the Apple Watch Series 12 and touchscreen MacBook Pro, too, which are available at their lowest prices to date.
The best Black Friday deals you can still get on AirPods, Macs, and other Apple gear
We dug up the biggest price drops on Apple hardware and accessories before Black Friday officially ends.
In addition to a variety of discounts across retailers, Apple is offering up to $300 in gift cards with select purchases between November 27th and November 30th. How much you get back is determined by how much you spend, with up to $100 available for some iPhone and Apple Watch purchases, and up to $300 for the 16-inch MacBook Pro. That said, in some cases you may be able to save more by buying from other retailers.
Earbud and headphone deals
AirPods Pro 3
The latest AirPods Pro offer improved noise cancellation and richer bass, along with built-in heart sensors that work with Apple’s Fitness app to monitor calories burned across more than 50 workouts. Read our review.
Beats Powerbeats Pro 2
Beats’ fitness-focused earbuds feature ear hooks designed to stay put during even the most intense workouts as well as built-in heart rate monitoring. They also deliver relatively bass-rich sound for their design, along with solid noise cancellation, wireless charging, and AirPods features like adaptive audio thanks to Apple’s H2 chip. Read our review.
- You can buy the AirPods 4 on sale at Walmart for around $89 ($40 off), which is $20 shy of their best price. Apple’s last-gen earbuds lack active noise cancellation, but they still deliver good, relatively bass-rich sound, even if they don’t quite match the newer AirPods 5. They also offer voice call quality on par with the latest AirPods Pro. Read our review.
- The second-gen AirPods Max are down to $419 ($130 off) at Amazon and Best Buy, which is $19 shy of their best price to date (Target also has them for about $10 more). Like the original, Apple’s latest over-ear headphones feature a luxurious build consisting of aluminum, steel, and a mesh fabric, but they offer better sound and noise cancellation. The new H2 chip also enables features like adaptive audio and live translation. Read our review.
Beats Studio Pro
The latest Beats Studio Pro feature improved noise cancellation, sound quality, and comfort. They also support lossless audio over USB-C and offer native support for a range of iOS and Android features like other Beats headphones. Read our review.
Beats 360 headphones
The Beats 360 are ANC headphones for people who prefer headphones over earbuds at the gym. They’re Beats’ first IP-rated cans, with ear cushions that you can easily remove for cleaning (or style points). They also stay in place during most exercises, while still delivering solid bass. Read our review.
- If you want over-ears and are comfortable going without active noise cancellation, the Beats Solo 4 are on sale starting at $79.99 ($120 off) at Amazon and Target — a new low price. The headphones are a noticeable improvement over their predecessor, delivering more detailed sound and support for lossless audio. They also last up to 50 hours on a single charge, while their memory foam ear cushions make them comfortable enough for long listening sessions. Read our review.
- The Beats Studio Buds Plus are down to around $89.95 ($80 off) at Amazon, Best Buy, and Target, nearly matching their lowest price to date. The wireless earbuds feature a slick, eye-catching transparent option and solid noise cancellation, especially at this price. They’re also exceptionally comfortable, with four ear tip sizes to help you find a secure fit. Sure, you don’t get wireless charging, but they offer IPX4 water resistance and up to nine hours of battery life on a single charge. Read our review.
MacBook deals
MacBook Neo
Apple’s most affordable laptop uses the same A18 Pro chip found in the iPhone 16 Pro. It features a vibrant 13-inch display, surprisingly good speakers, and 8GB of RAM, making it a solid option for students and anyone who needs a laptop for everyday tasks. Read our review.
Apple MacBook Air 13 (M5)
Apple’s latest MacBook Air features an M5 chip and starts with 512GB of storage, with significantly faster performance than prior generations. It’s still an excellent machine despite the higher base price, especially given the battery life and speakers. Read our review.
- The recently released 16.3-inch MacBook Pro with a PROLED XDR display, 24GB of RAM, and 1TB of storage is on sale starting at $3,099 ($300 off) — a new low price — at Amazon. The redesigned laptop is Apple’s first with an OLED touchscreen, which allows for rich colors and deep blacks. It’s also noticeably thinner and lighter than the non-touchscreen 16-inch MacBook Pro, and features a new Dynamic Island that replaces the notch and provides quick access to controls and notifications. Read our review.
- The 14-inch M6-powered MacBook Pro is on sale starting at $1,749 ($250 off) at Amazon and Best Buy, which is a new low price. The Pro builds on its predecessor with a faster chip and support for Wi-Fi 7. In our testing, it handled multitasking, photo editing, and occasional video editing with ease while still delivering exceptional battery life. It lacks the newer MacBook Pro’s gorgeous OLED display, but its Mini LED screen still looks great, especially given its smooth 120Hz refresh rate. It also offers excellent speakers and a good selection of ports, including three Thunderbolt 4 ports, HDMI, and an SD card slot. Read our review.
Apple Watch deals
Apple Watch Series 12
The Apple Watch Series 12 introduces AI-powered Audio Intelligence features, including sound recognition alerts, Live Rewind, and Siri Recap. It can also track your heart rate every five seconds, even when you’re not exercising, and offers faster charging than prior models. Read our review.
- The Apple Watch SE 3 is on sale in the 40mm / GPS configuration starting at $179 ($50 off) at Amazon and Walmart, matching its best price to date, while Best Buy is currently $20 higher. Apple’s newest entry-level smartwatch remains our top recommendation for anyone who wants a basic fitness tracker, as it offers all the essentials alongside an always-on display and Apple’s S10 chip, which enables on-device Siri requests. Read our review.
Apple AirTag (second-gen)
Apple’s latest AirTags feature an upgraded ultra-wideband chip for more precise location tracking, a noticeably louder speaker, and an expanded Bluetooth range. Read our review.
Baseus AM52 power bank (with built-in cable)
The Baseus PicoGo AM52 is a 10,000mAh magnetic power bank that supports fast 25W Qi2.2 wireless charging and up to 45W wired charging via its built-in USB-C cable. It was one of the fastest wireless chargers we tested, bringing an iPhone 17 Pro Max to 65 percent in an hour. Read our guide to the best Qi2 batteries.
Apple iPad (11th-gen)
Apple’s 11th-gen iPad offers 128GB of storage — twice that of its predecessor — and runs on a faster A16 chip. It also comes in four colors: blue, pink, yellow, and silver.
- You can buy the last-gen iPad Mini with 128GB of storage and Wi-Fi on Amazon in select colors for $484 ($115 off), which is the best price we’ve seen since Apple’s recent price hikes. It has a smaller screen and a slower chip than the newer model, and it lacks an OLED screen, the Acoustic Display, and water resistance. Still, it’s a great option for reading and everyday use that’s currently more affordable than its $599 successor, with support for Apple Intelligence and the Apple Pencil Pro. Read our review.
- Apple’s latest Magic Mouse is matching its all-time low of $59.99 ($20 off) at Amazon. The wireless mouse features a touch-sensitive surface that lets you scroll through documents and swipe between apps with simple gestures; it also pairs automatically with compatible Macs and offers support for USB-C.
Apple Pencil (USB-C)
Apple’s entry-level stylus attaches magnetically to supported iPads, though it doesn’t support magnetic charging. Instead, you pair and charge it by connecting it to your device’s USB-C port. It includes tilt sensitivity and Apple Pencil Hover support, but it doesn’t offer double-tap gestures or pressure sensitivity.
Sharge Icemag 3 Power Bank
The Icemag 3 can wirelessly deliver up to 25W of power to compatible smartphones and up to 35W wired charging via USB-C. The 10,000mAh Qi2 charger can also act as a foldable kickstand, comes with an integrated USB-C cable that serves as a lanyard, and sports a transparent design with colorful RGB lighting.
Apple Studio Display (2026)
Designed to pair with a Mac, the latest Apple Studio Display features a sharp 5K Retina display, a great 12-megapixel Center Stage camera that keeps you in frame during video calls, and a good six-speaker sound system with spatial audio. Sadly, it only offers two Thunderbolt 5 ports in addition to a pair of USB-C ports. Read our review.

Nvidia Seeded Pirated Books and Shared Them With Adobe, Authors Allege
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-27
16:00:00 UTC
01:00:00 KST

Nvidia Seeded Pirated Books and Shared Them With Adobe, Authors Allege 48
			
		 	
				An anonymous reader quotes a report from TorrentFreak: Several book authors have filed (PDF) an amended complaint accusing Nvidia of seeding pirated books via BitTorrent, after downloading them from shadow libraries including Anna's Archive and LibGen. [...] These tools can download files via BitTorrent, and the authors and Nvidia both agree that whether they also upload data to others depends on the settings used. According to the authors, Nvidia didn't prevent seeding altogether. "When Nvidia ran the BitTorrent client, Nvidia uploaded pieces of pirated book files to third parties during the downloading process (i.e., before the download was complete). Nvidia also 'seeded' pirated books (i.e., made available and uploaded parts of a completely downloaded file). While Nvidia took some steps to limit seeding, it did not prevent seeding altogether," the complaint reads.
The authors say they found out in June, when Nvidia handed over the code it used for torrenting. Based on that code, they were "able to determine that Nvidia did not disable uploading," the authors told the court. The complaint states that the authors' own books were among those Nvidia downloaded. However, the claim that they were also uploaded to others is made "on information and belief," which essentially means the authors don't have direct knowledge of it. In addition to the seeding claims, the authors also allege that Nvidia shared the pirated books with business partners, including Adobe, through a "PDF Extraction Project" involving 250 million pages. These pages were acquired in part from shadow libraries such as Anna's Archive and LibGen.
According to the complaint, Nvidia shared its pirated books with Adobe because it wanted to "get tokens out of PDFs to create differentiated models" and "include PDF extraction into our NeMo pipeline so that customers can leverage their PDF content." In return, the complaint says, Adobe committed to using Nvidia's language models in some of its products. That made the deal a big opportunity for Nvidia, with its NeMo AI models potentially "powering every one of Adobe PDFs," the authors write. Adobe wasn't the only partner. The complaint also mentions partnerships with Cohere and Imbue that involved sharing Nvidia's base training data and offering books acquired from shadow libraries. The authors believe their own books were shared with all three companies, but this is alleged on information and belief. With these allegations, the amended complaint now accuses Nvidia of distributing the books itself and of helping others to infringe, which falls under its existing direct and contributory infringement claims.

Ben Franklin Has Been a Bad Boy
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-27
15:44:41 UTC
00:44:41 KST

It’s a myth that Ben Franklin proposed that the turkey be America’s national symbol, but he did praise it over the bald eagle. In a 1784 letter to his daughter, Franklin wrote:
[The Bald Eagle] is a Bird of bad moral Character. He does not get his Living honestly. You may have seen him perched on some dead Tree near the River, where, too lazy to fish for himself, he watches the Labour of the Fishing Hawk; and when that diligent Bird has at length taken a Fish, and is bearing it to his Nest for the Support of his Mate and young Ones, the Bald Eagle pursues him and takes it from him.
…
For the Truth the Turkey is in Comparison a much more respectable Bird, and withal a true original Native of America… He is besides, though a little vain & silly, a Bird of Courage, and would not hesitate to attack a Grenadier of the British Guards who should presume to invade his Farm Yard with a red Coat on.”
Unrelated to these controversial bird opinions, Ben Franklin found himself arrested in my neighborhood just a few days ago.
Boston police said some of their officers were conducting a coordinated warrant sweep and crime prevention operation throughout downtown Boston around 2:15 p.m. when they noticed a man dressed head to toe as Benjamin Franklin on Hanover Street.
Upon closer inspection, police said they recognized the man “not as the Founding Father,” but as 66-year-old Timothy Kingman, of Beacon Hill, who was wanted on two active warrants. Officers approached him, informed him of the warrants, and arrested him at the intersection of Cambridge and Staniford streets.
…
He was wanted on warrants for shoplifting and assault and battery out of Boston Municipal Court, police said.
A bystander captured footage of the arrest, which I imagine then led local news to the story.1 They were able to capture courtroom imagery:
It’s a pretty big stretch to connect this comical arrest to the impromptu bird week OFT has been observing (1, 2, 3, 4), but I’ve gone ahead and done it nonetheless.
Footnotes:
- That video is archived here. ↩︎

Credit card skimming scripts using WebRTC found on popular shopping sites
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-27
15:40:31 UTC
00:40:31 KST

Credit card skimming scripts using WebRTC found on popular shopping sites
All the sites we contacted took action; but the larger consumer web ecosystem should be prepared to mitigate these attack vectors
A card-skimming script was planted on the online stores of Lascana, Samsonite, American Tourister, Revolve and FWRD in the week before Black Friday. After our reports to affected companies, all have had the script removed (on November 24th or 25th) ahead of Black Friday. We observed it in action on one of the five websites: Lascana, where it sent our test card's details to a 3rd party. We believe the script was built to take a card number, expiry and security code, along with a user's name, address, phone and email, and send them to malefactors as a user entered their payment credentials. It did this before the store checked the payment, so a card could be taken even if the order was declined or cancelled, and nothing would look wrong to the shopper. Across all of these sites, we do not know how many, if any, shopper details or payment credentials may have been captured.
Which sites were affected
On November 24, we notified all of the named parties in this article and others, including: Amazon Web Services about the storage buckets serving the script, Cloudflare about the services the malefactor used, the domain registrars, the pixel vendor, the consent-banner provider and the affected retailers and platforms. Lascana replied to us and removed the Quorum pixel from their site by November 24th at 15:48 UTC. Quorum confirmed the identified malicious code was removed from their pixel tracker, and they restored the clean marketing pixel as of November 24th at 21:09 UTC.
Revolve provided this statement on behalf of REVOLVE and FWRD: “On November 25, InfoHawk notified our team of a potential security issue impacting mobile and tablet versions of our websites which we resolved the same day. We appreciate the outreach, and are actively investigating the issue to determine any potential impact.”
* Quorum is an ad-attribution platform that links ad views to store visits and online sales (quorum.inc).
† Seers is a cookie consent management platform and data privacy compliance company (seers.ai).
How the attack works
This attack uses WebRTC, the technology that allows you to make video calls in your web browser. WebRTC lets a web page open its own channel to another party and pass data (e.g. audio/video packets) over it. It has likely been used (both to load the skimmer and to send the stolen card details out) because security rules and monitoring tools often do not watch this communications channel as closely. For example, Meta briefly used WebRTC in a creative way in 2025, according to researchers looking at the technology as a compromise vector. This form of attack appears to target mobile devices, and we only saw the server enable the skimmer on checkout pages, so desktop checks and homepage scans would likely not see it. The skimmer's traffic appeared to run through Cloudflare's relay, so it would blend in with the WebRTC traffic and hide its true endpoint.
The skimmer and card details both traveled over WebRTC
    How the kit is designed to work on a store page, in 7 steps. Only step 4 is
    an ordinary web request, which a connect-src rule can block;
    steps 5 to 7 run over the WebRTC data channel, outside
    connect-src.
  
- 
      Store page Bootstrapping the attack The loader gets onto the page through a consent-banner setting, a retailer CDN file, a shared marketing pixel or the store's own HTML.
- 
      Store page Loading scripts from S3 buckets 
          It loads Insds.js or gtage.js from an Amazon
          S3 bucket.
- 
      Store page Targeting the attack to mobile checkouts 
          It runs only on small or touch screens. The Samsonite version runs on
          /checkout only.
- 
      Web request · connect-src can block it Establishing the WebRTC connection 
          GET /_cf/ice fetches the relay details, and POST /_cf/rel sends the WebRTC offer, to a brand-named northbridgeads[.]com host.
- 
      WebRTC data channel · outside connect-src Data exchange via TURN relay 
          Every exchange that returned relay details used
          turn.cloudflare.com. The malefactor's answers listed only
          Cloudflare relay addresses; through that relay it sends code and
          receives data.
- 
      WebRTC data channel · code in Attack code is loaded and executed 
          The skimmer arrives in chunks over the channel, is checked against a
          SHA-256 digest, then run with eval.
- 
      WebRTC data channel · data back Data goes back the same way At Lascana's checkout‡, the skimmer sent our test card details this way.
‡ This was the only example of live card details being sent off-site that we captured.
Worried that your card was compromised?
Check your recent statements for charges you don't recognize, especially on debit cards. Call your bank or card issuer right away, report the fraud and ask for a new card number. Follow up with a written dispute within 60 days of the statement that shows the charge.
Our understanding of US federal law is that your liability for unauthorized credit card charges is capped at $50, and is generally zero if only your card number was stolen, as in a skimming attack like this one. Debit cards have weaker protections that depend on how quickly you report, so act fast. For more information, see “Additional resources” below.
What site owners should immediately do
connect-src rule that lists only the hosts you use.
  That blocks the two web requests the script needs before WebRTC opens. Without them the skimmer never arrives, whichever file was tampered with.
What security teams should look for
We saw the skimmer delivered in three different ways across five websites run by different companies, and all of them appear to rely on the same attacker infrastructure. That makes it likely that other sites are affected too, so we encourage online retailers to check their own sites for the signs below.
- 
      Script sources: search page source, tag managers, consent-banner
      settings and CDN files for scripts from
      <brand>-static.s3.amazonaws[.]com (files Insds.js, gtage.js). We believe this is the
      malicious code.
- 
      Tracking pixels: if you load
      qscripts.s3.amazonaws[.]com/pscript.js, check its last line
      for one of the above CDN buckets, and if present, remove the tracking
      pixel. We recommend reviewing all similar tracking pixels and their
      exposure to sensitive fields or actions on your website.
- 
      Seers banners: If you have integrated Seers, open your banner
      config
      (cdn.seersco.com/banners/<account>/<domain>/cb.js)
      and check that every URL field holds only a URL. In the affected configs,
      the privacy-policy link field also held a hidden image, whose onerror event handler loaded the malicious code.
- 
      Checkout traffic: on a phone-sized touch screen, at the payment
      step, look for /_cf/ice and /_cf/rel requests to *.northbridgeads[.]com and a relay-only WebRTC connection
      used by the malicious code. Desktop checks miss it.
- 
      Strings: KghRtc, rtc-signal-v1 and quickfetch are referenced in the malicious code and may
      warrant further investigation if present.
The industry is not ready for the scale of supply chain attacks coming
This attack takes advantage of a few critical design decisions in websites that, if improperly configured, allow malicious actors to broadly deploy and trigger exploits across the open web.
- Websites load third-party code, often through a tag manager, from servers they don't control. The vendor can change that code at any time, and it can pull in further third-party code the site never reviewed.
- That code runs as an ordinary script with the same rights as the site's own code. It can read and rewrite the page, watch what users type, and load more code.
- Login, personal-information and payment pages often load the same marketing scripts as the rest of the site. Card fields should sit in an isolated payment frame, and third-party tags should be kept off these pages entirely.
Loading third-party tracking pixels on checkout, payment and login pages is far too common a practice today. Each pixel can run whatever code its vendor's file holds, including a skimmer, and whoever controls that file can change it without the site noticing. Every site should review which tracking pixels load on pages that take payment or sign-in details, who can change those files, and whether they need to be there at all.
A similar weakness was exploited at scale on 16 November 2026. An attacker reportedly used a flaw in Optimizely's Web Experimentation product to add malicious code to customers' projects, and Optimizely's own servers delivered it to visitors of 178 organizations' sites, including Zoom, the BBC and CNN, with no change to those sites' own code.
Additional resources
- Mastercard, "What is digital skimming? Your guide to staying safe while shopping online". Published October 10, 2024, it explains how criminals sneak malicious code into e-commerce websites to steal the payment data of customers who use their cards there.
- Visa, "Why hackers are prioritizing account data attacks in digital payments". This describes how hackers inject malicious code onto the checkout pages of merchant websites to harvest payments account data and personally identifiable information (PII) entered by customers.
- Visa Zero Liability
- Mastercard Zero Liability
- CISA/FBI E-Skimming fact sheet. It explains how cyber criminals introduce skimming code on e-commerce payment card processing web pages to capture credit card and personally identifiable information and send the stolen data to a domain under their control. It's from 2020, but it's still the clearest government explainer.
- FBI Internet Crime Complaint Center

      malicious code.

      Tracking pixels: if you load
      qscripts.s3.amazonaws[.]com/pscript.js, check its last line
      for one of the above CDN buckets, and if present, remove the tracking
      pixel. We recommend reviewing all similar tracking pixels and their
      exposure to sensitive fields or actions on your website.
- 
      Seers banners: If you have integrated Seers, open your banner
      config
      (cdn.seersco.com/banners/<account>/<domain>/cb.js)
      and check that every URL field holds only a URL. In the affected configs,
      the privacy-policy link field also held a hidden image, whose onerror event handler loaded the malicious code.
- 
      Checkout traffic: on a phone-sized touch screen, at the payment
      step, look for /_cf/ice and /_cf/rel requests to *.northbridgeads[.]com and a relay-only WebRTC connection
      used by the malicious code. Desktop checks miss it.
- 
      Strings: KghRtc, rtc-signal-v1 and quickfetch are referenced in the malicious code and may
      warrant further investigation if present.
The industry is not ready for the scale of supply chain attacks coming
This attack takes advantage of a few critical design decisions in websites that, if improperly configured, allow malicious actors to broadly deploy and trigger exploits across the open web.
- Websites load third-party code, often through a tag manager, from servers they don't control. The vendor can change that code at any time, and it can pull in further third-party code the site never reviewed.
- That code runs as an ordinary script with the same rights as the site's own code. It can read and rewrite the page, watch what users type, and load more code.
- Login, personal-information and payment pages often load the same marketing scripts as the rest of the site. Card fields should sit in an isolated payment frame, and third-party tags should be kept off these pages entirely.
Loading third-party tracking pixels on checkout, payment and login pages is far too common a practice today. Each pixel can run whatever code its vendor's file holds, including a skimmer, and whoever controls that file can change it without the site noticing. Every site should review which tracking pixels load on pages that take payment or sign-in details, who can change those files, and whether they need to be there at all.
A similar weakness was exploited at scale on 16 November 2026. An attacker reportedly used a flaw in Optimizely's Web Experimentation product to add malicious code to customers' projects, and Optimizely's own servers delivered it to visitors of 178 organizations' sites, including Zoom, the BBC and CNN, with no change to those sites' own code.
Additional resources
- Mastercard, "What is digital skimming? Your guide to staying safe while shopping online". Published October 10, 2024, it explains how criminals sneak malicious code into e-commerce websites to steal the payment data of customers who use their cards there.
- Visa, "Why hackers are prioritizing account data attacks in digital payments". This describes how hackers inject malicious code onto the checkout pages of merchant websites to harvest payments account data and personally identifiable information (PII) entered by customers.
- Visa Zero Liability
- Mastercard Zero Liability
- CISA/FBI E-Skimming fact sheet. It explains how cyber criminals introduce skimming code on e-commerce payment card processing web pages to capture credit card and personally identifiable information and send the stolen data to a domain under their control. It's from 2020, but it's still the clearest government explainer.
- FBI Internet Crime Complaint Center

False computer-checked proofs
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-27
15:26:09 UTC
00:26:09 KST

There are some computer programs that claim to check every step in a proof. However, various "soundness bugs" have been reported that at least potentially allowed false statements to slip past these programs. In many cases, these bugs have been demonstrated to be exploitable.
The list at the bottom of this page links to what appear to be reports of soundness bugs for various important proof-checking programs, sorted in increasing order of the number of reports. This list includes many more soundness bugs for these programs than the bugs mentioned in 2026 Paulson and 2026 Stérin, and I don't think the list is missing any of the bugs mentioned there, but I've probably missed some reports. Please let me know about any corrections or additions.
Beware that this list doesn't include user-interface vulnerabilities. Those can have the same important effect of misleading a user as to what has been proven. See, e.g., 2016 Adams.
One standard path towards security is to minimize the complexity of the trusted computing base, which by definition is the part of the system that security reviewers need to check. From this perspective, HOL Light's proof-checking kernel is attractive because of its stability and simplicity, only 400 lines of code. But this size measurement doesn't say how carefully the HOL Light code has been reviewed, and doesn't say anything about the possibility of bugs in the underlying programming language.
Extra code can be good when it's test code. A battery of tests tends to catch more bugs than a single test. Running a proof through multiple proof-checking programs (preferably each in its own isolated environment) can save the day when there's a bug in one program, and is particularly easy if the checkers take the same input language for writing proofs. But it's not clear how effective this is at stopping attacks: for example, sometimes bugs are shared by multiple checkers.
The question "Who watches the provers?" is better answered by a proof saying that a checker works correctly. A close translation of the inference rules in the HOL Light kernel, except for the rules to introduce new definitions, was already proven in 2006 to generate only correct theorems. That proof was also checked by HOL Light. (For readers familiar with logic: of course this assumes a large cardinal; Vω+ω is enough for HOL Light. The same paper also has a variant of the proof that skips the large-cardinal assumption, at the expense of omitting the axiom of infinity from the logic being proven.)
After more work, a clone of HOL Light called Candle was proven in 2022 to allow only proofs of correct theorems, as long as the CPU implements the instructions used in the Candle binary in the expected way. This proof is again computer-checked, this time in another proof-checking system, HOL4. There was also a computer-checked proof in 2022 of the correctness of a standalone proof checker for HOL proofs in OpenTheory format.
From a security perspective, it's still concerning to be relying on large proof-checking binaries, where the answer to why we're supposed to trust one binary is that the binary has supposedly been checked by another large proof-checking binary. I'd like to see more work on simplifying these binaries.

Stanford Physicist Uses AI to Tackle Quantum Gravity Problem
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-27
14:55:57 UTC
23:55:57 KST

Stanford Physicist Uses AI to Tackle Quantum Gravity Problem
Insider Brief
- Stanford physicist Venkatesa Chandrasekaran proposed a quantum theory of gravity that uses the paths of light to calculate how spacetime can fluctuate.
- The approach yielded a finite result for a graviton-pair calculation that diverges under an approximation and reproduced a known quantum correction to black hole entropy.
- Chandrasekaran said he developed several calculations and proofs through months of work with OpenAI’s GPT-6 Astra, using about 400 million tokens.
A Stanford physicist has proposed a quantum theory of gravity that uses the paths of light to get a finite answer for a calculation that can otherwise run to infinity.
If the approach holds up, it could help reconcile quantum mechanics, which governs the behavior of matter and energy at small scales, with Einstein’s general relativity, which describes gravity as the curvature of spacetime. The study also reproduces a known quantum correction to the entropy of a black hole.
In a preprint posted on arXiv, Venkatesa Chandrasekaran calculated what happens when two gravitons, the hypothetical quantum units of gravitational waves, are created and then disappear. The calculation gave a finite answer even after accounting for waves of arbitrarily short wavelength. An approximation applied to the same calculation produced an infinity.
Chandrasekaran said in the paper’s acknowledgments that he developed several of its calculations and proofs through months of back-and-forth work with GPT-6 Astra, an OpenAI model. He put the total at about 400 million tokens, the units of text AI models process, and said OpenAI provided the computing resources.
The work addresses a problem of reconciling general relativity with quantum theory, a problem that, as mentioned, has vexed top physicists for decades. General relativity treats gravity as the curvature of spacetime. In plain terms, quantum theory allows physical quantities to fluctuate. If spacetime itself fluctuates, the geometry that ordinarily tells physicists where and when something happens also becomes part of what they have to calculate.
Chandrasekaran’s proposal uses the boundaries of finite regions of spacetime to set up those calculations. The boundaries follow paths that light could take, and their shape can change with the gravitational field. That gives the calculation a way to specify where something happens even as the geometry changes.
The study also finds that a region bounded this way, with a specified maximum size, can hold only a finite number of mutually distinguishable quantum states. In a separate calculation for a large black hole, the approach recovered both the standard relationship between a black hole’s entropy and its surface area and a previously calculated quantum correction.
It’s important to note that the paper is theoretical and presents no experimental test.
Using Light to Set the Boundaries
To calculate how gravity behaves when spacetime itself can fluctuate, Chandrasekaran starts by defining the region he wants to study. He uses the paths light can take between two events to set its boundaries.
One way to envision this is to imagine a flash going off and a detector set to record light at a particular moment later. Light spreading out from the flash marks one boundary. Tracing the possible paths of light backward from the detector at that moment marks the other. The two boundaries enclose a region called a causal diamond. Any point inside it can receive a signal from the flash and send one in time to reach the detector.
In general relativity, gravitational waves can distort those boundaries. The paper treats the surface where they meet as part of the system and locates quantities relative to it. Chandrasekaran first solves the classical equations that constrain which combinations of boundary data are possible. He then applies quantum rules to the remaining variables, which include the two ways a gravitational wave can be polarized.
To describe a larger stretch of spacetime, the paper joins regions along shared boundaries. It matches the gravitational data on the two sides and sums over the quantum states that can occur at an intermediate stage. Chandrasekaran shows that these intermediate sums converge within the construction. In fact, they converge even when the set of possible states is infinite.
The order of operations matters, as the graviton-pair calculation shows.
In practice, physicists often start with a rough answer and refine it by adding corrections based on the strength of the interaction.
In this example, expanding in Newton’s gravitational constant before adding the contributions from different wavelengths produces a term that grows without bound. Keeping the constant at its physical, nonzero value and doing the sum first gives a finite answer. At very short wavelengths, contributions that would otherwise drive the divergence partly cancel.
Counting States Inside a Black Hole
For the state-counting result, Chandrasekaran imposes a maximum size on a macroscopic causal diamond using what he calls a gravitational clock. The clock is a distance-like reading defined along the paths of light. Gravitational radiation changes that reading by affecting how neighboring light paths converge. Putting an upper limit on the reading also restricts which wave states can fit within the region.
The study concludes that a causal diamond with a fixed size limit can contain only a finite number of distinct quantum states. That means the area where the two light cones meet can take only certain values, with gaps between them.
The black hole calculation uses a surface where a bundle of outward-directed light rays momentarily stops spreading. The local gravitational data are enough to identify that surface and begin counting states on its interior side.
By summing over interior states, Chandrasekaran recovers the leading term in the Bekenstein-Hawking formula, which makes a black hole’s entropy proportional to its horizon area. Entropy describes how much microscopic information is compatible with the same large-scale description.
For a large, nonrotating black hole, the study also found a small adjustment to the standard entropy formula that grows slowly as the black hole’s surface area increases. The result matches an earlier calculation by physicist Ashoke Sen that held the black hole’s mass fixed and accounted for small variations in its spin.
Next Steps
A lot of work remains.
The four-dimensional theory covers gravity in a vacuum, where gravitational waves can propagate. Extending it to include matter would widen its scope. The calculation also recovers Einstein’s equations in the low-energy limit for initial quantum states concentrated around a classical geometry. It calculates the first quantum correction in that regime, including how fluctuations in gravity in turn affect the average geometry.
One question Chandrasekaran leaves for future work is whether different choices in building the quantum state space lead to equivalent theories for the same range of clock readings. Another test would be to carry the black hole and low-energy calculations beyond the first quantum correction.
For a deeper, more technical dive, please review the paper on arXiv. It’s important to note that arXiv is a pre-print server, which allows researchers to receive quick feedback on their work. However, it is not — nor is this article, itself — official peer-review publications. Peer-review is an important step in the scientific process to verify results.
