You can then adjust vertical_exaggeration to emphasize the terrain without changing the underlying unit conversion.
And this spatial data directionality can now be reversed. spatialize_image() turns a rayshader image, matrix, RGB array, or RGBA array back into a georeferenced terra::SpatRaster. It can inherit the cached extent and CRS from the active scene, convert rayshader’s linear color values to sRGB, and optionally append the source heightmap as an additional raster layer. So rayshader’s output can now feed back into other GIS software, rather than just assume rayshader is the final destination itself.
library(rayshader)
# Color cells below sea level before exporting the RGB raster.
rgb_raster = montereybay_spatial |>
  sphere_shade(vertical_exaggeration = 15) |>
  add_water(montereybay_spatial < 2, color = "dodgerblue4") |>
  spatialize_image(
    include_alpha = TRUE,
    include_height = TRUE
  )
terra::writeRaster(
  rgb_raster,
  "../images/2026/rayshader-100/monterey-georeferenced.tif",
  overwrite = TRUE
)
terra::plotRGB(rgb_raster, r = 1, g = 2, b = 3)
This release lets rayshader fit into a real spatial workflow: spatial input, then rayshader styling, followed by spatial output. So you can generate beautiful hillshades and terrains in R, and then bring them into other spatial software like QGIS or ArcGIS. Work in the GIS interface you already know, render the terrain in R, and drop the result right back into your map.
A watershed moment for rayshader water
Rayshader’s water was always a bit of an afterthought: it was good for rendering an ocean with bathymetry data, but it didn’t really work if the water was localized to one portion of the map. A lake halfway up a mountain shouldn’t require flooding everything below it to see the water, but rayshader’s old water meshing method assumed one water level for the whole map. Which was perfect if all you needed was that one large body of water: set a level and fill the scene to that height. Separate lakes, reservoirs at different elevations, and imperfect shorelines called for something more flexible.
Now water can arrive as a raster or a polygon, high in the mountains or below sea level. render_water() uses the terrain and coordinates already stored in the scene to properly place and render the water in a sane way.
The old single-level behavior remains available as water_render_method = "legacy", as it is much faster and is perfectly suited for coastlines and topobathymetry data.
Water can follow grid cells with water_render_method = "raster" or preserve polygon boundaries with the default, water_render_method = "polygon". Both draw a watertight mesh at the footprint and elevation you supply, even when that means an exposed vertical water wall where the supplied shoreline doesn’t quite meet the terrain. Fast, flexible, and suitable for the rare case where you need to visualize Moses crossing the Red Sea.
Another (much cooler, IMO) option is water_render_method = "optimized". It fits a closed shoreline to the actual 3D terrain mesh, adjusting the water elevation to match the supplied area. Using the elevations beneath your water footprint to seed candidate levels, the optimizer searches for a contour that comes as close as possible to the supplied water area without spilling out of the terrain. The animation below shows how candidate levels change the flooded area.
If no closed water body is possible—for example, the supplied water would flood the entire map—rayshader falls back to raster water.
A different problem arises when a DEM has no bathymetry: the lake bed and its surface occupy the same plane. shift_terrain() is a new function that can lower the terrain beneath a lake polygon before you add water, making room for the lake instead of letting the surfaces overlap. It can also raise terrain SimCity 2000-style (no tornados or alien invasions… yet).
Additionally, in render_highquality(), water can now use a rough dielectric material to spread reflections into the broad highlights you expect from a rippled lake. A perfectly smooth dielectric surface behaves like glass: reflected light stays coherent, so lakes and rivers can turn into polished sheets. Real water has small wind-driven ripples, each reflecting light in a slightly different direction. The microfacet material models this give a more realistic appearance to bodies of water, especially when viewed at a low angle.
Rayshader: now streaming!
Streams need to follow the terrain as they wind downhill. render_streams() takes spatial line data, samples the elevation along each path, and builds ribbons of water that hug the surface. Widths can vary by feature, and connected streams can be merged to reduce visible seams in high-quality renders. Put those streams together with lakes and you have a hydrologic scene driven by data.
Load and prepare Mono County terrain, lakes, and streams
library(rayshader)
library(sf)
library(terra)
library(dplyr)
library(tigris)
library(elevatr)
options(tigris_use_cache = TRUE)
target_crs = "EPSG:32611"
dem_z = 12
scene_ll = sf::st_as_sfc(
  sf::st_bbox(
    c(xmin = -119.12, ymin = 37.52, xmax = -118.68, ymax = 37.86),
    crs = sf::st_crs(4326)
  )
) |>
  sf::st_as_sf()
scene = sf::st_transform(scene_ll, target_crs)
water = tigris::area_water(
  state = "CA",
  county = "Mono",
  year = 2024,
  class = "sf"
) |>
  sf::st_make_valid() |>
  sf::st_transform(target_crs) |>
  sf::st_crop(scene) |>
  dplyr::filter(MTFCC %in% c("H2030", "H2040")) |>
  dplyr::mutate(area_m2 = as.numeric(sf::st_area(geometry))) |>
  dplyr::filter(area_m2 >= 5000)
dem_raw = elevatr::get_elev_raster(
  locations = scene,
  z = dem_z,
  src = "aws",
  clip = "bbox"
)
dem = terra::rast(dem_raw) |>
  resize_spatial(scale = 0.5, method_down = "mean")
names(dem) = "elevation_m"
water_level = terra::extract(
  dem,
  terra::vect(water),
  fun = median,
  na.rm = TRUE,
  touches = TRUE
)
water$water_level_m = round(water_level$elevation_m, 1)
water = water[is.finite(water$water_level_m), ]
streams = tigris::linear_water(
  state = "CA",
  county = "Mono",
  year = 2024,
  class = "sf"
) |>
  sf::st_make_valid() |>
  sf::st_transform(target_crs) |>
  sf::st_crop(scene) |>
  sf::st_collection_extract("LINESTRING", warn = FALSE)dem |>
  shift_terrain(
    geometry = water,
    amount = -10
  ) |>
  height_shade() |>
  plot_3d(
    phi = 10,
    zoom = 0.15,
    fov = 29.1,
    shadow = FALSE,
    normals = FALSE
  )
render_water(
  water_input = water,
  water_render_method = "polygon",
  water_edge_extension = 0.25,
  water_color = "dodgerblue",
  water_polygon_failure = "raster"
)
render_streams(
  streams = streams,
  water_color = "dodgerblue",
  width = 1
)
render_highquality(
  camera_location = c(-1752.16, 563.86, 415.55),
  camera_lookat = c(-155.98, -12.60, -246.84),
  fov = 10.5,
  width = 1500,
  height = 500,
  samples = 128,
  water_material = "microfacet",
  water_ior = 1.5,
  iso = 5,
  sky_sun_elevation = 20,
  sky_sun_azimuth = -113,
  sky_args = list(hosek = FALSE)
)
rgl::close3d()
Note that there’s no complex GIS concepts on the rayshader side: all the complexity is related to defining the visual style and appearance. As it should be–rayshader is a visualization package! The remaining complexity is just standard data complexity present in any analysis: get the data loaded, cleaned, and in a form that represents the thing you care about.
The road to believable roads
3D maps might seem like a fun little toy rather than a serious class of data visualization, but the third dimension is often surprisingly important for understanding geospatial data. This becomes less surprising when you realize that the most common mapping tool used by regular people–GPS apps–use 3D quite extensively. When you’re traveling through a city, buildings give you landmarks to match to your surroundings; on a walking or cycling route, the terrain shows where you’ll be climbing; and at a complex highway interchange, seeing which roads pass over one another helps you follow the right ramp and choose the right exit.
I already had surface-hugging polygons working for streams. Swap the water for asphalt and there’s a road! Easy, peasy, right?
No! What’s the issue? We’re spoiled with our GPS apps: we’re used to roads looking like roads. A strip of grey asphalt needs lane markings for someone to know it’s a road. With the help of some fun repeating UV coordinate tricks and our new spatial-first workflow, we can paint properly-sized lines on the road at the cost of a few small textures. And we can use the lane counts supplied in OpenStreetMap data to add the correct number of lanes to each road. Rayshader uses those counts to mesh and texture the road, while the scene’s spatial information keeps the markings correctly sized.
In Figure 11, each dash is 10 feet long and each gap is 30 feet. Those familiar markings do double duty: they improve the picture and act as little scale bars scattered through the scene. Beautiful! Pack it up, put a bow on it, and move on to the next feature, right?
The spacing is configurable for local standards.
No! Our roads are still smooshed together on one layer. At an interchange, that borders on uninterpretable.
OpenStreetMap doesn’t provide a full 3D road model. But it does carry one tiny (but extremely useful!) nugget of vertical information needed to draw roads correctly in 2D: drawing order. Draw layer 0, then layer 1, then layer 2, and the right road appears on top. That tells us which road crosses over which; it doesn’t tell us their heights or how the ramps return to ground level. Is this enough information to fully reconstruct our 3D roads?
The tempting shortcut is to turn each layer into a fixed height: find the crossings, lift each road by a standard overpass height, and ease it back toward the ground with some sort of ad hoc relaxation rule. What could go wrong?
Everything. Everything can go wrong.
Simple hacks? Don’t work here. Turns out the world is filled with little annoying complications like a bridge crossing a sunken highway (the highway is already below ground, so the bridge needs no change in elevation), or short segments in interchanges that are technically not stacked but fall between stacked layers, or places where the road is technically stacked over a traversable water body (which also counts as a layer!), or a thousand other pesky little details.
What are we to do then? How can we implement realistic highways? It’s not like there’s a manual with explicit guidance and standards for how roads should be built in 3D—wait, what is that? There is an explicit manual?
The American Association of State Highway and Transportation Officials (AASHTO) has a design manual for Interstate highways. Two of the things it covers are exactly what our map is missing. How much room does a truck need under a bridge? How steep can the road get on its way up there? Now that rayshader knows the scale of the scene, I can give the solver a clearance in feet and a maximum grade in percent. Here are the default settings I gave the road solver (these are customizable):
- Keeping crossing road surfaces at least 14 feet apart
- Limiting the road grade to 7%
- Ensuring roads default to hugging the ground when not part of an intersection and do not rise unnecessarily
We can turn these requirements into constraints on the road heights. While the rules aren’t guaranteed to generate roads that match the real-world roads exactly, it’s going to be pretty close: excursions from the bare minimum allowable ruleset will usually only increase cost, and we’re talking government contracts here. Around each interchange, we discretize the roads into short segments and solve for their elevations together: maintain the crossing order and clearance, limit the grades, and keep the roads close to the terrain wherever possible.
It’s not like there’s a manual with explicit guidance and standards for how roads should be built in 3D—wait, what is that? There is an explicit manual?
For example, a road can weave over and under several others. Lifting one crossing changes the grades leading into the next, so solving each crossing independently won’t work–we have to solve all the linked constraints at once. We build a graph of the road connections with {igraph}, locate crossings with {sf}, and follow the constraints along connected road segments. Each connected optimization region gets a sparse quadratic problem, assembled with {Matrix} and solved with {osqp}. It turns out, R has everything you need to build and optimize road profiles.
It’s pretty cool how closely a set of road-design rules and OSM’s layer ordering can recreate a complex interchange. The layer tags tell us which road goes over which, and the rules guide how the ramps connect them. Here’s Houston, now modeled in a sane way:
Beautiful. And since I went to Houston for this year’s Posit::conf, I was able to drive over specific intersections I rendered here and confirm that yes: the roads were accurately meshed. Minus the traffic.
Note“Why not cars?”
The novelty seeking half of my brain immediately followed this thought with: “You should put in cars now!” to which the logical, killjoy side of my brain responded “NO! STOP IT! YOU’VE IMPLEMENTED TOO MANY FEATURES ALREADY! You will never release this update if you keep indulging yourself! Stop it!”
Maybe in 1.1.0…
A human battery? No–a human measuring stick.
Lane markings made the scale of a scene easier to read. Naturally, that led me to the ultimate human-scale feature: humans! But I couldn’t just throw the first set of free human models I found on the internet into the package. I did a fairly extensive search for a suitable pre-made model with both male and female variants, but to no avail. I had some requirements:
- Low-poly enough to fit in the package when compressed (an R package is limited to 5 MB).
- Proportioned like actual people, which rules out a surprising number of free models.
I couldn’t find a model that met these requirements with a suitable redistribution license. So I decided this was a great opportunity to learning something new: mesh sculpting in VR! I pulled out my Quest 3 and downloaded Shapelabs, which after a bit of practice and futzing around got me exactly what I wanted. Low-poly, but still human enough that you can read the pose at a glance.
From Shapelabs, the models went into Blender as GLB files for rigging and posing, then out as compact OBJ meshes for the package:
Render both pose galleries
options(rgl.useNULL = TRUE, cores = 4)
library(rayshader)
pose_heightmap = matrix(1:16 / 2, nrow = 4, byrow = TRUE) |>
  rayimage::render_reorient(flipy = TRUE, transpose = TRUE) |>
  rayimage::render_resized(mag = 50, method = "box")
pose_positions = expand.grid(
  x = seq(25, 175, by = 50),
  y = seq(25, 125, by = 50)
)
poses = c(
  "standing",
  "walking",
  "stop",
  "stop_one_hand",
  "clapping",
  "yelling",
  "stretch",
  "slipping",
  "stack",
  "stack",
  "ironman",
  "rocky"
)
pose_positions$offset = ifelse(poses == "ironman", 1, 0)
# Keep labels below the platform edges and closer to their own models.
pose_positions$label_altitude = pose_positions$offset + 1.75
# Keep the flying pose label below the top edge of its platform.
pose_positions$label_altitude[poses == "ironman"] = 2.1
pose_positions$label_altitude[poses == "slipping"] = 1.6
stack_index = 10L
label_indices = setdiff(seq_along(poses), stack_index)
platform_colors = c(male = "#102D54", female = "#800000")
dir.create("figures", showWarnings = FALSE)
for (model_sex in names(platform_colors)) {
  platform_color = platform_colors[[model_sex]]
  pose_heightmap |>
    constant_shade(platform_color) |>
    plot_3d(
      heightmap = pose_heightmap,
      zscale = 1 / 25,
      vertical_exaggeration = 1,
      shadow = FALSE,
      solid = TRUE,
      solid_depth = 0,
      solid_color = platform_color,
      window_size = c(1000, 1000)
    )
  render_camera(
    theta = -28,
    phi = 16,
    zoom = 0.80,
    fov = 0,
    shift_vertical = -10
  )
  render_people(
    pose = poses,
    sex = model_sex,
    color = "white",
    x = pose_positions$x,
    y = pose_positions$y,
    offset = pose_positions$offset
  )
  # Measure this variant so the upper model rests on the lower one's hands.
  model_file = system.file(
    "extdata",
    "raypeople",
    if (model_sex == "male") {
      "person_man_stack.txt"
    } else {
      "person_woman_stack.txt"
    },
    package = "rayshader"
  )
  stack_vertices = rayvertex::read_obj(model_file)$vertices[[1]]
  stack_height = diff(range(stack_vertices[, 2]))
  render_people(
    pose = "stack",
    sex = model_sex,
    color = "white",
    x = pose_positions$x[stack_index],
    y = pose_positions$y[stack_index],
    offset = stack_height
  )
  # Keep the long right-edge label fully on its platform in both galleries.
  for (i in label_indices) {
    render_label(
      text = poses[i],
      font = "Helvetica",
      family = "serif",
      font_type = "standard",
      text_size = 1.5,
      text_color = "white",
      adjust_vec = if (poses[i] == "stop_one_hand") {
        c(0.85, 0)
      } else if (poses[i] == "slipping") {
        c(0.65, -0.5)
      } else {
        NULL
      },
      line = FALSE,
      x = pose_positions$x[i],
      y = pose_positions$y[i],
      altitude = pose_positions$label_altitude[i],
      relative_z = TRUE
    )
  }
  render_label(
    text = "stack",
    font = "Helvetica",
    family = "serif",
    font_type = "standard",
    text_size = 1.5,
    text_color = "black",
    line = FALSE,
    x = pose_positions$x[stack_index],
    y = pose_positions$y[stack_index],
    altitude = 2 * stack_height - 0.25,
    relative_z = TRUE
  )
  set.seed(42)
  render_highquality(
    filename = file.path("../images/2026/rayshader-100", paste0("people-poses-", model_sex, ".png")),
    width = 1000,
    height = 1000,
    sky_sun_elevation = 10,
    sky_sun_azimuth = 160,
    iso = 5,
    samples = 128,
    integrator_type = "metal",
    transparent_background = TRUE,
    interactive = FALSE,
    preview = FALSE,
    progress = FALSE
  )
  rgl::close3d()
}
Compressed, they’re roughly 18–21 KB each, checking the filesize box. Both model variants stand about 6 feet tall, with consistent proportions across their poses. Finally, a unit of measurement you can relate to!
“I know ggplot2.”
The original plot_gg() was based on a simple idea: take a ggplot2 object, turn a fill or color aesthetic into height, and map the original plot onto that extruded surface. This made it possible to create 3D ggplots without learning a separate 3D plotting API. Just drop in your existing plot and poof! A 3D version would appear.
But this came with one limitation: the data had to be attached to the surface.
Floating data needs something to ground it. I originally kept the data attached to the surface deliberately: actual 3D data is harder to understand on a 2D screen, since depth is ambiguous. Extruded 2D surfaces provide a continuous underlying substrate, which makes interpretation easier–the surface itself provides depth cues, by nature of surface continuity providing cues on depth ordering. It’s not hard to plop down some floating 3D data in a box and call it a day: before rayshader, that was what pretty much every 3D plotting library I encountered did. In my opinion, that lazy approach helped give 3D dataviz its terrible reputation. Adding 3D data disconnected from that substrate requires additional care and thought to keep the data interpretable.
Which rayshader now provides!
In rayshader 1.0, plot_gg() is much more tightly integrated with the render_*() family of functions that add real 3D data to the scene. Rayshader now extracts the data extent from the existing ggplot2 scene. Basically, we now track how ggplot2 transforms data into the image, so we can use the same transformation for our 3D data. Downstream render calls can use that extent to place real 3D geometry in the correct part of the plot. The ggplot is no longer just a surface extrusion: it’s now the base coordinate system for a complete 3D scene.
And this can really help visually, as that surface extrusion wasn’t ideal for certain types of data. The original raster extrusion worked well for smooth surfaces, density plots, and continuous fields, but sharp polygon boundaries and abrupt vertical edges were harder.
Take Figure 19: it shows a map of US states with height mapped to population. States share an boundary in the source polygons, but when rasterized to row of pixels, you can see the colors bleed down the step between states. This is an unavoidable problem when you try to force vector data into a raster format. So we avoid it by drawing the actual 3D data. On the right, each state’s own polygon makes a crisp vertical wall.
Compare raster extrusion with vector polygons
library(ggplot2)
plot_colors = scales::hue_pal()(6)[c(5, 4, 3, 2, 1, 6)]
us_states = spData::us_states
states_gg = ggplot(us_states) +
  geom_sf(aes(fill = total_pop_15), color = NA) +
  scale_fill_gradientn(colors = plot_colors, name = "Population (2015)")
plot_gg(
  states_gg,
  width = 6,
  height = 5,
  height_aes = "fill",
  raytrace = FALSE,
  solid_depth = -0.2,
  shadow = FALSE,
  theta = -15,
  phi = 35,
  zoom = 0.54,
  window_size = c(1200, 700)
)
render_camera(shift_vertical = -90)
render_highquality(
  width = 1200,
  height = 700,
  light_direction = 90,
  light_altitude = 35,
  samples = 48,
  transparent_background = TRUE
)
# Keep the ggplot as a base and add actual polygon geometry.
plot_gg(
  states_gg,
  width = 6,
  height = 5,
  height_aes = "fill",
  flat_substrate = TRUE,
  raytrace = FALSE,
  solid_depth = -0.2,
  shadow = FALSE,
  theta = -15,
  phi = 35,
  zoom = 0.54,
  window_size = c(1200, 700)
)
render_polygons(
  us_states,
  data_column_top = "total_pop_15",
  color = "height",
  clear_previous = TRUE
)
render_camera(shift_vertical = -90)
render_highquality(
  width = 1200,
  height = 700,
  light_direction = 90,
  light_altitude = 35,
  samples = 48,
  transparent_background = TRUE
)
The same integration lets individual points float above a flat ggplot2 substrate (enabled with plot_gg(..., flat_substrate = TRUE)). The ggplot establishes the scales and coordinate system, and rayshader adds the observed values as actual 3D points, positioned correctly. render_zaxis() gives those floating marks a scale you can read. It picks up the height scale from the ggplot, and you can put the axis inside a panel or along the outer edge of the plot. If you have facets, you can pick the panel. It works on regular terrain maps, too.
Add points above a ggplot coordinate grid
p = ggplot(mtcars, aes(wt, mpg)) +
  geom_point(aes(color = hp), alpha = 0) +
  scale_color_gradientn(colors = rep("#62676d", 2), limits = c(0, 350)) +
  coord_cartesian(xlim = c(1, 6), ylim = c(10, 35)) +
  labs(x = "Weight (1,000 lb)", y = "Fuel economy (mpg)") +
  theme_minimal() +
  theme(legend.position = "none")
plot_gg(
  p,
  height_aes = "color",
  vertical_exaggeration = 280,
  width = 5,
  height = 5,
  phi = 40,
  theta = -27,
  fov = 33,
  zoom = 0.83,
  flat_substrate = TRUE,
  raytrace = FALSE,
  window_size = c(800, 800)
)
render_points(
  x = mtcars$wt,
  y = mtcars$mpg,
  altitude = mtcars$hp,
  color = "dodgerblue",
  size = 2,
  offset = 0
)
render_zaxis(
  data = "point",
  breaks = c(0, 100, 200, 300),
  title = "Horsepower (hp)"
)
render_highquality(
  point_radius = 5,
  sky_sun_elevation = 90,
  sky_sun_azimuth = 20,
  light = FALSE,
  iso = 2,
  ground_material = rayrender::glossy(color = "rgb(130, 151, 134)")
)
This removes a ton of visual compromises. Instead of forcing every feature to become part of the surface texture, we use the surface texture as the base and add the features as real 3D geometry. The plot layout and elements are still determined by ggplot2: we’re just adding data to the z-axis.
As a note, while it raises the bar on what you can do, it also lowers the bar for making bad 3D visualizations, but with great power comes great responsibility! Oh, wait, wrong movie.
Faceted plots can receive geometry one panel at a time. Spatial plots built with coord_sf() carry their geographic transform into the scene, too. Your base plot can use familiar geoms—geom_hex(), geom_point(), geom_path(), geom_raster(), or geom_sf()—while rayshader adds polygons, points, paths, and a labeled z-axis in those coordinates.
Extrude county values and add a z-axis
counties = sf::st_read(system.file("shape/nc.shp", package = "sf"))
p = ggplot(counties) +
  geom_sf(aes(fill = BIR74), color = "white") +
  scale_fill_gradientn(
    colors = plot_colors,
    name = "Births (1974)",
    limits = c(0, 25000)
  ) +
  theme_void() +
  theme(legend.position = "none")
plot_gg(
  p,
  height_aes = "fill",
  flat_substrate = TRUE,
  raytrace = FALSE,
  width = 6,
  height = 3,
  window_size = c(1500, 500)
)
render_camera(theta = 25, phi = 25, zoom = 0.35, fov = 0)
render_polygons(counties, data_column_top = "BIR74", color = "height")
render_zaxis(
  data = "polygon",
  breaks = c(0, 10000, 20000),
  labels = c("0", "10,000", "20,000"),
  title = "Births (1974)",
  title_location = "top",
  title_offset = 1.5,
  label_size = 1,
  title_size = 1.1
)
render_highquality(
  sky_sun_elevation = 20,
  sky_sun_azimuth = 315,
  iso = 10,
  text_render = "screen",
  text_occlusion = FALSE,
  screen_text_args = list(halo_color = "white", halo_expand = 5),
  ground_material = rayrender::diffuse(color = "grey20")
)
rgl::close3d()
3D ggplots were always a cool, sometimes useful novelty. Now they can be a foundation for complex 3D visualizations. How complex are we talking about? Watch me demo it below with a data visualization showing ADSB flight path data over Washington, DC. Come fly over the National Mall with me and I’ll walk you through how deep the 3D rabbit hole goes.
Better buildings, paths, labels, and objects
The render_*() functions have been rewritten around the same principle: spatial data already encodes where it is, so there is no reason to throw that information out.
render_points() can accept spatial point objects. render_path() can accept spatial line objects. render_label() can accept points, multipoints, polygons, and multipolygons, converting polygon inputs to centroids when needed. render_polygons() and render_beveled_polygons() use cached heightmap and extent metadata. render_buildings() uses spatial footprints, roof materials, roof angles, height columns, and CRS-aware extent filtering. render_obj() can place OBJ models using spatial point data. You can see most of these in action in Figure 22.
New hillshading options
There’s some new basic hillshading options as well. haze_shade() adds an RGBA haze overlay based on an exponential atmospheric model. It is useful for low-elevation haze, valley haze, and scenes where you want the atmosphere to participate in the map rather than sit behind it. It is a good way to subtly separate mountain tops from valleys.
Add haze to the inland valleys
library(rayshader)
# Crop the bundled DEM to the inland hills and valleys east of Monterey Bay.
hills = terra::crop(
  montereybay_spatial,
  terra::ext(-121.73, -121.38, 36.20, 36.48)
)
dark_terrain = create_texture(
  light_color = "#879581",
  shadow_color = "#15252b",
  left_color = "#54634b",
  right_color = "#283f42",
  center_color = "#354c41"
)
hills |>
  sphere_shade(texture = dark_terrain, vertical_exaggeration = 5) |>
  add_overlay(haze_shade(
    color = "#dfe8f2",
    optical_depth = 1.5,
    scale_height = 200
  )) |>
  plot_3d(
    theta = 220,
    phi = 30,
    zoom = 0.4,
    fov = 50,
    background = "#20282f",
    solid_color = "#161e23"
  )
render_snapshot()
radiance_shade() renders a top-down radiance pass from a cached hillshade context, the active 3D scene, or a supplied heightmap. It gives 2D maps and 3D ggplot2 workflows access to more visually-striking physically based lighting without forcing the entire project into a final path-traced render. This is a nice way to get higher-quality shadows on a model you can still rotate and revise quickly. The result is still an RGBA overlay, so it can be composited back into the usual rayshader map pipeline or exported to other programs.
library(rayshader)
montereybay_spatial |>
  height_shade() |>
  add_overlay(
    radiance_shade(
      vertical_exaggeration = 10,
      integrator_type = "metal",
      samples = 16,
      sky_sun_elevation = 1,
      sky_sun_azimuth = 315
    ),
    alpha_layer = 0.9
  ) |>
  plot_3d(
    vertical_exaggeration = 4,
    theta = -70,
    phi = 35,
    zoom = 0.30,
    fov = 90,
    window_size = c(1200, 500)
  )
render_snapshot()
Earth’s curvature
For a small area, we can usually ignore Earth’s curvature. As a map grows to cover hundreds or thousands of kilometers, the bend of the Earth can become an important part of the story. For satellite orbits, long-distance flight paths, and regional coverage maps, curvature can help signal to the reader that we’re dealing with global-scale data without an explicit scale bar.
Setting earth_curvature = TRUE in plot_3d() adds that curvature to the scene. Mountains and valleys can look flatter beside the much larger bend of the Earth when no vertical exaggeration is applied, but the adjustment gives those other GIS layers a more useful spatial context.
For example, Figure 25 shows three Starlink satellites cross Alaska along paths calculated from real orbital data. Their simulated beams track populated places below, switching targets as they move within reach. Both views show the same moment, but on the right, the surface and orbital altitudes are adjusted to match the global curvature. This helps give the viewer important context clues for the spatial scales we’re dealing with here that the flat projection does not provide.
Even higher quality render_highquality()
Want to realistically light a scene at a very specific time and date? Just give rayshader a date and time–it already knows the place. For a scene with cached spatial coordinates, render_highquality(datetime = ...) can infer the location and generate the corresponding sky and sunlight. You don’t need to look up the latitude, longitude, or sun angle again. Depending on the sky model, that can include stars, planets, and the moon’s phase and brightness.
As an example, around the equinoxes, the low sun lines up with the National Mall’s east–west axis—a favorite alignment for photographers. On March 25, 2026, at 6:56:11 p.m. EDT, the modeled shadow of the Washington Monument runs parallel to the Mall’s main paths. A few days past the equinox, the sun is still high enough at that alignment to keep the shadow within this view.
Set up an overhead view of the National Mall
library(rayshader)
mall = readRDS("../include/rayshader-100/data/mall-scene.rds")
mall_dem = terra::rast("../images/2026/rayshader-100/mall-dem.tif")
mall_dem |>
  constant_shade("#819369") |>
  plot_3d(
    vertical_exaggeration = 1,
    solid = FALSE,
    shadow = FALSE,
    theta = 0,
    phi = 80,
    fov = 0,
    window_size = c(1600, 900)
  )
render_water(mall$water, water_color = "#537b86")
render_roads(mall$roads, lane_texture = TRUE)
render_trails(
  mall$walkways,
  color = "#d9cbb0",
  width = 3,
  width_units = "meters",
  offset = 0.2
)
render_multipolygonz(mall$buildings, color = "#dcd8ce")
render_multipolygonz(mall$monument, color = "#f3eadd")
render_tree(
  mall$trees,
  tree_height_column = "MAX_CROWN_HEIGHT",
  crown_color = "#516c3d",
  preview_simple = TRUE
)
render_label(
  x = mall$center[1],
  y = mall$center[2] - 210,
  altitude = 20,
  text = "Washington Monument",
  line = FALSE,
  adjust_vec = c(0.5, 0.5)
)render_highquality(
  datetime = as.POSIXct("2026-03-25 18:56:11", tz = "America/New_York"),
  integrator_type = "metal",
  ortho_dimensions = c(560, 315),
  light = FALSE,
  iso = 16,
  text_size = 24,
  text_occlusion = FALSE,
  screen_text_args = list(halo_color = "white", halo_expand = 2),
  samples = 256
)
And separately, text and annotation lines were physical objects in the rendered scene. Their letters and lines cast shadows across the terrain and buildings, giving annotations the same simulated weight as the things they were annotating. A label should help you read the map without becoming another object in it.
The labels in Figure 27 are drawn in screen space: their positions stay attached to the map, while the text keeps its size and faces the camera throughout the orbit, without casting shadows over the volcanoes. The horsepower axis in Figure 20 uses the same approach. The lines and text are composited in real time when you’re looking at the high quality preview render in rayrender, so you can set up the shot exactly how you want to. Oh, and you can set deferred_render = TRUE now as well, which removes the sample timer so you can have all the time in the world to set up your render. This is especially important now, since rayrender is now fully GPU-accellerated on ARM macs, leading to 30-50x speedups in rendering performance, so your render can often end before you got a chance to click on the screen. This blog post, however, is about rayshader, so I won’t steal its thunder talking about the awesome improvements in rayrender :)
1.0 and beyond!
It’s been a long, properly textured road–why 1.0 after eight years? I now feel that rayshader has firmly found its niche and purpose, and this overhaul feels like a graduation moment for the package. I’m no longer figuring out what a 3D GIS workflow in R should feel like–it’s now about making that workflow work for you. And thinking about all the people who used rayshader to produce everything from beautiful generative art and frameable cartographic works to scientific articles and award winning journalism made me decide to give rayshader the graduation it deserves, and make sure it knows: You’re number 1.0!
The grand finale and most exciting part of any publication: the bibliography.
Rayshader wouldn’t be possible without the work of all the packages and people that supported it and provided great user feedback during its development. In a normal blog post, this is where normally you’d have a list of citations and people to thank. But how often does R’s most cinematic package get a 1.0 release? I decided the people, packages, and data behind rayshader deserved the full cinematic treatment that rayshader has provided the R ecosystem. And that the cinematic treatment should be created entirely in R, using rayrender, of course. Please stick around and hit play for the credits below—and be sure to turn the sound on.

15 Leftover Turkey Recipes
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-27
14:30:00 UTC
23:30:00 KST

Got a mountain of leftover turkey sitting in the fridge and no idea what to make with it? After spending all that time (and money) on the big Thanksgiving meal, I want to make sure those leftovers don’t go to waste. These easy leftover turkey recipes include everything from a cozy pie and hearty soups to quick lunches and clever chicken-to-turkey swaps, so you can get a few more delicious meals out of that holiday bird!
All recipes are rigorously tested in our Nashville Test Kitchen to ensure they are easy, affordable, and delicious.
Hearty Mains
The last thing I want to do after cooking a big Thanksgiving feast is start another complicated dinner from scratch. These hearty meals put that cooked turkey to work alongside affordable ingredients like beans, rice, potatoes, and vegetables. You’ll get a completely different dinner out of it, and maybe even enough for lunch the next day!
1. Turkey Pot Pie
Turkey Pot Pie Recipe
My turkey pot pie is one recipe where I happily skip making pastry from scratch! Ready-made pie crusts save time, and the creamy filling is packed with shredded turkey, potatoes, frozen mixed vegetables, and a little Parmesan for extra flavor. If you’ve made turkey broth from your Thanksgiving bird, you can use that in the filling, too.
2. Turkey Chili
Turkey Chili
Had enough of Thanksgiving flavors for a while? This hearty turkey chili is just what I’d put on the menu next. It’s loaded with three types of beans, sweet corn, poblano peppers, and warming spices that give the turkey a completely different flavor. The recipe usually starts by browning ground turkey, but with leftovers, I’d skip that step and stir in 2-3 cups of chopped cooked turkey toward the end of simmering. Or if you’ve got ground turkey in the fridge, check out our ground turkey recipes roundup for more dinner ideas!
3. Turkey and Wild Rice Soup
Turkey and Wild Rice Soup
If you’ve got a few carrots, some celery, and leftover turkey in the fridge, you’re already well on your way to a big pot of turkey and wild rice soup. Mushrooms, herbs, and a splash of cream give the broth plenty of flavor, while the rice makes it filling enough for dinner. I’d stick with a wild rice blend rather than buying pure wild rice. It’s generally cheaper, and the different grains give the soup a lovely mix of textures.
4. Thanksgiving Bowls
Thanksgiving Salad Bowl
I love a big, colorful salad after the holidays, especially when I can use up all those little bits and pieces taking up space in the fridge. My Thanksgiving bowls combine fresh greens, rice, roasted vegetables, crunchy apples, dried cranberries, and a homemade maple cinnamon dressing. Shredded leftover turkey makes a tasty addition, and I’d absolutely use any roasted sweet potatoes or Brussels sprouts already in the fridge rather than cooking more!
Lunches and Light Bites
You don’t always need another full dinner to get through those leftovers. Sometimes a handful of turkey is all it takes to make a satisfying lunch or something to share while everyone’s still relaxing after the holiday.
5. Turkey Sliders
Turkey Sliders Recipe
Turkey sandwiches are practically a Thanksgiving tradition, but these turkey sliders take things a little further. Soft Hawaiian rolls are layered with cooked turkey, cranberry sauce, and Swiss cheese, then brushed with a buttery Dijon and everything bagel seasoning glaze before baking. The cheese gets lovely and melty, and the tops turn golden in the oven. You can swap the Swiss for leftover cheese from your holiday cheese board, too!
6. Turkey Taco Salad
Turkey Taco Salad
For something fresh, crunchy, and completely different from a roast dinner, try this turkey taco salad. The recipe originally uses ground turkey, but I’d chop up some leftover roasted turkey and warm it in a skillet with the taco seasoning and a splash of water instead! You can also swap the homemade avocado dressing for whatever creamy dressing you’ve got in the fridge to save a little extra prep.
7. Thanksgiving Leftovers Tortilla Wrap
Thanksgiving Leftovers Tortilla Wrap
You know those containers in the fridge with barely enough leftovers for one serving? This Thanksgiving leftovers tortilla wrap is exactly what they’re good for. It uses just ¼ cup each of turkey, stuffing, and cranberry sauce, along with some creamy Brie, all folded into a large tortilla and toasted until golden. I’d chop the turkey into small pieces before assembling, so the wrap folds neatly and holds together.
8. Thanksgiving Potato Skins
Thanksgiving Potato Skins
After all the cooking that goes into Thanksgiving, I’m usually ready to hang up my apron for a few days. That’s part of the reason I love these Thanksgiving potato skins. I just scoop out baked potatoes and mash it with butter and sour cream, then fold in leftover turkey, stuffing, and peas before piling everything back into the skins. A little cranberry sauce, melted cheddar, and warm gravy for dipping finish them off. 😋
9. Leftover Stuffing Muffins
Leftover Stuffing Muffins
Leftover stuffing makes a surprisingly handy base for savory breakfast muffins! These individual portions combine it with cooked turkey, spinach, eggs, and milk, so they’re just as useful for a quick lunch or snack. These leftover stuffing muffins bake in a muffin pan, so there’s no slicing or portioning to worry about afterward. I’d also throw in a little shredded cheese or some finely chopped roasted vegetables if there are any left.
10. Holiday Leftovers Wreath
Holiday Leftovers Wreath
This holiday leftovers wreath is a real showstopper, and most of the filling is already sitting in your fridge! Turkey, mashed potatoes, stuffing, cranberry sauce, and mozzarella are wrapped inside buttery crescent roll dough, then baked into a golden wreath. Serve it with warm gravy in the center for dipping, and you’ve got something that looks like you spent far longer making it than you actually did.
Easy Chicken-to-Turkey Swaps
Cooked shredded turkey works really well in dishes that usually call for chicken, particularly when there’s plenty of sauce, cheese, or seasoning involved. I’ve included a few easy swaps below, along with any adjustments I’d make to avoid cooking the turkey longer than necessary.
11. Traditional Chicken Salad
Chicken Salad (Traditional)
This traditional chicken salad is one of the recipes I always make when my dad visits (and it’s just as easy to make with leftover turkey!) Replace the four cups of shredded rotisserie chicken with cooked turkey, then mix it with crunchy celery, red onion, juicy grapes, walnuts, and the creamy mayo dressing. There’s no cooking involved, and you can serve it in sandwiches, lettuce cups, or with crackers for an easy lunch.
12. Cheesy Chicken Nachos
Chicken Nachos
Crispy tortilla chips, plenty of melted cheese, seasoned turkey, and all your favorite toppings. What’s not to like? These chicken nachos are made with chicken breast in the original recipe, but there’s no reason you can’t use diced leftover turkey. I’d toss the cooked meat with taco seasoning and warm it through with a splash of water before layering it with the chips and cheese. Add the avocado, jalapeños, green onions, and other fresh toppings after baking so they stay fresh and crunchy. A tray of these would disappear pretty quickly at a family movie night!
13. Chicken Enchiladas
Chicken Enchiladas Recipe
Our chicken enchiladas already call for cooked shredded chicken, which makes them one of the easiest recipes to turn into a leftover turkey dinner. Simply replace the chicken with three cups of shredded turkey, mix with the cumin, garlic powder, and green chiles, then roll everything into flour tortillas. They’re covered in homemade red enchilada sauce and plenty of cheese before baking until bubbly.
14. Creamy White Chicken Lasagna
White Chicken Lasagna Recipe
When there’s a generous amount of turkey left in the fridge, I’d use it to make this creamy white chicken lasagna for my family. Layers of pasta, ricotta, spinach, mozzarella, Parmesan, and a rich homemade white sauce make it a comforting change from the usual tomato-based lasagna. It makes nine hearty portions, but you could cut it into twelve smaller servings and add a simple side salad to stretch it further!
15. White Chicken Chili
Creamy White Chicken Chili
Here’s another cozy dinner that would make excellent use of already-cooked turkey. This white chicken chili is packed with beans, green chiles, corn, and warming spices, with cream cheese and sour cream giving it a thick, creamy finish. Instead of simmering raw chicken thighs as the recipe directs, I’d cook the bean and vegetable base first, then add about three cups of shredded turkey along with the corn near the end. So easy, cheap, and perfect for using up leftover turkey!

Norse Atlantic suspends all flights as it faces potential collapse
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-27
14:09:44 UTC
23:09:44 KST

Norse Atlantic suspends all flights as it faces potential collapse
The Norwegian low cost carrier Norse Atlantic Airways has suspended all flights as it rushes to resolve what it warned is a critical financial situation.
The airline said if no solution can be found, it intends to file for bankruptcy.
It uses a fleet of 12 Boeing 787 aircraft on long-haul routes from a number of European airports, including London Gatwick, Rome and Oslo.
Prior to the suspension of flights, it was advertising services from London to Orlando, Cape Town, Bangkok and Phuket.
Flight disruption
One aircraft was meant to depart London for Orlando this morning, while another was meant to arrive from Cape Town this evening.
The suspension has also affected Pakistan International Airlines, which had been leasing aircraft and crews from Norse Atlantic.
PIA was forced to cancel services from London to Islamabad and from Manchester to Lahore last night.
In a statement on social media, PIA said all UK flights from today would be operated by aircraft from its own fleet.
Londoner Fred was due to fly to Cape Town with Norse Atlantic next week for a friend's wedding, but has decided to change his plans.
"Yesterday the exact same flight I was going to get was cancelled, so I was worried" he told the BBC's Your Voice. "I sent an email to Norse and have had nothing back".
He decided to rebook with a different airline, which cost him an extra £1,000.
Another traveller, Claire from Herefordshire, is currently on holiday in South Africa with her daughter.
"We are due to fly home on Sunday and are now looking at a really expensive flight back," she said.
"We have tried contacting Norse but have been given an automated response saying someone will get back to us within three business days."
Advice for passengers
Consumer group Which? advised Norse Atlantic customers to not cancel their flights as the airline has not collapsed. Doing so removes passengers' right to a refund.
Rory Boland, Which? travel editor, said: "If Norse does go bust, whether you get a refund depends on how you booked and paid. If you booked directly with Norse, you may be able to claim a refund via your credit card provider, or possibly your debit card."
Those stuck abroad should contact insurers, while those with package holidays reliant on Norse Atlantic flights should contact their tour operator, he added.
Low-cost, long-haul
Norse Atlantic Airways was set up in 2021 as a low-cost long-haul carrier.
It entered the market as a successor to Norwegian, which withdrew from long haul routes as a result of the Covid pandemic.
But it has struggled to make money, and its problems worsened this year, as fuel prices rocketed due to the war in Iran.
Unlike many other European carriers, Norse Atlantic does not hedge its fuel bills by buying supplies in advance at fixed prices.
This left it fully exposed when prices soared, with the cost of fuel roughly doubling at one point. Prices remain significantly higher than they were before the conflict began.
In the first half of the year Norse Atlantic made a loss of $94.6m.
In a statement to the Oslo Stock Exchange on Wednesday, the airline said its financial situation was critical, and it needed to find short term funding to continue its operations.
After suspending flights, the company said it was in discussions with investors regarding a possible takeover that "would include a financing solution".
However, it said if short term funding could not be found quickly, "it is the Board's intention to file for bankruptcy".
Norse Atlantic had been looking for a buyer since July.
Its potential collapse follows the failure of Spirit Airlines in May. The US-based low cost carrier was another airline left exposed when jet fuel prices soared, as US airlines typically do not hedge their purchases.
If Norse Atlantic were to fold, it would be another major setback for the low-cost long-haul model, which has typically proved popular with passengers but difficult to sustain financially, especially on transatlantic routes.
Additional reporting by Rozina Sini

Buying a laptop right now is rough, but these Black Friday deals help
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-27
14:03:27 UTC
23:03:27 KST

Laptop prices have been a hot-button topic all year long thanks to RAMageddon. We’ve seen existing models get more expensive and new models arrive at higher prices than last gen — sometimes with lower specs, too. 2026 in general has been a bad time to buy a computer. Thankfully, even with Black Friday winding down, the remaining deals can offer a little relief.
Buying a laptop right now is rough, but these last-minute Black Friday deals help
Despite the year’s bevy of price increases, this is the best time to buy a new laptop (if you absolutely need to).
If the computer you currently use is working well for you, good. Happy for you. But if you’re in the market for something more powerful, bigger, smaller, thinner, etc. — and you prefer new over used — you can currently save some decent dough on MacBooks, Windows notebooks, and gaming laptops.
The big, multi-hundred-dollar savings are typically reserved for higher-end models with bigger specs, like Windows models running Nvidia’s new Arm-based RTX Spark platform — which are getting their first major discounts. More budget-friendly laptops are also receiving decent discounts right now, though, including the MacBook Neo. The current $100 discount on Apple’s budget laptop might seem like small potatoes considering it’s just a return to its original price, but it’s the best you can do for now (if you can’t hold out for a rumored new one in 2027).
As always, the deals below are for laptops we’ve reviewed or tested firsthand in some way at The Verge — either that exact model or a similar one we expect to be equally good. There are many laptops out there and myriad deals running now through Cyber Monday, but if any of these fit your needs, you can’t go wrong.
MacBook Neo
Apple’s most affordable laptop uses the same A18 Pro chip found in the iPhone 16 Pro. It features a vibrant 13-inch display, surprisingly good speakers, and 8GB of RAM, making it a solid option for students and anyone who needs a laptop for everyday tasks. Read our review.
Apple MacBook Air 13 (M5)
Apple’s latest MacBook Air features an M5 chip and starts with 512GB of storage, with significantly faster performance than prior generations. It’s still an excellent machine despite the higher base price, especially given the battery life and speakers. Read our review.
Apple MacBook Pro 14 (2026, M6)
Apple gave its base model MacBook Pro an upgrade to a 12-core M6 chip for 2026, and not a whole lot else. Read our review.
Apple MacBook Pro (M5 Pro / Max, 2026)
The non-OLED M5 Pro and M5 Max versions of the MacBook Pro deliver exceptional power and speed, making them ideal for creative work while offering best-in-class battery life. Read our review.

Making a 3D fantasy map like a 16th-Century atlas
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-27
14:02:52 UTC
23:02:52 KST

Making a Fantasy Map Like a 16th-Century Atlas
Hugo -
Over the last few weeks, I worked on a map of various game dev techniques for people to refer to and learn from. Like, a real map! With each town being a technique's home, provided with a concise, illustrated example. And here we are. Today we are diving into the making of The Game Dev Atlas.
That's so 1570, bro.
If you want to pull off a cohesive art direction, you need to spend most of your time learning about your reference materials. No C code, no IDE, no sub-agents doing things. Just explore and wander on Google Images (old-school, I know), Wikipedia, and online museum galleries.
Gather things, ideas, and visuals! You should feel the inspiration (literally, as you inspire fresh air) inflating your mind, and the first scraps of decisions starting to flow through your head, like: I want a bit of that, none of that, this thing specifically for that part etc.
That is the whole work: what do you want? It's on you to set the quality bar and how things should be.
If you don't already know, typography conveys most of the design weight. That's just it.
Example: after some research I stumbled on that Luminari font that was a great fit for what I wanted my map to look like. It is a paid font, and there were plenty of free alternatives, but not quite as right as the Luminari font. Instead of saying "that's ok, this free one is the same vibe", I just paid for the font I found just right. Never concede terrain on your instinct!
A real island.
Claude, please generate a procedural terr...
Just pick an existing, real island. Nowadays we do have precise elevation data for most islands on Earth. If you want a realistic island, well, pick a real one.
Engraving in real time.
Some styles and colourings I've tried (click on the tabs to compare):
Don't be perfect!
Add some imperfections, color blends, etc. Nothing is perfect in real life, and having an eye for imperfections is what makes things look interesting.
Making towns interesting.
The point of the atlas is to showcase game dev techniques, one per town. So I decided two things:
First, I wanted small visual touches on each town's shape reflecting the technique it's about. For instance, the Procedural Legs town shows a crawling house.
Second, I wanted organic town buildings, so I built a small procedural building system to add diversity to buildings. Risky move: I'm a big fan of Houdini, so I could have thrown away the whole thing, and this blog post would be titled "I'm making Houdini, but running in the browser".
Anyway, here's what my small system can build:
60fps on the web.
The map application itself is written in C using the Kaigen engine as a headless C lib. I'm a Rust guy, but I must admit the compile time freaks me out for game dev with Rust. It took me several iterations to reach smooth 60fps in the browser, especially with the night mode (try it!).
But I'm finally happy with how it runs right now, while I'm still tweaking things atm.
The web app is written in Svelte, because I like Svelte. So the shallow routing and anything UI happen as part of the SvelteKit app.
Conclusion
It was a very fun project! At the end of the day, I love the creative process of making things, shaping ideas like a material, refining details. I strongly believe that when artists leverage AI, y'all will be cooked; because they alone know how to touch the human soul and create the surprise.
The Game Dev Atlas
Acknowledgments and tools used
- Sprite Fusion for the pixel art in the techniques
- Svelte and SvelteKit for the web app
- Luminari by Canada Type for the lettering
- EB Garamond Initials by Georg Duffner (SIL Open Font License) for the illuminated initials
- Kaigen for the engine
- NASA Earth Observatory for the photo of Kerguelen (28 October 2016)
- Terrain Tiles on AWS by Mapzen, and the data sources they name, for Kerguelen's elevation

‘The Book of the Human Body’ Surveys 2,000 Years of Remarkable Anatomical Inquiry
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-27
14:00:00 UTC
23:00:00 KST

‘The Book of the Human Body’ Surveys 2,000 Years of Remarkable Anatomical Inquiry
As long as humans have been around, we’ve been curious about existential questions like, Why are we here? And how do things work? That goes for our own bodies, too. But in our era of modern science and medicine, it’s difficult to imagine a time when physicians weren’t familiar with all of the bones in the skeleton, how a baby grows inside a womb, or how the cardiovascular system works. Even the profession that comes to mind when we think of a doctor didn’t really emerge until the past few centuries. During the medieval period, barber-surgeons were the primary medical practitioners (yes, the people who cut hair also conducted surgeries).
As medicine morphed into a scientific and intellectual pursuit, academies cropped up starting in the late 1700s, such as the College of Philadelphia—the first medical school in the U.S.—and the Edinburgh School of Surgery in Scotland. Ethics were, let’s say, malleable. In Edinburgh, a legal shortage of fresh cadavers used for study in the early 1800s spawned a shady underground trade in bodies, many of which were dug up from new burials in the dead of night. And then there were the famous serial murders committed by William Burke and William Hare, who cashed in by selling well over a dozen of their victims’ corpses to anatomist Robert Knox for use during his lectures. Suffice it to say, some scholars really wanted to understand our innards.
Slated for release in January, The Book of the Human Body delves into the art and science of anatomy, disease, reproduction, and more. Published by TASCHEN, the two-volume set compiles more than 950 images from over 75 rare books and manuscripts from around the world. From ancient analyses to medieval studies to modern diagrams, the anthology surveys the breadth of eras and cultural influences that have shaped our understanding of our physical selves.
Authored by medical historian Richard Barnett, the book celebrates universality and unquenched curiosity. Despite sleeping, breathing, and walking around in our bodies every day, we are often still mystified by much of what makes them work. Throughout history, that sense of wonder has remained constant. Barnett says, “Whatever they might represent, whatever they might express, these extraordinary images have lost none of their power to shock or captivate, to teach or inspire.”
Illustrations include a silk manuscript dated to 168 B.C.E., discovered in a tomb in southern China, which features 44 figures engaged in exercises of “self-cultivation,” plus excerpts from Andreas Vesalius’s anatomical atlas, Fabrica, published in 1543. “In all of these examples, the illustrations stand out not only as objects of medical understanding, but as conspicuous works of art in their own right,” the publisher says.
Pre-order your copy on Bookshop.

Jason Arday's research cleared in investigation
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-27
13:50:05 UTC
22:50:05 KST

Jason Arday research for Queen's University cleared in investigation
Research by academic Jason Arday, who was accused of plagiarism before his death, has been cleared by one of the institutions that was investigating him.
Queen's University Belfast (QUB) began looking into Jason Arday's work after he resigned from his University of Cambridge roles.
The university had published a paper on its academic research site co-authored by Arday and other academics from Queen's.
In a statement, a spokesperson for QUB said: "Having reviewed the matter, we are satisfied that the paper meets the appropriate academic standards and have no concerns regarding its publication."
The paper, published in 2021, was corrected in 2024 to clarify that it used methodologies similar to a previous study Arday had written, which in turn had been based on another research paper published in 2016 by a team of academics at Brighton and Sussex Medical School.
Arday, who became Cambridge's youngest black professor in 2023, was found dead in Battersea on 14 August, having resigned as a professor of sociology of education the week prior.
The 41-year-old's death followed weeks of media coverage over plagiarism allegations and questions about his achievements. He denied the claims. An inquest into his death is under way.
Prime Minister Andy Burnham described the death of the former professor as "a tragedy on so many levels".
Accusations of plagiarism in Arday's work were first voiced by Nathan Cofnas - a US researcher and self-defined "race realist".
Cofnas has previously written that black people are less intelligent than other groups and would "disappear from almost all high-profile positions outside of sports and entertainment" under a meritocratic system.
He claimed on the online publishing platform Substack that he had put Arday's work through a plagiarism detection software, which found that "many passages are lifted with minimal editing, sometimes retaining copy-editing mistakes from the original source".
Following Arday's death, Cofnas was suspended from his post at Ghent University, after it launched an investigation into him "for discriminating against" the former Cambridge professor. He was later handed a three-month teaching ban.
Public figures and friends of Arday had called for a public inquiry into media coverage surrounding the former professor before his death.
Labour MP Diane Abbott and actress Jameela Jamil were among 30,000 signatories of a letter to the prime minister, which claims Arday's death followed "two weeks of relentless harassment".
The University of Cambridge launched a two-part independent review examining the support available to Arday before his death, as well as the wider context of his time at the university.
Liverpool John Moores University - which awarded Arday his PhD in 2015 - concluded that he had not plagiarised work.
Other institutions including Glasgow University, Durham University and Queen's University Belfast all began investigating Arday's work or his appointments to various positions.
Back in August, Queen's University said it was "looking into" a paper, published in 2021, on the impact of what was taught in universities on "Black, Asian and Minority Ethnic (BAME) learners" and "academics of colour".
The paper was called "Attempting to break the chain: Reimaging inclusive pedagogy and decolonising the curriculum within the academy".

You can grab a 12-pack of Zepath’s rechargeable AA batteries for just $24
Each set of AAs includes a clamshell charger, which lets you top off multiple batteries with a single connection.
My colleague Sean Hollister tested Zepath’s higher-capacity 3,600mWh batteries against competing lithium-ion AAs in April, and he picked them as his top choice. In his tests, they even outlasted Panasonic’s pricier Eneloop Pros in both a Game Boy Advance and a high-powered flashlight. Instead of relying on a multi-headed USB-C cable that plugs into each cell, you simply drop them into the included clamshell charging case, like a pair of AirPods or a similar set of wireless earbuds. The case also charges via USB-C, so you can top off eight batteries at once with a single connection in about two and a half hours.
