Paolo Soleri built a utopian eco-city in the Arizona desert
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-27
13:30:20 UTC
22:30:20 KST

From the tops of cypress trees, an assemblage of Brutalist buildings rises amid the high Sonoran Desert in Arizona – Arcosanti, Paolo Soleri’s unfinished, 860-acre prototype city, which began construction in 1970 and is more relevant today than ever. Planned for 5,000 residents, the architect’s dense urban vision favours compactness over sprawl and three-dimensionality over the flatness of suburbia, emerging from the landscape as a series of earth-cast concrete apses and vaults. In reality, numbers have never reached more than 150 and, today, just two dozen people carry on Soleri’s work. It functions more as an experiment in communal living than as the small-scale city he envisaged, but this is precisely what many ‘Arconauts’ are drawn to. They live simply, creatively and collectively. Weekly gatherings, a community room, a library, gym and farmers’ market structure their days. Manny Garcia has lived at Arcosanti for a year. ‘I feel like this is the future,’ he says. ‘Housing is so unaffordable. Co-living cuts costs and offers a built-in community.’
Soleri moved to the USA from Turin in 1946, studying under Frank Lloyd Wright at Taliesin West. The postwar economic boom saw American cities begin to grow exponentially. This impulse for expansion was mirrored by Lloyd Wright’s unrealised Broadacre City, which proposed increased development outside of urban centres so that each person might have their own acre of land. Arcosanti emerged in direct opposition, built on Soleri’s philosophy of ‘arcology’ – architecture fused with ecology – introduced in his 1969 book of that name, which is subtitled The City in the Image of Man. In it he wrote: ‘The liveliness of our world is hindered by the physical extension of our shelters.’ Jeff Stein, an architect and a director of the Cosanti Foundation, which promotes Soleri’s ideals, paraphrases thus: ‘In essence, spreading out kills culture.’
Soleri’s concerns resonate today as much as they did in 1970. Cities continue to swell, yet we are acutely aware of the damaging impact of urban expansion. With average house prices in the USA surpassing $410,000, record high building costs, unaffordable rentals and post-pandemic isolation, the recent surge in shared living arrangements begins to make sense. People are staying in the parental home or flat-sharing for longer out of necessity. Some are exploring communes and ecologically minded ‘intentional communities’. High-end manifestations of the trend, such as Ark in London’s Canary Wharf – a 21-storey residential tower with shared kitchens, work spaces, a pool, spa and rooftop restaurant – are a far cry from Soleri’s pursuit of ‘elegant frugality’, but they do signal a desire for connection.
Yvonne Brook, an artist, shares a two-bedroom unit in Arcosanti’s East Crescent Wing – a common arrangement in the development, where housing is generally assigned by a committee according to seniority, and one that offers Yvonne many advantages. ‘It’s easier to talk over cooking dinner and eating together. Having people to bounce ideas off of helps keep me moving forward,’ she says.
In October 2026, amid Britain’s housing crisis, the William Morris Gallery in London will present An Earthly Paradise, exploring 200 years of living experiments, housing activism and emerging social structures. Hadrian Garrard, the museum’s director, suggests that a broader shift away from traditional family units has contributed to the rise in alternative living arrangements. ‘Here in the West, we have moved to a more fluid way of thinking about gender identity,’ he says. Polyamory and other relationship structures are now in the mainstream… To think about how we are to live is to think about where we want to live and with whom.
‘This is by no means new but is perhaps fuelled by two contemporary factors – the ongoing global housing crises, where sharing housing costs with others just makes more sense, and an epidemic of loneliness in Western culture.’ Soleri’s question endures: how can we plan for this? In an age of digital connection but physical separation, Arcosanti remains an antidote – an experiment in density and shared humanity.
‘We survive on giving people a chance to experience that,’ says David Tollas, who arrived in 1981. He met his wife, Nadia Begin, during a workshop, and together they raised two sons among the compound’s courtyards and half-domed rooms. At first, he lived in one of the small ‘cubes’, spartan concrete dwellings scattered below the mesa. Today those early quarters resemble a ghost village. He and Nadia now occupy an earthen-domed home behind the foundry, where art, books and rolls of architectural plans fill the shelves. Much of Arcosanti’s furniture, its pictures and ceramics, are generational and have moved through various spaces. ‘The stuff in our place that has been saved over the years represents all the people who have made up the community,’ says Tollas.
Larger homes are generally assigned to longer-term residents, but sometimes people get lucky. Garcia lives alone in a five-bedroom unit and takes a ‘whatever you can get approach’ to decorating. Phoenix is an hour’s drive away, so Arconauts often rely on repurposed items left in the community space – a repository of clothes, appliances and furniture.
‘Arcosanti is traditionally about minimal living – a deliberate poverty of living simply for the sake of equity,’ says Ray Paskus, a resident of two years and an archivist for the Cosanti Foundation. Perhaps this is why so many co-living projects fail to last the journey. Arcosanti is the exception, not the rule – and it’s hardly thriving. Less than 10 per cent of Soleri’s masterplan has been realised and, despite a broader interest in communal living, Paskus says applications are at a standstill. ‘People project all sorts of things on to Arcosanti, but the lifestyle is harsh and isolating and full of hard work – it’s almost monastic. To grow the community you have to be able to sell that to people.’ There are few, he’s found, that are truly interested in the asceticism needed to make earth-conscious co-operative living work.
It’s a shame, he says, because a philosophy of reuse and collaborative living could be one solution to the ills of our time. Soleri died in 2013 at the age of 93, still convinced that ‘we must reinvent the city to develop our civilisation’. Arcosanti endures as that living sketch – unfinished, alive and quietly radical.
Edit: It is important to note that allegations of abuse have been made by Daniela Soleri about her father, the architect Paolo Soleri.
For more information, visit arcosanti.org. A version of this story appears in the March 2026 issue of ‘The World of Interiors’. Learn about our subscription offers

====================================================================================================

She Reported Her Sexual Assault. The Marine Corps Kicked Her Out
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-27
13:06:34 UTC
22:06:34 KST

Marina was training at the Basic School in Quantico, Virginia, when she attended the Marine Corps Ball in November 2023. The annual celebration of the Corps’ birthday was supposed to be a night of revelry and camaraderie. Instead, Marina said, a lieutenant sexually assaulted her at knifepoint.
“It should have been the safest place to be,” she said, “and it was quite literally the most dangerous.”
At Marina’s request, The War Horse is using a pseudonym to protect her identity. Her identity was verified and parts of her account were corroborated through interviews and documents, including a military protective order against her alleged assailant, her retirement and discharge paperwork, and emails from the Marine Corps Special Trial Counsel. The War Horse does not identify survivors of sexual assault without their explicit consent.
Marina initially hesitated to report the assault.
“I had not heard great things in the past [about] the way people were treated after reporting this kind of thing,” she said.
Marina would later say that what her command put her through was worse than the assault itself.
Within about a week, she decided to tell military authorities about the attack. First she filed a “restricted” report, which would allow her to receive support and medical care without automatically triggering an investigation, but ultimately changed her report to an “unrestricted” report, which launched an investigation and notified the leaders of her unit.
“I did it more for my own peace of mind than anything else,” she said, “because I genuinely don’t think I could have slept at night knowing what I did, and then just letting him walk around.”
Marina struggled in the months afterward. Mental health challenges prevented her from continuing in training, and she eventually sought treatment for post-traumatic stress disorder.
“I hadn’t been doing great,” she said, “but I was working really hard to get better.”
She thought the Naval Criminal Investigative Service would thoroughly investigate the matter, and the Office of Special Trial Counsel, which launched about seven weeks after her assault to investigate violent offenses, would prosecute her assailant.
Instead, five months later, Marina received a shocking letter: She was being kicked out of the military—not the lieutenant who had allegedly sexually assaulted her. It would be an administrative separation, which the military uses to involuntarily separate service members with a lower burden of proof than a court-martial.
Marina’s paperwork cited a military doctor’s recommendation that she should be discharged after dealing with mental health issues from her assault. The official diagnosis, and the reason for the discharge, was “adjustment disorder with depressed mood.” Her commander recommended that she receive a general instead of an honorable discharge, which would strip her of veterans’ education benefits.
Her name was also entered into the Marine Officer Disciplinary Notebook, also known as the “Black Book,” a database the Marine Corps uses to track officer misconduct.
“Whatever happened initially from that assault has just been insurmountably added to by the way my command treated me,” Marina said. “If you’re put under an administrative discharge, everybody just automatically assumes that you’re a criminal.”
Marina hired a lawyer to fight the discharge and to have her name removed from the Black Book. It took years of appeals, but she was finally able to leave the Marines in the spring of 2026 with a medical retirement for post-traumatic stress disorder, instead of the administrative discharge.
It was a bitter victory for Marina, who had only about a week to pack up her apartment on base and had to stay with her parents for a couple of weeks before finding an apartment.
Marina’s story isn’t unique.
In 2021, the Pentagon launched a coordinated effort to combat sexual assault in the military. But even after organizational changes, service members are still being discharged not long after reporting their assaults, an investigation from The War Horse found.
Over the past five years, 30 Marines—12 women and 18 men—have been kicked out of the military through the administrative discharge process within two years of reporting their sexual assault, according to public records obtained by The War Horse. Most were discharged for “misconduct,” classified as either drug or alcohol abuse or another serious offense, though some were separated for “unsatisfactory performance” or medical conditions that did not rise to the level of disability.
This process is often overseen by the survivor’s chain of command. Advocates for military sexual assault victims say the practice is military-wide, but is especially common in the Marines, the most male-dominated military branch.
One of the survivors who spoke with The War Horse said she was told less than a year after reporting her sexual assault that she would be administratively separated, a potential red flag for retaliation highlighted in Department of Defense regulations that can warrant review from a senior officer. Another was told of her separation 13 months after her assault, just outside that window.
“We strictly enforce statutory and regulatory protections to ensure all Marines can report sexual misconduct, free from fear of retaliation,” a Marine Corps spokesperson said when asked for comment on Marines who have been administratively separated after reporting sexual assault.
“Victims of sexual assault are provided with an independent Victim’s Legal Counsel to advocate for and defend a victim’s rights, including protection from any retaliation. Claims of retaliation are referred to the Naval Criminal Investigative Service or the [Department of Defense] Office of Inspector General for independent investigation,” the spokesperson said.
“I have no doubt that every woman who will report a sexual assault understands that it could cost them their career,” said Rita Graham, policy director at Service Women’s Action Network (SWAN), an advocacy group for women in the military. “Survivors should not have to choose between reporting a crime and staying in uniform.”
Service members and members of the military community affected by sexual assault can contact the DoD Safe Helpline for confidential support 24/7 at 877-995-5247 or through online chat at SafeHelpline.org. Anyone affected by sexual assault can also contact RAINN’s National Sexual Assault Hotline at 800-656-HOPE (4673), text “HOPE” to 64673, or chat at RAINN.org.
An ‘Inconceivable’ Amount of Power
Administrative separations have long been a tool used by the military to get rid of those it perceives as causing trouble, according to victim advocates and researchers.
In contrast to a court-martial, the military’s version of a legal trial, there are no rules of evidence or procedural law during an administrative separation, said Francesca Graham, vice president of Walk the Talk Foundation, a nonprofit that defends military whistleblowers.
Most courts-martial are overseen by a judge and decided by a panel—similar to a jury in a civilian criminal trial—but administrative separations often allow for a service member’s immediate chain of command to decide their fate.
“The amount of power that is inherent in this process, it’s almost inconceivable,” Graham said.
The use of courts-martial has declined sharply in recent decades, raising concerns that military commanders have increasingly relied on administrative separations to address misconduct. In 2018, Defense Secretary Jim Mattis warned commanders against taking the easier route over pursuing military justice proceedings.
“Administrative actions should not be the default method to address illicit conduct simply because it is less burdensome than the military justice system,” Mattis wrote.
The U.S. military’s treatment of sexual assault survivors has also faced international scrutiny. In 2015, advocates filed petitions with the Inter-American Commission on Human Rights, alleging that the U.S. military failed to adequately address sexual violence and retaliated against service members who reported it. Similar concerns have been raised before the United Nations.
“We have clients who’ve said, ‘The trauma of the sexual assault that I experienced, it was horrific,’” said Elizabeth Brundige, a law professor at Cornell University’s Gender Justice Clinic who represents over two dozen military sexual assault victims. “But it was really the lack of response and retaliation by my superiors that was the real torture or the real violation that I felt.”
Have you been retaliated against for reporting a sexual assault in the military?
The War Horse is continuing to report on military sexual assault and retaliation. If you have firsthand knowledge, documents, internal communications, or other information that could help our reporting, email us at tips@thewarhorse.org or learn how to submit a tip securely here. We take source protection seriously and will not publish your name without your permission.
Service members with at least six years of military service and those at risk of an other-than-honorable discharge can request to appear before an administrative separation board of three other service members—usually two officers and a senior enlisted member—to make their case. Otherwise, the decision to retain them rests solely with their chain of command.
“You’re just really relying on a commander to make the right call, and sometimes they do, sometimes they don’t, and that’s when it gets really scary,” said Rita Graham from SWAN.
Administrative discharge can have long-term consequences. Though some are characterized as honorable and general discharges, those discharged with other-than-honorable discharges—known colloquially as “bad paper”—can face lifelong challenges.
“It marks you for life,” said Stephanie Schroeder, a Marine veteran who reported being sexually assaulted in 2002 and was later discharged from the service with a personality disorder, despite never being seen by a therapist to diagnose the condition. “Any type of federal job you want, any type of background check, it shows up.”
It’s difficult to determine exactly how many have been impacted by “bad paper” discharges, but a 2025 report from the Government Accountability Office found that between January 2018 and March 2024, more than 3,000 service members petitioned for discharge upgrades with cases that involved sexual harassment or assault. A 2016 Human Rights Watch report also found that potentially thousands of service members had been administratively discharged between 1960 and 2015 with bad paper in retaliation for reporting sexual assault.
“If people are still being punished for reporting a sexual assault, then that is a huge problem,” said Sara Darehshori, who authored the Human Rights Watch report.
In 2025, about 6.4% of active-duty Marine Corps women said they had been sexually assaulted in the prior 12 months, according to the Pentagon’s report on sexual assault in the military—more than any other branch. The number of men in the Marine Corps who experienced a sexual assault was 1%, on par with other branches.
“They say the Marines are top-notch when they’re doing something. Whether it’s good or bad, they’re gonna do it 150%,” said Amy Franck, a former Army sexual assault program manager and founder of Never Alone, a nonprofit organization that provides resources to survivors of military sexual assault. “I have never seen such strategic, in-sync retaliation.”
According to Franck, those 30 Marines who were administratively discharged over the past five years don’t show the full picture. The figure doesn’t take into account people whom the Marines tried and failed to administratively separate, or those who, like Marina, used a lawyer to change the administrative discharge to another type of discharge, like a medical discharge.
When it comes to Marines who have been sexually assaulted and then threatened with an administrative discharge, “I can count 10 myself,” Franck said.
‘I Still Mourn the Life That I Wanted’
In the fall of 2021, Rosemary, a 24-year-old Marine stationed at Camp Pendleton, went to a local bar with a male colleague and his friends. Afterward, she offered to let him stay on a blow-up mattress in her apartment, which was closer to downtown, instead of driving back to his place after a night of drinking.
When she woke up the next morning, her body felt sore and she was surprised to find her underwear on backward.
That day, a friend took Rosemary to a military medical center for a forensic sexual assault exam. Rosemary brought along her underwear and a towel left on the floor. Then she reported the assault to the Naval Criminal Investigative Service. The agency opened an investigation and conducted a digital forensics download of her phone.
In March 2022, the NCIS investigation determined that “no further investigative action was deemed necessary.” But like Marina, several months later Rosemary learned that she was under investigation herself.
Rosemary is married to another service member, who had been training on a different base on the night of the alleged assault. Now, she was being accused of lying about her sexual assault and committing adultery, which is a crime in the military.
“This can’t be real,” she remembered thinking. “You’re kidding.”
The War Horse interviewed Rosemary multiple times, corroborated parts of her story with a friend, and reviewed dozens of pages of records, including the NCIS investigation, an inspector general complaint, her notification of administrative separation, and excerpts from her journal.
The War Horse also reviewed Rosemary’s military fitness reports and showed them to a retired Marine colonel. He said the evaluations showed that Rosemary had been in good standing before the alleged assault, with her commanding officer continuously recommending her for promotion.
Yet in March 2023, Rosemary’s commander recommended her for administrative separation over the allegations of adultery and lying about her sexual assault. The paperwork cited “substandard performance of duty, misconduct, and moral or professional dereliction.”
Her case went before an administrative discharge board in the spring of 2024. At the hearing, Rosemary explained the assault to three strangers who would decide her fate in the military.
“I couldn’t run from the truth anymore. He chose me. … He raped me. And I was going to implode,” she wrote in her journal the day before the hearing.
Amy Franck, Rosemary’s victim advocate, was in the room with her that day, incensed.
“Her husband doesn’t think that she was cheating,” Franck told The War Horse. “So how the fuck does the Marine Corps think she was cheating?”
The board voted 2-1 to allow Rosemary to stay in the Marines.
By that point, however, she had already made her choice.
“I was done,” she said.
She voluntarily left the Marines six months later when her contract ended with an honorable discharge.
Rosemary had wanted to be in the Marine Corps since she was 8 years old. She had worked out with the Marine Corps delayed-entry program while in high school, then received an ROTC scholarship for college. She intended to stay in until retirement, but that plan changed.
“I loved it,” she said. “I still mourn the life that I wanted.”
No More ‘Walking on Eggshells’
Last year, Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth ordered a review of the military’s equal opportunity programs, directing officials to dismiss complaints lacking credible evidence and protect service members from knowingly false allegations.
The changes were part of a broader effort Hegseth described as ending a military culture of “walking on eggshells.”
In October 2026, Hegseth announced further changes to the military’s inspector general complaint process, which handles allegations of misconduct and abuses of authority.
“Commanders are now back in charge,” he said, describing the inspector general process as having been “weaponized.” He said investigations would no longer be launched based on “speculation, personal gripes, or hearsay.”
But Pentagon data show that false sexual assault claims are exceptionally rare. In 2025, the military found that only about 1% of cases were “unfounded,” meaning investigators determined the allegations were false or baseless.
Hegseth’s policy changes marked a major shift. For several years, the military was attempting to reform how it handled sexual assault and harassment complaints. Those changes followed years of pressure from survivors and advocates, intensified by the 2020 murder of Army Spc. Vanessa Guillén at Fort Hood, Texas. An Army investigation later found that Guillén had reported sexual harassment before her death but supervisors failed to elevate the complaints.
In 2021, at President Joe Biden’s direction, Defense Secretary Lloyd Austin ordered an independent review of sexual assault in the military. Its nearly 300-page report made more than 80 recommendations, including moving the responsibility for prosecuting serious crimes, like sexual assaults, away from commanders.
Lindsey Knapp, an Army veteran, attorney, and advocate for military sexual assault survivors, said Hegseth’s changes would discourage service members from coming forward.
“That creates a chilling effect for people already trying to report in an environment where reporting sexual assault was already horrendous,” Knapp said.
But several military lawyers interviewed by The War Horse disagreed, saying the pendulum had swung too far in favor of sexual assault survivors.
“A lot of times I do feel as though people’s careers are ruined over accusations that, in my opinion, are not supported by the preponderance of the evidence,” said Matthew Barry, a former Army judge advocate.
Gary Barthel, a retired Marine judge advocate, said he has never seen negative action taken against an alleged sexual assault victim just because of their report.
Typically, he said, survivors face charges when they also engage in misconduct, like underage drinking. In that case, Barthel said it was fair to discipline the survivor.
“I think they have to be held accountable if there’s some misconduct,” he said. “Because really, the misconduct that’s associated with it kind of put them in a position that caused them to become a victim.” (That view conflicts with the military’s Safe-to-Report policy, which since 2022 has barred commanders from disciplining sexual assault survivors for minor collateral misconduct such as underage drinking.)
While reports of sexual assault in the military have decreased between 2023 and 2025, the small amount of public data that exists shows that retaliation remains a military-wide problem.
In its annual report on sexual assault, the Pentagon found that more than 50 service members, most of whom were junior enlisted and women, said they were retaliated against after reporting their assault. They typically said the person retaliating against them was “a superior in the chain of command.”
Only 16 of those complaints were investigated. None resulted in disciplinary action against the alleged attacker.
“There is a long, pervasive tradition of this happening,” said Josh Connolly, senior vice president of Protect Our Defenders, a nonprofit group that works to end sexual misconduct in the military, “and it is still occurring.”
12 Pullups
Elizabeth’s appointment with a Navy doctor in the fall of 2023 seemed routine. The doctor, a lieutenant commander, and a Navy corpsman listened as she described feeling nerve pain after a car crash.
Then the doctor asked the corpsman to leave. After the door closed, Elizabeth said, he began touching her inappropriately over her shorts.
Elizabeth quickly reported the assault, and the next day NCIS opened an investigation that lasted seven months. The doctor refused to be interviewed, invoking his right to remain silent. NCIS ultimately closed the case, citing “insufficient evidence for probable cause.”
In the months afterward, Elizabeth said instructors at the Basic School refused to coach her. She was also ordered not to enter the building where her sexual assault victim advocate worked.
“I reported it because I thought it was the right thing to do,” Elizabeth said. “I ended up regretting it.”
Then, in August 2024, Elizabeth took a physical fitness test. She reported completing 12 pullups—as she had several times in the past, according to military records—but the sergeant overseeing the pullup test later claimed she had only done five, fewer than she had ever done.
Two weeks later, Elizabeth was pulled aside during a field exercise and told she was under investigation for allegedly falsifying her physical fitness test score.
Elizabeth had no reason to lie. Even if she had completed only five pullups, that still would have been enough to pass the test. She said her commander had allowed everyone else in her unit to retake the test but denied her the same opportunity.
Soon after, Elizabeth received a disciplinary counseling. About six weeks later, her commanding officer, a colonel, recommended her for administrative separation.
The War Horse interviewed Elizabeth multiple times, and reviewed hundreds of pages of records, including the NCIS investigation into her alleged assault, a command-directed investigation into the fitness test allegations, her administrative separation notification, and 16 letters from current and former service members recommending that she be retained.
Elizabeth fought the administrative separation, filing multiple appeals. But because she had served fewer than six years, she was not entitled to present her case before an administrative separation board.
“All they had to do was let her take the physical fitness test over,” said her lawyer, Ruth Cresenzo, a former Army National Guard judge advocate and executive director of CHANCE, a nonprofit organization that provides legal advocacy to crime victims.
Cresenzo said the ordeal was “straight up retaliation” and “the craziest case I’ve ever seen.”
In September 2026, Elizabeth learned she would not be granted an administrative separation board hearing. Instead, she was honorably discharged from the Marines in October. But her discharge paperwork, known as a DD-214, cites misconduct for a “serious offense” as the reason for her separation.
Now out of the Marine Corps, Elizabeth is trying to find a new job and move out of her apartment. She doesn’t know where she’ll end up.
Still, she hasn’t given up on being a Marine. She plans to petition the Board of Corrections of Naval Records, hoping to be reinstated.
“People have asked me what I want to do on the outside, and I don’t have an answer,” Elizabeth said. “This is what I want to do.”
This article from The War Horse was edited by Paul Szoldra, fact-checked by Jess Rohan, and copy-edited by Mitchell Hansen-Dewar.

She Reported Her Sexual Assault. The Marine Corps Kicked Her Out
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-27
13:06:34 UTC
22:06:34 KST

Marina was training at the Basic School in Quantico, Virginia, when she attended the Marine Corps Ball in November 2023. The annual celebration of the Corps’ birthday was supposed to be a night of revelry and camaraderie. Instead, Marina said, a lieutenant sexually assaulted her at knifepoint.
“It should have been the safest place to be,” she said, “and it was quite literally the most dangerous.”
At Marina’s request, The War Horse is using a pseudonym to protect her identity. Her identity was verified and parts of her account were corroborated through interviews and documents, including a military protective order against her alleged assailant, her retirement and discharge paperwork, and emails from the Marine Corps Special Trial Counsel. The War Horse does not identify survivors of sexual assault without their explicit consent.
Marina initially hesitated to report the assault.
“I had not heard great things in the past [about] the way people were treated after reporting this kind of thing,” she said.
Marina would later say that what her command put her through was worse than the assault itself.
Within about a week, she decided to tell military authorities about the attack. First she filed a “restricted” report, which would allow her to receive support and medical care without automatically triggering an investigation, but ultimately changed her report to an “unrestricted” report, which launched an investigation and notified the leaders of her unit.
“I did it more for my own peace of mind than anything else,” she said, “because I genuinely don’t think I could have slept at night knowing what I did, and then just letting him walk around.”
Marina struggled in the months afterward. Mental health challenges prevented her from continuing in training, and she eventually sought treatment for post-traumatic stress disorder.
“I hadn’t been doing great,” she said, “but I was working really hard to get better.”
She thought the Naval Criminal Investigative Service would thoroughly investigate the matter, and the Office of Special Trial Counsel, which launched about seven weeks after her assault to investigate violent offenses, would prosecute her assailant.
Instead, five months later, Marina received a shocking letter: She was being kicked out of the military—not the lieutenant who had allegedly sexually assaulted her. It would be an administrative separation, which the military uses to involuntarily separate service members with a lower burden of proof than a court-martial.
Marina’s paperwork cited a military doctor’s recommendation that she should be discharged after dealing with mental health issues from her assault. The official diagnosis, and the reason for the discharge, was “adjustment disorder with depressed mood.” Her commander recommended that she receive a general instead of an honorable discharge, which would strip her of veterans’ education benefits.
Her name was also entered into the Marine Officer Disciplinary Notebook, also known as the “Black Book,” a database the Marine Corps uses to track officer misconduct.
“Whatever happened initially from that assault has just been insurmountably added to by the way my command treated me,” Marina said. “If you’re put under an administrative discharge, everybody just automatically assumes that you’re a criminal.”
Marina hired a lawyer to fight the discharge and to have her name removed from the Black Book. It took years of appeals, but she was finally able to leave the Marines in the spring of 2026 with a medical retirement for post-traumatic stress disorder, instead of the administrative discharge.
It was a bitter victory for Marina, who had only about a week to pack up her apartment on base and had to stay with her parents for a couple of weeks before finding an apartment.
Marina’s story isn’t unique.
In 2021, the Pentagon launched a coordinated effort to combat sexual assault in the military. But even after organizational changes, service members are still being discharged not long after reporting their assaults, an investigation from The War Horse found.
Over the past five years, 30 Marines—12 women and 18 men—have been kicked out of the military through the administrative discharge process within two years of reporting their sexual assault, according to public records obtained by The War Horse. Most were discharged for “misconduct,” classified as either drug or alcohol abuse or another serious offense, though some were separated for “unsatisfactory performance” or medical conditions that did not rise to the level of disability.
This process is often overseen by the survivor’s chain of command. Advocates for military sexual assault victims say the practice is military-wide, but is especially common in the Marines, the most male-dominated military branch.
One of the survivors who spoke with The War Horse said she was told less than a year after reporting her sexual assault that she would be administratively separated, a potential red flag for retaliation highlighted in Department of Defense regulations that can warrant review from a senior officer. Another was told of her separation 13 months after her assault, just outside that window.
“We strictly enforce statutory and regulatory protections to ensure all Marines can report sexual misconduct, free from fear of retaliation,” a Marine Corps spokesperson said when asked for comment on Marines who have been administratively separated after reporting sexual assault.
“Victims of sexual assault are provided with an independent Victim’s Legal Counsel to advocate for and defend a victim’s rights, including protection from any retaliation. Claims of retaliation are referred to the Naval Criminal Investigative Service or the [Department of Defense] Office of Inspector General for independent investigation,” the spokesperson said.
“I have no doubt that every woman who will report a sexual assault understands that it could cost them their career,” said Rita Graham, policy director at Service Women’s Action Network (SWAN), an advocacy group for women in the military. “Survivors should not have to choose between reporting a crime and staying in uniform.”
Service members and members of the military community affected by sexual assault can contact the DoD Safe Helpline for confidential support 24/7 at 877-995-5247 or through online chat at SafeHelpline.org. Anyone affected by sexual assault can also contact RAINN’s National Sexual Assault Hotline at 800-656-HOPE (4673), text “HOPE” to 64673, or chat at RAINN.org.
An ‘Inconceivable’ Amount of Power
Administrative separations have long been a tool used by the military to get rid of those it perceives as causing trouble, according to victim advocates and researchers.
In contrast to a court-martial, the military’s version of a legal trial, there are no rules of evidence or procedural law during an administrative separation, said Francesca Graham, vice president of Walk the Talk Foundation, a nonprofit that defends military whistleblowers.
Most courts-martial are overseen by a judge and decided by a panel—similar to a jury in a civilian criminal trial—but administrative separations often allow for a service member’s immediate chain of command to decide their fate.
“The amount of power that is inherent in this process, it’s almost inconceivable,” Graham said.
The use of courts-martial has declined sharply in recent decades, raising concerns that military commanders have increasingly relied on administrative separations to address misconduct. In 2018, Defense Secretary Jim Mattis warned commanders against taking the easier route over pursuing military justice proceedings.
“Administrative actions should not be the default method to address illicit conduct simply because it is less burdensome than the military justice system,” Mattis wrote.

Similar concerns have been raised before the United Nations.

12 Pullups

Elizabeth had no reason to lie. Even if she had completed only five pullups, that still would have been enough to pass the test. She said her commander had allowed everyone else in her unit to retake the test but denied her the same opportunity.
Soon after, Elizabeth received a disciplinary counseling. About six weeks later, her commanding officer, a colonel, recommended her for administrative separation.
The War Horse interviewed Elizabeth multiple times, and reviewed hundreds of pages of records, including the NCIS investigation into her alleged assault, a command-directed investigation into the fitness test allegations, her administrative separation notification, and 16 letters from current and former service members recommending that she be retained.
Elizabeth fought the administrative separation, filing multiple appeals. But because she had served fewer than six years, she was not entitled to present her case before an administrative separation board.
“All they had to do was let her take the physical fitness test over,” said her lawyer, Ruth Cresenzo, a former Army National Guard judge advocate and executive director of CHANCE, a nonprofit organization that provides legal advocacy to crime victims.
Cresenzo said the ordeal was “straight up retaliation” and “the craziest case I’ve ever seen.”
In September 2026, Elizabeth learned she would not be granted an administrative separation board hearing. Instead, she was honorably discharged from the Marines in October. But her discharge paperwork, known as a DD-214, cites misconduct for a “serious offense” as the reason for her separation.
Now out of the Marine Corps, Elizabeth is trying to find a new job and move out of her apartment. She doesn’t know where she’ll end up.
Still, she hasn’t given up on being a Marine. She plans to petition the Board of Corrections of Naval Records, hoping to be reinstated.
“People have asked me what I want to do on the outside, and I don’t have an answer,” Elizabeth said. “This is what I want to do.”
This article from The War Horse was edited by Paul Szoldra, fact-checked by Jess Rohan, and copy-edited by Mitchell Hansen-Dewar.

New York and California Age Gating Rules Go into Effect in the New Year
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-27
12:43:40 UTC
21:43:40 KST

Come January 2027, everyday social media browsing in two of the country's largest states will look noticeably different. In California, AB 1709 (2026), AB 1043 (2025), and SB 976 (2024) are slated to take effect on January 1, 2027. Meanwhile, state attorney general regulations for New York’s Stop Addictive Feeds Exploitation (SAFE) for Kids Act take effect on January 25, 2027.
EFF has opposed these laws across legislatures, regulatory proceedings, and the courts. The core problem is simple: age-gating mandates trade away everyday user privacy and free expression under the guise of safety, while pushing platforms to gather even more personal data on users.
What California’s Rules Say
Three interlocking California laws take effect on January 1, 2027, reshaping how platforms handle age checks and design features for young users. First, SB 976 (2024) requires verifiable parental consent before platforms can offer algorithmic feeds or nighttime notifications to anyone under 18. Second, AB 1709 (2026) goes even further by outright banning "addictive features," including personalized recommendation algorithms, autoplay, and endless scroll, for users under 16, even with parental permission. Third, the Digital Age Assurance Act (AB 1043, 2025) mandates age-assurance checks across devices (operating systems) and apps, serving as the enforcement backbone for those restrictions. Together with the California Attorney General’s proposed rules on SB 976, which outline age-gating methods, accuracy, circumvention, and parental consent, these laws force platforms into a two-tier design that walls young people off from basic content-discovery tools, puts everyone's privacy at risk to prove their age, and has already drawn First Amendment lawsuits from social media companies against both SB 976 and AB 1709.
For example: rather than minimizing corporate surveillance and data collection by passing stronger privacy laws, the SB 976 regulations encourage companies to evaluate behavioral patterns, collected user data, digital interactions, and metadata to estimate age (Sec. 561(a)(2)), alongside location data collected for other purposes (Sec. 565). Good privacy laws should discourage collecting that information in the first place, not reward broader tracking.
At the same time, these age assurance rules build in steep error rates and weak safeguards. They acknowledge that age-verification tools can misclassify young people as adults, allowing false-positive rates of up to 8% for 16-year-olds and 15% for 17-year-olds (Sec. 561(c)(4)(iv)). Yet the rules set no benchmarks at all for false negatives, meaning adults (especially 18- to 20-year-olds) are likely to be wrongly flagged as under 18. They also recognize demographic bias in facial recognition, yet offer no concrete fix, vaguely telling companies to take mitigating measures like correcting dataset imbalances (Sec. 563(a)(6)). While users can appeal wrong age estimates, the rules set no firm deadlines, meaning mistakes can drag on without a prompt fix, blocking user access (Sec. 564). Worse, requiring platforms to let users report others they suspect are under the age of 18 (Sec. 567) invites targeted harassment by bad actors, while rules telling companies to account for users trying to “conceal or misrepresent” their California location (Sec. 565) risk penalizing everyday VPN users.
What New York’s Rules Say
Along the same lines as the California regulations, New York’s SAFE for Kids Act restricts personalized recommendations and nighttime notifications (between midnight and 6am) without required consent, and mandates that platforms run age assurance and determine user location. If a user does not verify their age or is estimated to be under-18, they will either be relegated to an account with no personalized recommendations and nighttime notifications (if the platform creates one), or prohibited from accessing the platform/service.
As we detailed in our December 2025 comments to the New York Attorney General, age-verification mandates inevitably chill access to online speech. And the final regulations introduce several troubling specifics.
The New York regulations use broad definitions for "age estimation," "age inference," and "age verification" (700.1(h)–(j)) that push companies to collect and retain more personal information from users. Even where privacy protections apply to that information, users cannot sue or enforce those rights themselves, since enforcement rests solely with the Attorney General (700.8). Worse, the rule’s definition of deletion appears to let companies "de-identify" age-assurance data and keep using it in that form, potentially feeding AI model training and creating re-identification risks if leaked or stolen. At the same time, platforms are required to hold onto records (including a user's age status and compliance data) for five years, increasing the risk of misuse or breaches (700.7(b)).
Echoing the problems in California, location and accuracy regulations bring further privacy and security risks. The regulations demand that operators must weigh all reliable location information and take reasonable steps to detect user efforts to “conceal or misrepresent” being in New York, which expands location tracking and threatens VPN use (700.2(d)(2)). On accuracy, the rules permit up to a 15% false-positive rate for 17-year-olds (700.1(b)), yet just like the California rules, say nothing about false negatives—meaning adults, especially 18- to 20-year-olds, are likely to be wrongly flagged as under 18. When that happens, the appeals process can take longer than 10 days, leaving misclassified adults locked out of standard features (or platforms altogether) for over a week.
What This Means for Users in NY and CA
Under both states' regimes, many users will be pressed to hand over personal information just to access standard features like personalized feeds or notifications, while social media companies are encouraged to monitor (and monetize) user behavior even more closely. Because these laws define "addictive feeds" so broadly that the label covers virtually every basic recommendation tool (including feeds built from who you follow, posts you like, or topics you say you are interested in), platforms face a stark choice: either build stripped-down versions of their apps without algorithmic feeds, likes, or notifications, or simply boot any account suspected of belonging to someone under 18. Either way, all users, and especially young people across both states, risk being shut out of major online spaces as they exist today.
When young people lose accounts or get pushed into stripped-down experiences, they lose contact with friends, support groups, community organizing, local news, creative audiences, and ways to speak out on current issues. U.S. law has long recognized that people under 18 have their own constitutional rights to speak and receive information, and sweeping age gates directly interfere with those rights.
At the same time, lawmakers often sell age-verification mandates as a way to rein in Big Tech, yet the opposite happens in practice. Megacorporations like Meta and Google have the resources to build expensive age-estimation systems and absorb steep fines (such as $50,000 per violation in California) while they figure out compliance. Smaller platforms and independent communities such as Bluesky or Dreamwidth, however, cannot afford those legal risks or engineering costs. As we have already seen in states like Mississippi, crushing compliance burdens push smaller sites out of the market and drive users back toward the biggest corporate giants. Instead of holding Big Tech accountable, these rules entrench its dominance; leaving everyone with fewer community options, less innovation, and a more centralized surveillance machine.
Help Us Help You
Legislative and regulatory action are setbacks, but they do not settle the debate. If these rules affect you in California, New York, or anywhere else: keep a record while the details are still fresh, and share it with our team at agegates@eff.org.
Take screenshots and note the date, time, service, and what you were trying to do; write down what you lost (whether that means an account, a club or volunteer group, contact with friends, local news, or a favorite creator) or note if you chose to walk away from a service rather than submit an ID or personal data. You do not need to hand over sensitive information to show that these rules burdened you. If you hit an age check or parental-consent snag, save what the platform asked for, whether it worked or failed, any privacy promises or laws cited, and any appeal attempts.
If these restrictions get in your way, email EFF at agegates@eff.org and tell us what happened. Tracking real-world lockouts, age-check failures, and infringements on free speech puts us in a stronger position to challenge the worst of these restrictions in legislatures and the courts. At the same time, we will keep fighting for laws that actually make people safer online by curbing corporate surveillance and giving parents, youth, and all internet users meaningful choice and control.

====================================================================================================

Kimi for Science More scientific ideas, made possible
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-27
12:03:11 UTC
21:03:11 KST

More resources for your research
Selected applicants will receive up to $20,000 in Kimi API credits (approx. 7B tokens), with the exact amount determined by research needs and review outcomes.
Science has always been driven by human curiosity, rigorous inquiry, and a passion for the unknown
A valuable hypothesis can require extensive literature review, data analysis, and repeated validation. An unexpected result can also spark the next breakthrough. Yet limited time, compute, and people mean many ideas worth exploring never receive the attention they deserve. We believe AI can accelerate research and expand the boundaries of discovery, helping researchers move beyond constraints of time, resources, and methodology and bringing more ambitious ideas within reach
From life sciences and health to energy and materials, and from fundamental science to the unknown, countless important questions remain unanswered. We hope Kimi can be a trusted partner for scientists, supporting researchers as they take on harder, more original problems
Give every bold idea the chance to be tested, and turn more discoveries of lasting value into reality
Kimi for Science welcomes researchers across the natural sciences, engineering, and related interdisciplinary fields to apply
We welcome research at every stage, from improving existing workflows with Kimi to exploring new methodologies and tackling more challenging scientific problems
Technical support from the Kimi team, plus early access to new models and curated research and community resources
Outstanding researchers and teams can access longer-term, more in-depth technical and resource support
You may submit your application more than once; only the most recent submission will be reviewed

Doing It the Hard Way
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-27
12:00:06 UTC
21:00:06 KST

Dawson James, Senior Direct Air Capture Mechanical Engineer
Where I come from
I am the fifth generation of my family to live and work on the same piece of land in the prairies of Saskatchewan, Canada. For over a century, my family has read the sky, worked the soil, and depended on the land staying productive for the next generation. On the farm, I learned a simple rule: you don’t take from tomorrow to pay for today.
That rule is harder to follow than it sounds. A modern farm runs on diesel, fertilizer made from natural gas, and grid power. In the last few years, fuel and fertilizer prices have swung so wildly that if you bought at the wrong time, your profit for the year evaporated. For my grandfather, fuel and fertilizer were a fraction of the total cost. Now they are among the largest.
Looking for the right problem
I left the farm to find an answer to one question: how do we power a modern farming operation, and everything else, without drawing down resources that won’t come back?
I’ve spent most of my career chasing pieces of that answer. Right out of school, I joined Areva (now Orano) at McClean Lake in northern Saskatchewan to work on SABRE, a way of mining uranium by drilling from the surface and cutting ore out with a high-pressure water jet. I ran a shift during the 2014 campaign, the first time the program pulled a significant amount of ore with equipment built for the method, and that season locked in most of the techniques the program still uses. Then the project slowed into a long design phase, and after two years I left to find work where I could build things faster. The first commercial SABRE mining started in 2025, more than a decade after our campaign.
Alongside all of that, I was building a company. A few friends and I founded Innocorps Research in university, starting with equipment built in my garage, and I worked other jobs to keep it going while we patented a method for recovering clean water from industrial waste and black water. That work took us into the Department of National Defence’s IDEaS Pop Up City contest, a four-round competition to rethink how the Canadian Armed Forces supply temporary camps with water, power, and waste handling. Our Circular City team won the $2 million grand prize at Canadian Forces Base Suffield in 2023, cutting solid waste tenfold and recycling the camp’s wastewater into drinking water that met Health Canada guidelines. I drank it straight from the output before the lab results came back, and I’m still here. But a prize isn’t a procurement contract, and a small company can’t wait years for one. Eventually I couldn’t justify paying myself anymore, and I moved on.
Then my brother and I went back to the farm and built off-grid homes, with the goal of producing more than they used. We designed for thermal efficiency first: thermal mass walls to store winter sun, and roof overhangs sized to let the low winter sun in and shade the glass from the high summer sun. Solar panels and batteries supplied the power, water and wastewater were treated on site, and our second building was coupled to a four-season greenhouse that grew food year round. The first one overshot and ran too hot to live in, so it became our workshop. They hold up from -40°C to +40°C. A house can collect enough sunlight to run itself, but a farm, let alone the plants that make its fuel and fertilizer, needs far more energy than that.
Each of those projects solved a piece of the problem but none got to the core. SABRE showed me how long good technology can take to reach the field. Innocorps showed me that technology that works still loses if it can’t win on cost and timing. The houses showed me that sunlight can run a household, but not an industry. Back on the farm, we still needed diesel and fertilizer to turn a profit, and fossil fuels keep winning because they are cheap, dense, and available on demand. Anything that replaces them has to beat them on cost.
Finding Terraform
I was researching ways to turn solar power into fuel and fertilizer, the two biggest bills on the farm. Fertilizer interested me most: the nitrogen is free in the air, and the hard part is making the hydrogen and finding the energy to break the nitrogen bond. But I was still looking at fuel too, and that research led me to episode 36 of the S3 YouTube channel, which covered Terraform Industries. They had just run their first full-loop Terraformer, making methane from air, water and electricity, with the goal of producing it cheaper than gas pulled out of the ground. It was almost exactly what I wanted, because farm machinery can run on methane. The only problem was that there was nothing I could buy yet. So I decided to go help build it.
By then, I was mostly a seasonal hand on the farm, and my dad, uncle and brother could handle the work without me. I wanted a challenge, so I applied. A day or two later I had a screening call with Casey Handmer, Terraform’s founder and CEO. The way he thought about the problem convinced me the project was feasible. After a deep dive with one of the DAC team members, they invited me for an onsite and gave me a week to prepare, along with a homework problem: design a flash calciner with cyclones that could calcine carbonate material. Flash calciners with cyclone separation are a standard industrial method, and at the time it was the path Terraform’s calciner was on. It later proved infeasible for our process, but that homework was my first look at the problem I’ve been working on ever since. I flew from Saskatchewan to Burbank, from farm life to the biggest metro area on the West Coast, did the day-long onsite, and got a job offer that evening. They wanted me to start in three weeks and they wanted me to lead the Direct Air Capture team, to pull carbon dioxide (CO2) from the air as cheaply as possible.
The problem
We had to build three major components that work together in a loop to pull CO₂ out of the air and deliver it as a pure stream suitable for a reactor. This is called the Lime cycle. The carbonator moves a large volume of air past the sorbent, which captures CO₂ and turns into carbonate. The calciner heats the carbonate to release pure CO₂ and leaves quicklime behind. The hydrator turns the quicklime back into fresh sorbent so the loop can start again. None of the three is as simple as it sounds, and that’s where doing it the hard way starts.
Pulling CO₂ out of the air
To make fuel from air, you first need a pure stream of CO₂. CO₂ in the atmosphere is about 425 to 430 parts per million, so you have to move an enormous amount of air to collect a useful amount of it. On a volume-to-volume basis, it’s roughly one million to one.
Our Direct Air Capture (DAC) system uses hydrated lime, Ca(OH)₂, which reacts with CO₂ in the air to form calcium carbonate, CaCO₃. The chemistry is old and well understood. The hard part is material handling. Lime wants to be a powder, and powder blows away. We have to form it into a structure that holds together and doesn’t turn to dust, leaves room for air to flow through, and still reacts fast enough to be worth the energy spent moving the air. That means hitting several narrow windows at once: humidity, temperature, and how the material is formed. One of my first successes at Terraform was finding a way to form flakes effectively in large amounts using off the shelf equipment.
The team
When I started at Terraform, the DAC team was small: one senior engineer and two technicians. Several approaches to calcium looping had already been tried, and none had worked yet. Casey was upfront with me: I might be taking on a problem that didn’t have a viable solution, but the company needed one to succeed. I’m not one to back down from a challenge, so we got to work.
Russ, the senior engineer, had been on the team the longest and had seen nearly every method we’d pursued. His focus was hydration, but he got me up to speed on where the system stood and what had already been tried. He had an encyclopedic knowledge of the tests available to us and was good at explaining complex processes, like the energy flows through a system, with diagrams. Our two technicians, Jo and Eghan, each brought their own set of skills that made the builds possible, spanning trades and art forms from welding to pottery.
We built out the rest of the team with interns, starting with Jake and AK, and later Aggy. They came straight out of school with a lot of drive and energy, and all of them moved up the skill tree quickly and took ownership of projects across the three main branches of calcium looping. Along the way, Katie joined as a summer intern and Brian as a consultant. That was the team for the first year, and this year we’ve started expanding again.
Getting the CO₂ back out: the calciner
Once the CO₂ is locked up as limestone, we have to get it back out. That step is calcination, one of the oldest chemical processes people use. Lime has been made by heating limestone since the Neolithic.
Heating limestone drives off CO₂ and leaves quicklime, CaO. Pure CaCO₃ loses about 44% of its mass as CO₂ when it calcines. The CaO goes back to the hydrator and the loop starts over.
Industry does this every day in large kilns, but the normal way breaks every one of our rules.
Why conventional calcination doesn’t work for us
A cement or lime kiln burns fuel to make heat, and the CO₂ from the fuel and from the limestone goes up the stack together. We can’t burn anything, and the whole point is to capture the CO₂ that comes off. Industrial kilns also take days to reach temperature and run continuously until they need a rebuild. Because our power comes from solar panels, the calciner has to go from cold to operating temperature in about an hour each morning, run through the day, and cool down overnight. That combination rules out almost every conventional design and a lot of unconventional ones.
What we tried
Before building anything new, we went back to how calcination is done elsewhere. Rotary and shaft kilns, and the flash calciners used for fine material, all burn fuel, so the CO₂ they release comes out diluted with combustion gases. Vacuum calcination drops the decomposition temperature from about 900°C under one atmosphere of CO₂ to roughly 750°C at a tenth of an atmosphere, but nobody has made it work as a continuous process. Plasma calciners deliver enormous power in a small space, but they overshoot the temperature by thousands of degrees and wear out their electrodes. Electrochemical routes run at room temperature, but they use more energy and make Ca(OH)₂ instead of CaO. The closest match to what we wanted was the electrically heated fluidized bed, where the CO₂ released by the reaction is itself the gas that fluidizes the bed.
Terraform had also built numerous calciners of its own before I arrived, and each one taught us something. One of the early ones was a tube furnace: a quartz tube inside a clamshell of Kanthal wire heaters, loaded with a batch of carbonate and sealed. It made CO₂, but it was a batch process, and hot CaO attacked quartz. Next came what the team called the giant toaster, an array of plates with embedded heating wire that flakes flowed down between. The material sintered, bridged the gaps, and locked up the whole machine with no easy way to clean it out. The rotary valves at the top and bottom couldn’t handle the powder, and steam clogged the inlet and corroded the heating wire.
The Thor heater was the flash calciner with cyclones that Terraform was building when I interviewed. An array of silicon carbide elements heated a stream of CO₂ that carried the carbonate through a cyclone, then a recuperator cooled the gas and a blower sent it around again. It was partly built when I joined. After some initial tests on the gas heater, I ran the numbers and showed it couldn’t work: the gas couldn’t be heated enough in a single pass, and the cyclone would lose heat faster than the heaters could put it in. We changed direction before the buildout was finished.
So, I pursued the drop tube calciner instead. Silicon carbide elements surrounded a ceramic tube inside a sealed vessel, and material dropped through the heated zone to flash calcine above 1100°C. It worked in the way that mattered most: It produced CO₂ and fully calcined material. However, it never reached our purity or production targets. As a continuous process, it depended on feeding material in and taking it out without interruption, and we couldn’t make the feed and takeoff systems run reliably. The tubes were prone to failure, the CaO attacked the insulation, and too many parts were running at their limits.
We stepped back and ran a fast, cheap prototyping sprint, building and testing candidate approaches in weeks instead of months. Most were ruled out, some on paper before we built anything, and we settled on a new design.
Then testing surprised us. One of the hardest parts of the design turned out to be unnecessary, and taking that part out deleted a large share of the system’s complexity, along with the grinding, the particle sizing, and the material loss that came with it. We reused what had worked in earlier systems and deleted everything else. The result is a simple, electrically heated batch calciner that produces CO₂ at 99.9% purity and meets our production target.
We scaled this architecture from a simple prototype to a ~30 kW unit in just two weeks, brought it out to the Muroc test site, and operated our first fully solar-powered calciner, generating pure CO₂ directly from off-grid DC electricity. It was our tenth ground-up redesign, and now I understand why many other teams have not managed to solve this problem – they gave up after the ninth attempt.
The hard part for me
The hardest part wasn’t the technical problems. It was managing the team. The DAC system threw a wide spectrum of problems at us at once, and trying to cover all of them pulled my focus away from overseeing the work and delegating it well. Our leadership saw it before I fully did, and brought in Stephanie to manage the team so I could focus on the engineering. That’s why I spent the final stretch on the calciner’s latest prototype, the one that hit our production and purity goals and proved our models for scaling. It’s where I did my best work. Knowing where you’re most useful is part of doing it the hard way too.
Why this matters
Every one of these problems is solvable, but none of them are solved by copying what industry already does. That is what doing it the hard way means here: going back to first principles on processes that have barely changed in centuries, because the old way only works if you can burn fuel and vent the CO₂. Now, we have a calciner that works, and the next hard problem is building them by the thousands.
Someday I want the sixth generation on our land to fill the tractor with fuel made from the same sunlight that grows the crops. No more taking from tomorrow to pay for today.
Join us
We’re hiring. If you want to work on problems like the calciner, where the textbook answer doesn’t work and you have to build your way to one that does, there’s a place for you here. Open roles right now include Direct Air Capture Mechanical Engineer and Direct Air Capture Technician, along with roles across our electrolyzer, reactor, methanol, ammonia and solar teams. The full list is at terraformindustries.com.
Send your resume and one page of evidence that you can execute, like a summary of hardware you’ve designed, built and tested, to hiring@terraformindustries.com. I showed up from a farm in Saskatchewan with a homework problem and a week to prepare. If you build things, we want to hear from you. Every role is on site at our castle in Burbank.

Pizza history: AI agent's cardless purchase is a first for UK
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-27
11:41:11 UTC
20:41:11 KST

An order of Papa John’s margherita pizzas has become the first purchase in the UK to be made using an AI agent using cardless payment technology.
The order of nine mini pizzas was made as part of the Financial Conduct Authority’s (FCA) regulated sandbox trials to test the technology, using Lloyds Bank and payment specialist TrueLayer.
London-based TrueLayer, which enables payments to be made between bank accounts without using cards, said it was the first time an AI agent had been used to pay a UK retailer for an order for a real customer.
“We completed this payment transaction with Papa John’s with no account numbers, no password [and] handled with the highest possible security profile,” said the TrueLayer chief executive, Francesco Simoneschi, in an interview with BBC Radio 4’s Today programme. “We talked with the AI agent, described the occasion and dietary requirements. Of course the agent suggested different flavours and quantities. We confirmed the order and the pizza arrived later.”
The team ordered the pizzas using the Papa John’s AI agent, which had been given permission to access their bank account and request payment to the retailer’s account.
Papa John’s AI agent was set to work with a strict mandate established with Lloyds, including a maximum spend limit per transaction and per month, with no account security information shared.
Simoneschi said the test was the first step toward mass adoption by the public of a technology that would fundamentally change purchasing habits.
“This is possibly the last Black Friday as we know it,” he said, referring to the annual online sales discount day. “At the moment plenty of us are already using AI to write up the shopping list. I think next year the agent will buy gifts on our behalf. Agents will do shopping on our behalf, will manage our finances, and will help us understand and compare complex products. This idea that they can really help us to sort out a lot of different aspects of our lives is a little bit like having an assistant available to millions of consumers out there.”
However, Simoneschi said consumers would still need to make informed decisions about how they used AI agents for making purchases.
“I think good [personal] judgment still applies, of course,” he said. “It does require control and supervision, and we need to make sure that the underlying technology is fit for purpose, while enforcing the highest possible security standards.”
He added that if there was an error or mistake in the order process, such as the agent ordering the wrong product, consumers would still be protected by existing rules and regulations covering buyers’ rights.
Earlier this week the FCA issued a warning to financial services companies developing AI agents that they must comply with existing regulations.
“The reason we are testing with regulators in this sandbox is to really understand all the aspects of the technology ahead of its mass adoption,” he said. “This is a great place to start.”
Meta’s personal AI assistant, Muse, which launched in the UK on Tuesday, can also make purchases online. Unlike TrueLayer, Muse makes payments using a customer’s bank card.
